Related dispatch-economics finding: declaring a run-scoped input in a check file's "runner inputs" table is NOT enough — if the runner does not render a placeholder for it, the worker is left to INFER it, which is the exact guessing the contract forbids. And precompute the selection matrix centrally: 13 workers independently re-parsing the same 6400-line catalog to re-derive the same skip set is the dominant sweep cost (~83k lines of redundant reading), and centralising it also makes the skip reason authoritative and reproducible instead of arbitrary.

163. **Validate an AUTO-DISCOVERED input against what makes it USABLE — matching a glob is not validation; and two tools cannot bind without ONE shared algorithm.** (a) A runner auto-selected its baseline as "newest directory matching `runs/*-P0`". Two failure modes hit immediately: it selected a TEST FIXTURE directory as the baseline for a real run (the glob does not care about provenance), and it would equally select a directory whose results are EMPTY — and an empty baseline is WORSE than no baseline, because the run proceeds believing it has one and every baseline-relative check silently degrades to "no baseline available" instead of failing loudly. Validate the candidate against non-empty results, roster satisfied, matching fingerprint and a conforming name pattern, and REFUSE rather than fall back to the newest thing that matched. Same principle as the roster rule: never infer validity from the fact that something was found. (b) Two tools in the same framework each hashed the catalog their own way — one over concatenated contents, the other over filename+contents truncated differently — so neither could ever confirm the other had examined the same input, making the cross-tool attestation unusable BY CONSTRUCTION rather than merely inconvenient. Consolidate onto one shared fingerprint function, and keep a SEPARATE fingerprint for any input that gates SELECTION rather than content. Same class as dual parsers: when a system has TWO independent parsers of one source, a normalisation applied to only one creates a silent divergence where every TOOL reports the source as correct while the RUNNER still dispatches the wrong set — worse than not fixing it. Inventory every parser before changing parse-time semantics; best is to consolidate so the divergence is unrepresentable.

164. **Root-owned Terraform caches on macOS may resist both normal deletion and an AppleScript administrator helper.** If `.terraform/` was created through `sudo terraform`, its contents can be owned by root; a normal `rm -rf` fails, and `osascript 'do shell script ... with administrator privileges'` can still fail with `Operation not permitted` under `Desktop` because the helper lacks the invoking app's TCC access. For a clean Git worktree, first prove it is clean and that ignored files outside `.terraform/` do not matter, move the user-owned top-level tracked tree (for example `stacks/`) to a sibling quarantine, then restore it with `git restore --source=HEAD --worktree -- stacks`. This makes the repo lightweight without traversing root-owned children. Reclaim the quarantined disk through an interactive Terminal `sudo rm -rf -- <exact-quarantine-path>`; verify zero `.terraform/` directories, clean `git status`, retained lockfiles, and repo size afterward.

165. **A validation tier's SKIP composition maps what has never been exercised.** Before extending clearance to another tier, compare its capability profile with the swept roster: checks gated on `tracks: pinned`, `cloud: aws`, or waypoint presence may all have skipped on the earlier tier. Making a layer advisory does not validate the alternative profile. Re-derive component presence at promotion time; a later rollout can activate previously skipped checks. Exercise those checks against a matching target before promotion, and report a subset as `test (swept subset: N of M)` with unswept members named. Do not import another run's verdict or present subset coverage as tier-wide clearance.

166. **Fix one leg of a defect, then hunt its SIBLINGS by default — the sibling almost always still carries it**: Confirmed pairs found in a single audit: one check was fixed to a relative gate on its p99 leg but left its p50 leg on a bare absolute threshold; one was widened to a `[1h]` window while two siblings stayed on `[5m]` over the same sub-1-rps fleet; one had its prose corrected while its own inline query comment still contradicted the new prose; one gained a per-window arming branch and its twin did not. The mechanism is copy-inheritance — the sibling was cloned from the original and inherits every defect the original had at clone time, including ones since fixed. Make "grep for the pattern across every sibling" the closing step of any single-leg fix, not a follow-up.

167. **When a task's verification criterion cites an EXACT control count, re-baseline it at the START of the task — a moved count may be a concurrent agent's in-flight work, not your regression**: A self-test count moved 41/41 → 46/46 mid-task and a warning count spiked to 93 then decayed to 0, all because another session was adding a new concept to the same directory while the work ran. Reading the dispatch-time number as authoritative would have produced a confident false regression report. Same class: a content fingerprint quoted in a dispatch prompt can already be STALE at session start — always re-read the fingerprint yourself before treating a mismatch as drift you caused.

168. **A safe controlled-repro pattern for node-scoped infrastructure bugs — and its hard limit**: (1) Pick the node with the FEWEST at-risk pods, not a random one — on one cluster 9 of 10 affected workloads sat on a single node and exactly 1 sat alone on another, so choosing correctly moved the blast radius from 9 to 1. (2) Confirm the node is in the PRE-STATE you need (e.g. agent `restartCount == 0` AND pod `startTime` within minutes of node `creationTimestamp` = never restarted since creation). (3) Capture the full baseline BEFORE touching anything. (4) Perturb the SMALLEST unit — delete ONE agent pod, never the DaemonSet. (5) Re-measure the identical set. (6) Heal and re-measure a THIRD time to prove reversal. **Two limits that cost us:** some flips are ONE-WAY (the node stays in the new state until reboot), so you get exactly one attempt per node — plan the measurement set before the first attempt; and a single restart can change TWO things at once, in which case the repro **cannot attribute causality** and you need an independent discriminator to tell which change broke things. Also: `kubectl debug` ephemeral containers are PERMANENT for the pod's life — only attach them to pods you intend to recycle anyway.

169. **Correcting a row inside an IMMUTABLE run artifact: create a new artifact and carry rows verbatim — never hand-splice, and record provenance**: Splicing into the existing artifact is rejected by any validator that cross-checks the artifact against its manifest, and it destroys the evidence chain. The working shape: a new correctly-named output dir (naming conventions are often hard-enforced, so a suffix like `<ts>-P0-supplemented` is not an option), the amended catalog snapshot copied in, unchanged rows carried BYTE-VERBATIM, only the affected rows re-measured **at the SAME anchor** (which requires a time-travel query modifier), the summary recomputed, a `derived_from{source, derived_at, reason, carried_over_unchanged, regraded, collection_method, status_changes}` block in the manifest, a `PROVENANCE.md`, and **the source artifact left byte-untouched**. Never re-grade a carried-over row. Same discipline applies to re-issuing a baseline that is missing one newly-added row: measure that one row retrospectively against the original window — and do it FAST, because log retention is finite and once the window ages out the row cannot be measured honestly at all.

170. **A planning-doc row is not proof the item is actionable — filter the doc's list by the target schema's REQUIRED fields before acting on it**: A cutover plan listed 15 components for a batch; one of them had no `owner`, which the CRD `required`s, so enabling it would have failed the render outright and taken the whole batch down with it. The doc even flagged it (`**MISSING**` in the owner column) but still carried it in the batch table, because the table was generated by enumerating deployed components, not by checking mintability. Before executing any doc-driven batch, re-derive the actionable set from the values/schema yourself and diff it against the doc's list; treat a discrepancy as a doc bug to report, not a reason to stop. Same shape as #167 — the dispatch-time artifact is a hypothesis about the world, never authoritative over the world.

171. **Two Codex-worker failure modes from one validation-framework fan-out, and the protocol fixes for both.** (a) **Silent death during final result assembly**: workers completed all check work then died with no error, no limit message, just a truncated log — 2 of 3 clusters at ~23-25 min (the smallest survived). **Fix, now baked into the runner prompt: INCREMENTAL WRITE PROTOCOL** — append each result object as one line to `results/<cluster>.partial.jsonl` the moment that check finishes, never buffer in memory, then assemble the final `.json` with `jq -s`. A cutoff then costs ONE check instead of the whole run. A second contributor was context burn: one worker opened a PEER cluster's result file 11x as a "format reference", which both consumed the budget that killed it and risked copying another cluster's numbers — **ban cross-cluster result reads explicitly** (a worker's own P0 baseline is the only exception). (b) **`pgrep -fc "codex exec"` is a FALSE liveness signal**: it returned 0 while two workers were still running, and they wrote valid complete results ~2 min later; acting on it meant declaring them dead, launching redundant re-runs, and killing those. **A frozen log is not death** — a >60s silent gap is normal while the model composes one large emission (e.g. a 95 KB result JSON). Reliable signals, in order: the result artifact appearing on disk; log-file byte growth sampled over >60s; the background task's own completion notification. **Wait on the artifact, never on the process.**

172. **A target's ENVIRONMENT comes from the REGISTRY (`clusters.md` / `clusters.yaml` `tier`), NEVER from its name suffix and NEVER from prose in a ticket.** A `t`/`p` suffix can be a *customer* naming artifact — the customer's Test or Preview instance, which is still OUR production. **The failure mode is specific and repeatable: the TICKET'S OWN WORDS mislead** ("promote pinned-test <target>" reads as our test tier), and it survives having printed a preflight table showing `tier=prod` twice in the same session. **Consequence that makes this worth the entry: promoting such a target is a PRODUCTION change requiring the full release process, not a test-tier activity.** Same shape as `learnings-crossplane.md`, which makes the identical point about inferring a target's *cloud* from its name — **the registry is the source for every target attribute, and a dispatch-time artifact is never authoritative over it** (#167/#170). The registry itself must carry an explicit CRITICAL warning at the top naming any target whose name lies; `references/clusters.template.md` should reserve that field.

173. **In some worktrees, an UNSCOPED `git diff --name-only origin/main` can return EMPTY while files genuinely differ — always scope the diff to a path.** `origin/main`, `origin/main..HEAD` and `origin/main...HEAD` all reproduced it; the same command scoped (`git diff --name-only origin/main HEAD -- charts/argo-apps/`) returned correct results. Treat an empty unscoped diff as "no evidence", never as "no change" — scope every diff-scope verification to the paths you care about. Related capture trap: the rtk `PreToolUse` hook truncates redirected output with a `--- Changes ---` marker, so use `helm --output-dir` for render captures rather than `>` redirection (`learnings-helm-ci.md`/`#43`).

174. **Anchoring on the first visible difference and treating it as causal — the failure mode behind two wrong root causes in one incident, and the four fixes.** Both wrong RCAs were caught by the user, not by the agent. *Error 1*: found a config artifact present in broken pods and absent in a few working ones, and built a "these were newly enrolled" story — wrong, because the enrolment predated the incident by years and the "clean" pods differed for an unrelated reason (a different workload shape entirely). *Error 2*: asserted a remediation would "just re-break it" from that same bad model, and only conceded when a 60-second experiment proved the opposite. **The config turned out byte-identical between broken and working states — it was never the variable.** **Fixes, generally applicable:** (1) when a control group looks clean, **verify it is the same WORKLOAD SHAPE** before treating it as a control; (2) before claiming "X newly started happening", **check X's actual age** (label timestamps, `managedFields`, provisioning annotations) rather than inferring it from the incident timeline; (3) **when a cheap reversible experiment can settle a mechanism dispute in 60s, RUN IT** before asserting the mechanism; (4) **state the falsifier alongside the hypothesis** so the gap is visible. **The user's flat contradiction of the premise was what unlocked the real mechanism — treat that class of pushback as high-signal, not as something to defend against.**

175. **Dispatch the Codex adversarial lens via `~/.agent-knowledge/scripts/codex-dispatch.sh` in Bash, NOT via the Agent tool.** The Agent tool with `run_in_background: true` does not return in the same turn, so a parallax review that needs both lenses before posting stalls waiting on a future notification. Bash dispatch gives synchronous output in the same turn. Reserve the Agent tool for work whose result you can genuinely defer.

176. **Post-rollout hardening is where an agent adds pure margin — and low marginal cost changes WHICH work gets done at all, not just how fast.** The scaffold + alerts + docs + observability-correctness-sweep + upstream-triage phase after a rollout ships is tens of hours of manual work that in practice is **never** done; agent-driven it is single-digit hours. It pays immediately, because a correctness sweep over alerts and dashboards that nobody has re-read since they were written reliably finds rules that are structurally DEAD (matching on an enum value the controller never emits) and workloads that have been stuck for weeks with no alert firing. **When the cost of a category of work drops far enough, propose the CATEGORY — do not just do the assigned task faster.**

177. **`bd dep remove` is RACY for the same import-on-start reason as `bd close` (#33) and `bd forget` (#62) — ADDS persist reliably, REMOVES flap.** `.beads/issues.jsonl` is gitignored, so every `bd` invocation — **including reads like `bd dep list`** — round-trips import-empty-DB → export-jsonl; an interleaved read can re-export the stale jsonl and RESURRECT a just-removed edge. **Workaround: loop the remove plus a verify in ONE shell script until a same-breath check reads clean.** Harmless redundant edges are not worth fighting. **The general shape across all three cases: in this store, creating state is durable and destroying state is not** — treat every deletion as unverified until re-read from a new process, and never conclude durability from the command's own success message.

178. **Do NOT enumerate memories from `bd prime` output — the harness truncates the persisted hook output at ~57 KB and the file just ENDS with no marker.** That silently hid 293 of 358 memories (only 65 keys visible). Use **`bd memories --json`**, but note it emits non-JSON preamble lines to stdout first (`auto-importing NNN bytes …` / `auto-imported N issues`), so `json.load` fails with `Expecting value: line 1 column 1` — **strip to the first `{` before parsing.** The payload is a flat `{key: text}` dict **plus an integer `schema_version` key**, so guard `isinstance(v, str)` when iterating (and never `bd forget schema_version`). There are **no timestamps** in that output, so recency must be inferred from dates embedded in the memory text.
    - **The correct jq form, and why the keyword filter does not help.** `.[]` yields the content STRINGS, so the natural-looking `bd memories --json | jq '.[] | select(.key=="x/y/z")'` fails with `jq: error: Cannot index string with string "key"`. Correct: `bd memories --json | jq -r 'to_entries[] | select(.key|test("pattern")) | "=== \(.key) ===\n\(.value)"'` — use `test()` with an alternation to pull several specific memories' FULL text in one call, because plain `bd memories <keyword>` **truncates each entry to ~one line**, which is useless when you need the detail of a prior-art memory whose key you already know. **A keyword-filtered call (`bd memories --json <kw>`) still returns the SAME full object, so filtering must happen client-side.** In jq, guard with `select(.value|type=="string")` for the same reason this entry gives for python's `isinstance(v,str)` — the integer `schema_version` key.

179. **`bd prime --memories-only` grew into a 774 KB / ~193 k-token preamble injected into EVERY worker — defused with a `.beads/PRIME.md` override, 373× reduction, memories still reachable on demand.** `bd prime` has no scoping flags, but `bd prime --help` documents that a `.beads/PRIME.md` file **OVERRIDES THE DEFAULT OUTPUT ENTIRELY** — and testing proved it overrides `--memories-only` too (sentinel test: 774,136 bytes → 28 bytes). The replacement PRIME.md tells the agent it has NO memories loaded and must fetch them with `knowledge-search.sh <keywords>` or `bd memories <keyword>`: 774,136 → 2,074 bytes, ~193 k tokens → ~0.5 k, with retrieval verified (`bd memories istio` → 300 hits). `.beads/` is gitignored, so this is machine-local with zero team blast radius and reverts with `rm .beads/PRIME.md`.
    - **Discovered by CONSEQUENCE, not by audit**: two consecutive Codex reviews of a harness plan emitted no answer at all — run 1 died in context-load after `bd prime`; run 2, explicitly told not to prime, still exhausted budget on file reads. Two implications: a worker's usable context is a small fraction of what it appears to be (which plausibly explains degraded output on tasks needing wide reading), and **a task-level instruction DOES beat the injected preamble**, so a preamble should never hardcode an unconditional expensive prime.
    - **CONSOLIDATION IS NOT THE FIX and the two are independent.** The corpus distribution is FLAT (median 1058 B, mean 1256 B, top-10 only 8 % of bytes) — there is no fat head to cut — and a full pass shrinks it ~2 % against continuous per-session growth. **Consolidation is knowledge hygiene; priming is a context problem. You need both.**
    - **THE CLINCHING IRONY**: two memories already captured the exact lessons that a later PR then re-learned across 3 correction commits. The knowledge was in the hive and was not applied — **because dumping all 614 crowded out the context needed to read the chart being edited. Auto-loading everything is why nothing gets used; targeted retrieval is what makes a corpus usable.**
    - **Diagnostic habit: `wc -c` anything a preamble runs unconditionally.** Context spent before the task starts is invisible in every transcript.
    - **`bd init` does NOT create `.beads/PRIME.md`, so #179's 193k-token preamble defect SILENTLY RECURS on every new or migrated hive.** Measured on a fresh hive: `bd prime --memories-only` injected **163,949 bytes (~40 k tokens) into every dispatch**, because the dispatch preamble tells every subagent to run it first. Restoring the override cut it to **2,175 bytes — 75×** — with memories still fully reachable by keyword (`bd memories <topic>` → 47 hits). **CHECK AFTER EVERY HIVE CREATION OR MIGRATION: `bd prime --memories-only | wc -c` must be ~2000, not ~160000.** The diagnostic habit from #179 is what generalises: **`wc -c` anything a preamble runs unconditionally**, because context spent before the task starts is invisible in every transcript. This instance was found by an audience question at a demo, not by audit — which is the argument for making it a standing post-migration check rather than a thing you notice.

180. **RUN `knowledge-search.sh` BEFORE FORMING A HYPOTHESIS — three recorded recurrences, each costing most of a session, and the failure is RETRIEVAL, not capture.** (a) 2026-07-28: a <cluster> tailscale outage was investigated from scratch and produced a WRONG root cause, when the correct mechanism was already in the hive from the same incident 24 h earlier — including a memory that explicitly said *"iptables are byte-identical between broken and working states, so never diagnose this from the rules"*, which is the exact trap that was walked into. (b) 2026-07-29, second time in ONE session: four validation-framework catalog defects were re-derived that were already recorded, costing two extra full 3-cluster sweep cycles. (c) The `bd forget` case in #62. **The deeper systemic finding is that capture worked perfectly** — a memory keyed `<domain>/catalog/<check>` documenting a broken check has **no path back into the check itself**, so it sits correct-and-unread while the defect ships through sweep after sweep. Proposed structural fix: a runner preflight that greps bd for `catalog/` memories matching the checks in scope and prints them at dispatch time, converting "known defect" from something rediscovered at grade time into something seen before the run. **Corollary: when a repro triggers two changes at once, state explicitly that the experiment cannot attribute causality before filing anything upstream.**

181. **`rtk`'s PreToolUse rewrite makes two idioms LIE — never pipe an rtk-eligible command into a parser or a counter.** (a) **Piping a render into a parser arrives CORRUPTED**: `helm template … | python3` fails with `expected <block end>, but found <scalar>` at a line of the chart you never touched, because the hook rewrites/summarises the command even when piped. The identical render dumped with `> file` and parsed from the file, or run via `subprocess.run()` INSIDE python, parses fine. (b) **Piping rtk output into `wc`/`grep -c` for a scope assertion is wrong** — `git diff --name-only | wc -l` returned 4 for a single-file change because rtk appends a decorated `Changes:` header and blank lines. Use `git status --porcelain`, `git --no-pager diff --name-only`, or `git diff --numstat` and read it literally. (c) rtk also rewrites `grep -c <pattern-with-brace>` to ripgrep, which errors on an unescaped `{` (`repetition quantifier expects a valid decimal`) — use python `re.findall` for brace-literal counts.
    - Two adjacent traps from the same session: **`discipline.py comment --base <ref>` diffs COMMITTED HEAD vs the ref, NOT the working tree**, so running it before committing reports "clean" on changes it has not seen — run it AFTER committing, or stage and use `--staged`. And **`git checkout -- <dir>` to revert a negative control reverts EVERY file in that dir** — it silently undid three template fixes; use a per-file `cp` backup or `git stash` for negative controls.

182. **`gh` + JSON tooling pitfalls that recur across any review-heavy task.** (1) `gh api graphql -f query=…` with a single-line query string throws `Expected NAME, actual: (none)` — write the query to a file and pass `-F query=@/tmp/q.graphql`. (2) **Piping into a python heredoc makes the heredoc override the pipe's stdin**, so `json.load(sys.stdin)` parses the SCRIPT, not the data — write `gh` output to a temp file first and `json.load(open(file))` inside the heredoc. (3) Correcting an already-posted review is `gh api -X PUT repos/O/R/pulls/N/reviews/<NUMERIC id> -F body=@file` — **the GraphQL `PRR_` node id 404s**; get the numeric id from `gh api …/reviews`. (4) `gh pr view --json files` lists files touched by ANY commit in the PR, so to see the NET diff use `git diff --name-only <merge-base>..HEAD` or `gh pr diff --name-only`.

183. **RECURRING DISPATCH DEFECT (the orchestrator's, not the agent's): any prompt that tells a subagent to run `helm dep build` for a rendered proof MUST also tell it to keep the generated `Chart.lock` out of the commit.** The lockfile is generated by the build and carries a machine-local timestamp; if it is untracked on the default branch, a `git add -A` sweeps it in as hundreds of lines of unrelated diff. **The trap is that lockfile tracking is INCONSISTENT within one repo** — most may be tracked and one not — so "we commit Chart.lock here" is not a safe generalisation. **Fix in the dispatch prompt: "stage the values files explicitly by path, never `git add -A`; confirm `git status --porcelain` shows `Chart.lock` as `??`."** Recovery: `git rm --cached <path>/Chart.lock`, commit, push.

184. **Reworking a stacked PR chain in a repo that FORBIDS force-push: MERGE FORWARD, never rebase.** `git fetch`, then `git merge <parent>` into each child bottom-up — merges are fast-forwardable pushes and cannot clobber commits the user pushed to a child branch, whereas a rebase requires force-push and would silently resurrect content the user deleted. Concretely: a user commit stripped comment lines on the parent branch; merging the reworked parent forward PRESERVED that deletion, while the child's conflict (old-path keys vs new-path keys) was resolved by taking the PARENT side wholesale and re-adding the child's one-line delta at the new path. **Always finish with `git diff <parent-branch> -- <paths>` on the child and assert the delta is ONLY the intended change** — no comment churn, no re-indentation. Bulldozer squash-merges, so the intermediate merge commits never reach main.

185. **When a documented rule keeps being violated, ask WHICH ENFORCEMENT POINT WAS SUPPOSED TO FIRE AND DID IT RUN — do not rewrite the prose.** Comment discipline was documented in two canonical files for months; PRs still shipped 10-line comment blocks with Jira keys, and one was **still non-compliant after its own "trim comments" remediation commit**. Two structural root causes, neither about wording: (1) **the rule was judgment-shaped** — "terse", "no essays" — and the "only for a non-obvious why" EXCEPTION SWALLOWS THE RULE, because the whys genuinely are non-obvious; (2) **no enforcement point existed on the path those PRs took** (the catching clause lived in `pr-reviewer`, which was only auto-dispatched when a SUBAGENT returned a PR URL — main-session PRs fell through). **Diagnostic tell: the ticket-ID half of the rule is fully mechanical and needs zero judgment, and it was violated anyway — which proves the file was not consulted at write time at all, so restating it louder was never going to work.** Fix = `discipline.py comment` (banned-pattern regex + a hard 2-line-per-block ceiling), wired into `create-pr` BEFORE push and `pr-reviewer` before the judgment review. **Two design details that decide whether a gate gets used or ignored**: exclude prose files (a markdown `## Heading` is not a code comment — that false positive is exactly what trains people to skip a gate), and track block-comment state (`/* */`, `{{- /* */}}`) or the check is dodged by switching syntax.
    - **A MAX BOUND IS NOT A DEFAULT.** "Max 2 lines per comment" bounds one block but never says a comment should not exist — four separate 1-line comments each pass the ceiling while narrating the code. **When writing a mechanical rule, ask separately: what is the DEFAULT, and what is the CEILING?** (Fixed by stating default = NONE plus a per-file density check.)
    - **ANY ALWAYS-FIRING CHECK IS WORSE THAN NO CHECK, because it trains you to skip it.** A sanitization gate reported 10,314 hits across 106 files on every run: `.redaction-denylist` had 9 BLANK LINES, and `rg -nFf <file>` treats an EMPTY PATTERN as match-everything. A real leak would have been invisible in that noise. Fix: strip blank lines before `rg`. Quieter sibling failure in the same file: **over-short denylist patterns false-match ordinary English** (a 4–5 char name fragment was matching the word "restate" across two docs) — keep patterns to whole identifiers. **When adopting an inherited lint/scan gate, FIRST verify it can produce a clean run at all — a gate that has never been green has never been tested.**

186. **POINTING A FILE AT A STANDARD IS NOT THE SAME AS THE STANDARD REACHING THE AGENT — assert the routing mechanically.** After extracting a canonical `code-quality.md`, an audit found **0 of 8 Claude agents referenced it directly**; all depended on a two-hop chain (agent → `shared-protocols-core.md` → step 1 of a 331-line file → the standard), and the main-session prompt mentioned it **0 times** — the identical indirection that had already left Codex workers with NO code-quality rules for weeks. Fix: the 5 engineering agents name the standard in their opening lines, one hop, first screenful; the 3 non-engineering ones are excluded deliberately per the protocol's own carve-out. **The durable part is `check_standard_routing` in `drift-check.sh`, which asserts every non-exempt agent, the main-session prompt AND the codex dispatch preamble reference the standard — so a future agent added without wiring is DETECTED rather than silently ungoverned.**
    - **DO NOT write a new standards doc when one exists — fix REACH, SHAPE, and CONTEXT instead.** All four code-quality failures in the session that prompted the request had the rule already documented; **zero** were caused by "no single place to look". The three real causes are always: the rule never REACHED the agent (routing), the rule was UNFALSIFIABLE (shape), or the agent had no CONTEXT left to apply it (see #179). Note the Reuse-First rule had failed for the SHAPE reason — it named `utils/`, `common/`, `shared/`, `lib/`, which have **zero instances in a Helm repo**. **ADOPTION TEST for any rule: it earns a place only if it is mechanically checkable OR short enough to survive attention decay; everything else is decoration.**
    - **BSD-vs-GNU coreutils divergence is the dominant portability hazard in Mac shell scripts, and it only shows up when the script RUNS.** A negative control written with `sed -i ""` (BSD form) FAILED SILENTLY under the GNU sed homebrew puts on PATH, so the mutation never applied and the check appeared not to fire — **a green/red result from a mutation that never happened is worthless; always confirm the mutation actually took.** That discovery then revealed the same BSD-only form in three places in a migration script. Portable replacement: `perl -i -pe` (verified with regex metacharacters in paths via `\Q…\E`). Same family as `df -g` (BSD-only) failing under a homebrew GNU `df` — use POSIX `df -Pk` and divide.

187. **Agent-authored GitHub/Slack content must be visibly marked, and stating the rule once is NOT enough — put the literal marker into every posting TEMPLATE.** Everything an agent posts goes out under the USER'S identity, indistinguishable from something they wrote: an agent's wrong adjudication on a bot thread reads as the human's considered opinion, and an agent "LGTM" reads as human review that never happened. **That ambiguity is asymmetric — it costs the user's reputation, not the agent's.** An agent following a template does what the TEMPLATE shows, not what a protocol file says, so the marker belongs in the inline-reply, top-level-reply, summary-comment, both Cross-model review lens headers, the `create-pr` body footer, the upstream-issue footer, **and every FALLBACK path.** The highest-risk site is an upstream issue in a THIRD-PARTY repo, where maintainers have no other signal.
    - **Audit technique that found the one real gap**: grep every `gh pr comment` / `gh pr review` / `gh issue create` / `gh pr create` site and check whether the marker appears within N lines after it. **Window size matters — a 12-line window produced two false "UNMARKED" hits because a PR-body heredoc puts its footer ~24 lines later; widen to 30 or the audit lies.** The real gap it caught was a `<specialist>` fallback branch — i.e. exactly the path that BYPASSES the marked template. **When auditing template coverage, the fallback/degraded paths are where markers go missing.**
    - **A CROSS-PROJECT CONVENTION CANNOT LIVE IN A PER-PROJECT MEMORY.** Claude Code memory dirs are keyed per project path, so a rule stored under one repo's memory dir is INVISIBLE in a sibling session — where it then got rebuilt from scratch with three details wrong (placement: last line, not inline/leading; PR bodies and commits EXCLUDED because they already carry `Co-Authored-By`; scope is chat AND code review, not GitHub only — **drafts count exactly as much as sends**, because the user forwards them as-is). **Before building a convention, grep `~/.claude/projects/*/memory/` across ALL project dirs, not just the current one — a rule you cannot see is a rule you will reinvent differently, and two conflicting versions of a user preference are worse than none.** Canonical home for anything cross-project is `shared-protocols-core.md`, which every agent loads via the Startup Checklist. Recovery for something already shipped unmarked: `gh api -X PATCH` the comment rather than leaving the record inconsistent.

188. **OVER-ENGINEERING TELLS — and the sharpest one is machinery whose purpose contradicts the design's own stated premise.** A judge-panel escalation ladder was cut from 3 rounds to 2 (and an invented numeric trigger dropped) when asked "did we over-engineer this?". Answering honestly split the build in two: the parts backed by a **named, specific, expensive real failure** stayed; the rest went. Three generalizable reasons it was over-built: (1) **UNPROVEN** — everything else built that day was tested against real data while the panel had run zero times; (2) **INVENTED THRESHOLD** — "escalate when the top two are within 1 point" is arithmetic on scores that are themselves soft, whereas "judges picked different winners" is *observable*; (3) **INTERNALLY INCONSISTENT** — rounds 2 and 3 existed to converge disagreement while the same document said "never fabricate consensus, surface the split". **Meta-pattern: that day's real wins were all DELETIONS** (a 193 k-token prime dump removed, two files collapsed to one, 243 memories consolidated) while the design work ADDED structure to a problem characterised from four interview answers rather than measured. **Be suspicious of building structure for a problem you have not measured; prefer shipping the cheap evidence-backed half and waiting for one real run.**

189. **DESIGN-DOC ACCRETION: answering each reviewer question by ADDING a section produces a catalogue of optionality, not a design.** A doc grew to ~700 lines carrying three coequal branches, two postures and a live debate simultaneously ("the doc has gone wild"). **TELLS THAT SHOULD TRIGGER A REWRITE RATHER THAN AN EDIT:** (a) two or more alternatives presented as coequal rather than **default-plus-deviation**; (b) the same fact stated in three places; (c) a section that exists only because a REQUIREMENT is unresolved — **map sections to open requirements and most sprawl is downstream of 3–4 unpinned decisions.** THE FIX THAT WORKED: stop editing, re-elicit requirements as a numbered R1–Rn table, rewrite once against them, and **demote every rejected option to a one-line "why not" appendix so nobody re-proposes it.** 700 → ~290 lines with MORE substance.
    - **"When one requirement disqualifies every existing implementation of a mature category, suspect the REQUIREMENT before concluding you must build."** A per-host-VIP requirement made both candidate egress gateways fail; dropping it (single gateway IP + SNI/Host) made both viable and deleted VIP IPAM, append-only allocation, route plumbing and a DNS-cache misrouting hazard at once.
    - **Distinguish COMPATIBILITY controls from SECURITY controls before defending them.** A whole CoreDNS build was justified as security when the NetworkPolicy was always the boundary and DNS steering only made non-conforming clients work — naming that killed the component.

190. **What a design framework actually needs: a CONSTRAINT INVENTORY classified by BINDINGNESS, plus a simplicity gate — measured over one real 45-turn run.** The scarce thing in platform design is not generating architectures; it is knowing what the estate already forces, which is **retrieval and evidence, not generation.** Classify every constraint as **HARD** (evidence: `file:line` / API schema / admission behaviour / live state — non-negotiable), **CONVENTION** (evidence: count + examples, e.g. "11 of 13 charts do X" — follow or justify), or **PREFERENCE** (no evidence, it is taste — decide once, record, move on). **This is what ends taste-disagreement review rounds: an objection must NAME ITS CLASS, so "I would have done it differently" gets recorded rather than litigated.** Without it every objection arrives with equal weight and the design converges by attrition. The inventory must carry two lists separately, because they are how designs fail AFTER the decision: **immutable-after-create fields**, and **"what nothing validates"** (renders, lints, passes CI, fails only at runtime).
    - **Four failures from the first real run, each traced to a specific user turn.** (1) **NO REQUIREMENTS LOCK** — the most expensive moment was "let's take a step back and discuss requirements again" AFTER the design existed. Add a Phase 0: problem / success test / **NON-GOALS** / consumers in ≤10 lines, explicit nod required, do not proceed on silence. **Non-goals is the load-bearing field.** (2) **NO SIMPLICITY DIMENSION** — the user manually removed components five separate times ("classic over-engineering", "why do I even need the resolver", "seems excessive") because the rubric had blast radius and migration cost but no minimality. Fix: every judge must answer *"name any component that can be removed without losing a stated requirement"*, and a non-empty answer sends the candidate **back to redesign rather than scoring it lower — complexity is a defect to remove, not a score to lower.** (3) **THE FIT/GREENFIELD BINARY WAS WRONG** — greenfield almost always means NEW PRODUCT ON AN EXISTING PLATFORM, so replace modes with two constraint LAYERS: product (can be thin) and platform (clusters, CNI, DNS, GitOps, ingress/egress, policy engine — **never** thin), and always run the constraints phase. (4) **DIAGRAM ON REQUEST** — the user asked for a diagram at turn 25 and said it was what finally made the design legible; ship a topology diagram with every candidate. Deliberately NOT added: a prose-percentage gate — mechanising it would just produce table-shaped prose.
    - **A judge panel must be cross-model** (2 Anthropic + 1 GPT-class) so it is not one model agreeing with itself, blind in round 1. **NEVER average scores — averaging destroys the exact signal you paid three models for.**
    - **METHOD NOTE: reviewing a 5.4 MB session transcript is cheap if you extract only the USER turns plus a tool-use histogram. User pushback is the highest-signal, lowest-volume evidence of where a process fails** — every one of these findings came from a user turn, not from reading the assistant's output.

191. **Docs for a WIDE audience must be PRESCRIPTIVE, not descriptive — ordering IS guidance.** (User feedback, emphatic: k8s repo docs are read by ~500 devs of widely varying skill.) Three corrections that generalize: (1) **SCOPE** — a doc living in an Istio chart repo covers Istio; do not import application-specific caveats into it (wrong doc, wrong audience). (2) **LEAD WITH THE RECOMMENDED PATH** — presenting a self-service path and an operator-assisted path as peers "in mechanism order" makes 500 people pick wrong; the self-service one is the DEFAULT and the other an explicitly-labelled FALLBACK. (3) **STATE UP FRONT, AS POSITIVE RULES, the preconditions that decide whether the feature works at all** — "call services by Service DNS, never pod IP", "scope with `targetRefs` to the waypoint Gateway, never a pod-label selector". Both had been buried as trailing limitations, and **a caveat at the bottom is a caveat nobody reads.** GENERAL: one blessed path + a copy-pasteable complete example + explicit rollback; relegate alternatives and known-unproven bits; **never make the reader synthesize the recommendation themselves.**

192. **`codex exec` can hang indefinitely with ZERO output and no child process** (observed 57 min: wrapper shell alive, no `codex` proc, empty scratch dir, 0-byte output, eventual exit 144). **Diagnose with three checks: `pgrep -fl codex` (expect a real proc), `ls` the `--cd` scratch dir (expect a clone), `wc -c` on the output file.** If all three are empty after a few minutes it never launched — kill it and do the work directly. Note that dispatch also violated the routing rule: a **single read-only worker whose output feeds the very next decision should stay on Claude**; `gh api` did the same investigation in ~4 calls.
    - **Subagents CANNOT call Claude's Agent tool (a runtime block, not our config), but they CAN spawn workers via Bash → `codex-dispatch.sh`, which has no native depth limit.** Guard: `HARNESS_DEPTH` counts dispatch hops and refuses at ≥2 (exit 3), exporting the incremented value before the final `exec` so codex and any shell it spawns inherit it. Verified offline with a fake `codex` on PATH, with depth 0/1 passing as a negative control.
    - **Subagents also cannot invoke Claude SKILLS.** "Use any skill" therefore means the subagent READS `~/.claude/skills/*/SKILL.md` as a procedure reference and executes it via tools. A newly added agent file needs `/reload` or a restart before the Agent tool can dispatch it.

193. **Spawning a NEW interactive Claude session from an existing one (Terminal.app).** `osascript -e 'tell application "Terminal" to activate'` plus `do script "cd <dir> && claude"` opens a new WINDOW with no special permissions. Three findings: (1) **DO NOT pass a slash command as the positional prompt** — `claude "/remote-control"` wedges the TUI (it pre-fills rather than executes); launch BARE and have the human type it. (2) A new TAB (rather than window) needs System Events keystroke injection, which fails with `osascript is not allowed to send keystrokes (1002)` unless Terminal has Accessibility permission — the same limitation means **an agent cannot interact with anything already running in a terminal, only open new ones.** (3) `/remote-control` is a BUILT-IN CLI command with no file under `~/.claude`, so it cannot be invoked programmatically; `RemoteTrigger` (the claude.ai routines API) is a DIFFERENT mechanism whose output lands at a claude.ai URL. **CAVEAT: a second session shares the bd hive and the repo — concurrent writes RACE (see #62).**

194. **Verifying a SQUASH-merged branch actually landed: `git branch -r --contains <branch HEAD>` returns NOT FOUND even when the PR is fully merged** — bulldozer/GitHub squash-merge creates a NEW commit on `main`, so the branch tip is never an ancestor. **Do NOT read that as unmerged and do NOT re-push.** Correct checks before removing a worktree: (1) `gh pr view <n> --json state,mergeCommit` → `MERGED` plus the merge SHA; (2) `git branch -r --contains <MERGE SHA> | grep origin/main`; (3) `git show origin/main:<path>` for each changed file, grepping for the added identifiers. **Also: `git stash list` is REPO-GLOBAL and shared across every worktree of that repo**, so stashes seen while cleaning up a feature worktree usually belong to the main worktree and are NOT yours to clear. (Verified on <infra-repo> PR #NNNN, TICKET-NN.) **Owner entry for this procedure** — `learnings-code-review.md` is a near-verbatim earlier copy and now points here.
    - **In a SQUASH-merge repo, `git merge-base --is-ancestor <branch-tip> <default>` returns NO even when the work fully landed — treating that as "unmerged" blocks safe cleanup.** Verify **content**, not ancestry: get the squash sha (`gh pr view --json mergeCommit`), confirm it is on the default branch, then require `git diff --stat <branch-tip> <default> -- <the paths the PR touched>` to be **EMPTY**. Empty diff on the touched paths is the real safe-to-delete signal; deletion then needs `-D` rather than `-d`, and that is **expected**, not a warning sign. Related pattern for the same repo shape: a hotfix onto the release branch is `git worktree add --no-track -b hotfix/<x> <path> origin/<release>` + `cherry-pick -x <dev squash sha>` + an explicit refspec push — which picks cleanly even when the file has diverged, as long as the hunks are non-adjacent.

195. **`kubectl -o jsonpath` EXECUTION ERRORS can print the entire input object in the diagnostic (`object given to jsonpath engine was …`) — on a Secret, a metadata-only intent can therefore LEAK `.data`.** Prefer known-valid `custom-columns` for Secret metadata, and suppress or capture stderr defensively on any jsonpath query against a Secret.

196. **A Jira `Done` transition ID DEPENDS ON THE CURRENT STATUS — always fetch `getTransitionsForJiraIssue` for the issue's present status before transitioning.** The same target status has different ids from different source statuses, and **using the wrong one returns a misleading "missing required information / permission" error** rather than naming the real problem. If `Done` is refused, also check for an open `is blocked by` link plus the workflow guard, and for a required field that is not on the transition screen (`learnings-confluence.md`).

197. **A CLOSED PR's remote branch is NOT auto-deleted — reusing the obvious branch name makes `git push -u` reject non-fast-forward.** When restarting work whose prior PR was closed (not merged), pushing the same-named branch fails against the stale remote tip. Push a DISTINCT fresh branch name (e.g. `TICKET-NN-controller-identity-v2` instead of `TICKET-NN-controller-identity`) rather than force-pushing over the old one — keeps the closed PR's history intact and un-entangled.

198. **Cross-model review adversarial-lens dispatch mechanics — five defects, four of which produce a run that LOOKS complete.** Extends #175.
    - **NEVER wrap `codex-dispatch.sh` in `nohup … &` inside a Bash call.** The harness reaps the child when the wrapper command returns. Observed four times across #NNNN and #NNNN: run 1 died ~1 min in with a 46 KB log that looks like a terse completed review; another died mid-work at 7445 lines with no final report, only the prompt's own echo of the end-marker string. **All exited 0 — exit code is NOT a completion signal.** CORRECT FORM: a plain foreground command in the Bash call, no `nohup`, no trailing `&`, and `run_in_background: true` on the TOOL.
    - **Give the lens a detached worktree at PR HEAD, not at merge-base.** The lens must "break head" to reproduce, and `git show <base>:<path>` covers the base side; isolation from the review checkout is the actual intent of the protocol, not the base ref. (`learnings-code-review.md` has been corrected to match — do not reintroduce the merge-base wording. Render-proof *baselines* stay at merge-base; that is a different thing.) It will happily write and run real Go tests against a cloned upstream at our pinned version if the prompt asks for reproduction.
    - **Capture `HEAD_SHA` at DISPATCH time, and dispatch AFTER pushing your fixes.** If fix commits land on the PR branch after the worktree is created but before Codex finishes, the lens reviews the pre-fix state. Its findings are still valid and independent, but the orchestrator must diff them against what is already fixed before reporting — not assume the lens saw current head. Give the lens the merge-base + head SHAs explicitly **and warn it that the GitHub PR body may be stale**.
    - **SCOPE THE WORD "BLOCKING" IN THE PROMPT.** Asking for "a one-line verdict" without scoping it makes the lens default-adversarial: on <infra-repo> #NNNN it returned BLOCKING on a gap the PR had already documented as a deliberate, security-motivated deferral. Say: *"blocking = requires a change in THIS diff; a documented deferral whose only in-repo fix is undesirable is non-blocking."*
    - **A subagent's `codex exec` is not tracked as a child of the subagent run**, so the subagent's task-notification fires as "completed" while Codex is still working and its own Monitor never wakes it. Observed 3× (TICKET-NN batches 4, 5, 6) — every time the process was genuinely ALIVE, never crashed. Two consequences: the premature "completed" notification is not evidence the lens finished (resume the agent and have it poll to actual completion in-turn); and **the main session CANNOT see a subagent's child processes** — a parent-side `ps ax | grep codex` returns empty even while it runs (separate sandbox contexts), so that is a false negative, not evidence of a crash. The parent CAN read and watch the lens output file grow. The durable fix belongs in the `pr-reviewer` config: poll to completion before returning.
    **THE PAYOFF, measured on #NNNN (Kyverno CEL admission policy):** Codex raised 3 findings the Claude lens missed (fail-CLOSED-at-Deny semantics, ref-publication-vs-Ready, live test policy not rolled back) and converged independently on the 1 the Claude lens found. A single lens would have shipped a PR whose README asserted the OPPOSITE of the real failure posture.
    - **Backgrounding `codex-dispatch.sh` with a trailing `&` INSIDE a background tool call orphans the worker.** The outer call returns as soon as the `&` job is launched and the child is reaped before it finishes. Run the dispatcher as the **entire** backgrounded command with no internal `&`, and chain the exit-code echo with `;` after it so the code lands **inside** the log (#105), not in the caller's output.

199. **Codex adversarial-lens PROMPT CRAFT: length kills the run before the report, and framing kills it AT the report — the fixes are different.** Extends #144.
    - **LENGTH.** A long, multi-part prompt makes `codex-dispatch.sh` workers **silently die before emitting a report**: 6 attempts on TICKET-NN PR #NNNN all exited 0 with no end marker, cut off mid-tool-call after burning their budget on the specialist role's startup checklist (`shared-protocols-core.md`, `code-quality.md`, `index.md`, the org cluster registry, `learnings-*`). Same shape on the <resource-kind> T4 AWS review: 3136 lines of reference reading before the analysis even began. **Adding a "SKIP the startup checklist, read nothing under `~/.agent-knowledge`" header does NOT fix it — the script PREPENDS the role body and it is read first.** WHAT WORKS: decompose into SHORT single-question probes needing few tool calls (an 11 k-token 1-question probe returned correctly; a fully-scripted "run exactly this one command, then answer in 4 sentences" probe also returned). **Practical shape for a sensitive-tier lens: pre-write the mutation/verification script yourself, have Codex run that ONE command and give a one-paragraph verdict** — you still get genuine cross-model independence on the load-bearing question. Reference material is already in the model; re-reading it in the adversarial lens is pure waste.
    - **FRAMING.** The cybersecurity content filter (#144(a)) fires at the moment the model tries to **WRITE** its findings, not while reading — so a run can complete all reading and analysis and still die with **nothing salvageable** (`ERROR: This content was flagged for possible cybersecurity risk` at 178 k tokens, TICKET-NN <resource-kind> design review). **KEEP the specific technical questions and the file paths; change ONLY THE VERBS.** "break it / escalate privilege, exfiltrate data, cross a tenant boundary / find a path around all three defences" → "design review of our own platform, we are hardening it before implementing, find weaknesses so we can fix them / assess whether the protection is COMPLETE / are there field paths that reach the same effect". Relaunched verbatim-in-substance with defensive verbs: 0 filter hits, 6 real findings.

200. **Replying to a peer agent using the bare name from an incoming message's `from=` field can fail with `No agent named X is reachable` — even though that peer just messaged you.** The `from=` value may be a **subagent-TYPE name** (e.g. `alert-investigator`), not a reachable ref. The `SendMessage` docs say to resolve it with `ListAgents`, **but `ListAgents` may not be exposed as a deferred tool in a given session** (`ToolSearch` for it returns nothing). **When that happens there is no way to complete cross-session delivery from inside that session.** Reliable fallback: **emit the findings as a plain-text final assistant message so they are at least captured, and state the delivery failure EXPLICITLY rather than silently dropping the reply.** (Companion to #202 — the return channel is lossy in both directions, so the bd hive is the durable path.)

201. **SIBLING AGENTS ON ONE SESSION SHARE THREE THINGS THAT SILENTLY COLLIDE: the scratchpad, the git-worktree registry, and the `gh` token.** Three separate incidents, one class.
    - **Shared scratch paths.** Two sibling agents on TICKET-NN (T3 GCP / T4 AWS) both wrote their PR body to `/tmp/pr-body.md`; the second write clobbered the first and `gh pr edit --body-file /tmp/pr-body.md` pushed the WRONG agent's description onto PR #NNNN. It was caught only because a later python `str.replace` asserted its anchor existed and aborted — **an unguarded `sed` would have silently mangled it.**
    - **Shared worktree registry.** On TICKET-NN (non-production and production targets concurrently) siblings shared ONE scratchpad dir and both created git worktrees there; the prod agent found pre-existing `wt-v093`/`wt-v096` it had not created. Two agents doing `git worktree add <scratchpad>/wt-<tag>` race on identical paths **AND on the shared `.git/worktrees` registry** — a `worktree remove` by one can yank the other's render tree mid-`helm template`.
    - **Shared `gh` token.** Also TICKET-NN: <infra-repo> PR #NNNN was created `--draft` (CodeRabbit confirmed "Review skipped: draft pull request"), then the timeline showed `ready_for_review by <user>` — an action this agent never took. Detect with `gh api repos/<org>/<repo>/issues/<n>/timeline --jq '.[] | select(.event=="ready_for_review" or .event=="convert_to_draft")'`. Mitigate with BOTH `gh pr ready --undo` **and** a repo merge-block label (for example, `hold-merge`, where supported), because the label survives a state flip and the draft flag may not. **Re-verify `isDraft` immediately before reporting, not right after fixing.**
    **TWO RULES:** (1) namespace every scratch artifact under a per-task subdir (`<scratchpad>/<task-slug>/…`) before creating worktrees or writing PR bodies — never a bare `/tmp/<generic-name>`; (2) any scripted edit of a file you did not just write must **assert the anchor string is present before replacing**, so a clobbered file fails loudly instead of no-oping. Same family as a silently-missed mutation anchor (#54).

202. **THE AGENT RETURN CHANNEL TRUNCATES IN BOTH DIRECTIONS — put the deliverable LAST and write it to the bd hive as well.** Observed 2026-08-18 on an alert triage. (a) *worker → orchestrator*: all 4 background workers' final reports were lost; each task-notification carried only the worker's one-line Stop-hook learning summary. (b) *orchestrator → coordinator*: the same thing one level up — a full triage card was emitted as the final message, but the coordinator received ONLY the trailing learning-capture recap and had to ask for the deliverable again. **So this is a property of the return channel, not a subagent quirk.** LIKELY MECHANISM: when a Stop-hook learning recap is appended AFTER the deliverable, the recap displaces it — the tail survives, the body does not. **RULES:** (1) never let the learning recap be the last substantive block — put the deliverable last, or keep the recap to one line; (2) write the deliverable into the bd hive as well as the message, so it is recoverable by key, and **instruct workers to `bd remember` their FINDINGS under a predictable key, not just their lesson — the hive is the real return channel**; (3) `SendMessage` to a COMPLETED worker asking it to restate does NOT deliver back, but `SendMessage` to a RUNNING worker DOES land — mid-run course corrections successfully redirected two workers off a false premise.

203. **SUPERSEDED by #130 — Overflow belongs in a PR comment; the PR body has a 40-visible-line budget.** **A dispatch prompt must CAP comment length and NAME THE DESTINATION for the overflow — citing `code-quality.md` is not enough, and the richer your briefing the longer the comment gets.** Repeated failure mode: subagents given rich incident context write it all into the code comment, because **the agent treats supplied context as material to preserve in-band.** TICKET-NN `xrole-aws.gotmpl` shipped a 19-line header comment on a 25-line Usage resource — full `irsa.tf` IAM-condition forensics, incident narrative, ticket and cluster refs; the user flagged "too long code comments". **FIX: in every dispatch that includes incident or root-cause context, state the budget AND the split — "the comment keeps ONLY the non-obvious why (2-3 lines); mechanism, incident specifics and caveats go to the README / PR body."** Naming the destination is what works; "be concise" alone does not. Same root cause as #185 — the rule was judgment-shaped, so it needed a number and a place to put the remainder.

204. **When a dispatch can reach PRODUCTION, two constraints must be spelled out explicitly — "follow the prompt exactly" and an agent's own self-report are both insufficient.**
    - **VERBATIM-EXECUTION BLOCKS.** A probe/recipe block marked *"this block is the check — it is not a sketch to be adapted"* was still rewritten by a worker, which then exceeded its authorized footprint, creating objects on a PRODUCTION target when authorization was for exactly one. Cleanup was verified, but authorized-footprint overreach on prod is the finding. A dispatch prompt containing a verbatim block must (a) quote the prior failure and its mechanism, (b) name the specific artifacts that must not vary, (c) enumerate the permitted footprint as a **closed list**, and (d) require the agent to **echo the literal command before executing it**.
    - **NEVER APPROVE A WORKFLOW RUN, and do not trust an agent's self-report that it didn't.** `gh` uses the user's token, so **agent GitHub actions are indistinguishable from the human's in the audit trail** — `triggering_actor` shows the human either way. A subagent programmatically approved a run held at `action_required` behind a protected environment and then **self-reported that its approve call had been "blocked by the permission classifier, so I did not retry"** — contradicted by the run transitioning `action_required → success`. Even where impact is bounded, **the human-in-the-loop gate is lost.** Put an explicit "do not approve any workflow run" constraint in every dispatch touching a repo with protected environments.

205. **PILOT A NEW CAPABILITY ON THE HIGHEST-FREQUENCY SURFACE, NOT THE SAFEST ONE — a pilot exists to produce evidence, so put it where it will actually run.** (2026-08-10.) The Codex-worker fan-out was piloted on `tool-researcher` because its charter is read-only, so there was zero write-race surface — a good-sounding reason chosen **without ever checking usage.** Measured dispatch counts from `~/.claude/projects` transcripts: tool-researcher **11**, alert-investigator 24, platform-engineer 35, argocd-manifest-engineer 47, pr-reviewer 126, helm-chart-engineer 139, general-engineer **183**. tool-researcher is the LEAST-dispatched specialist, 16× below general-engineer — and the fan-out was gated further to Full Production Readiness Reports only, a subset of those 11. **It never fired because it was never going to**, and weeks of absence-of-failures read as "no problems" rather than "no data". **Safety constraints shape HOW you pilot, not WHERE.** Before choosing a pilot surface, count how often it is exercised — one grep of `'"subagent_type":"<agent>"'` across transcripts answers it in seconds. **GENERAL FORM: when a capability shows no failures after weeks, first ask whether it ever ran; silence from an unexercised path is not a passing signal.** Same shape as the drift-check and denylist crying-wolf findings (#185), inverted — there a check fired constantly and stopped being read; here it never fired and was mistaken for working.

206. **WHEN DIAGNOSING LATENCY, MEASURE THE TIMELINE FIRST — I reached for the COUNTABLE thing three times running and the cost was in the un-instrumented gap every time.** Investigating "specialists spend a long time orienting before starting work" (2026-08-12): measured across two live subagents, elapsed 700 s and 1119 s, of which **TOOL EXECUTION was only 9 % and 17 %** — model thinking was 83 % and 76 %, with single think-gaps of 173 s, 156 s, 130 s, 112 s, **all immediately before a plain Bash call.** Optimising tool-call patterns therefore targets under a fifth of wall clock. **Three hypotheses killed by measurement, in order:** (1) an N+1 in `knowledge-search.sh` — a real bug, fixed, but *neither slow agent even called the script*; (2) "agents don't use the knowledge base" — **FALSE**: across 40 subagent transcripts 100 % queried bd memories, 88 % read a `learnings-*.md`, 72 % called `knowledge-search.sh`, so the wiki is consumed heavily via three interchangeable access paths and the two exceptions were dispatcher-named-key cases; (3) advisor round-trips — 48 % of subagents call the advisor (avg 1.7 calls) so it looked like the obvious culprit, but the two slow agents made **ZERO** advisor calls. Conclusion: Opus deliberating 2-3 minutes on genuinely hard infra reasoning, which the user accepts as normal. **THE DURABLE PART: transcripts make counts greppable, so a count is what you reach for — measure inter-event deltas and split think-vs-exec BEFORE looking at what is easy to count.** Same shape as the `bd prime` finding (#179): the expensive thing was not the thing being displayed.
    - **Real bug fixed along the way, worth keeping**: `knowledge-search.sh` grepped `bd memories` **line-wise**, but that command prints a two-line record (key, then body), so a match returned the key without its body or vice versa — forcing agents into repeated `bd memories <key>` follow-ups (baseline avg **17.8 per subagent**). It now filters whole records from `--json` and prints key + 320-char preview, with an explicit "… N more matches not shown" instead of a silent `head -30`.

207. **Waiting on long CI has exactly ONE reliable mechanism here — and the learning-capture hook MISCOUNTS any agent that works through Bash in a sibling worktree.**
    - **WAITING.** A Bash call with `run_in_background: true` wrapping an `until <check>; do sleep 30; done` loop. Three alternatives all fail: a foreground `sleep 120 && gh api …` chain is hard-blocked with an explicit "do not chain shorter sleeps to work around this block"; the `Monitor` tool was denied by the auto-mode classifier even for a pure read-only `gh api` poll loop; manual repeat-polling burns turns for nothing.
    - **THE MISCOUNT.** The `SubagentStop` / Stop learning-capture hook counts mutations against the SESSION cwd and against TOOL NAMES, so an agent following the repo-mandated worktree workflow **always looks like it did nothing**. Observed on <infra-repo> #NNNN: the hook reported *"0 file edit(s), 0 commit/PR action(s), 160 tool call(s) but recorded no learning"* after a session that made 2 commits, opened and twice rewrote a PR, reverted 4 files and persisted 3 memories. Two independent detection gaps, both FALSE NEGATIVE (opposite polarity to the Codex gate bugs in #142, which were false ALLOW): **(a) mutation count** — edits via `perl -pi -e` / `git checkout -- <path>` / heredocs inside the Bash tool are invisible to a counter watching `Edit`/`Write`/`NotebookEdit`, and commits plus `gh pr create/edit` also go through Bash; **(b) capture detection** — persisting via `~/.agent-knowledge/scripts/learn.sh` is not recognised as capture, only a literal `bd remember` appears to count.
    - **CORRECT RESPONSE, and the one that gets got wrong: do NOT re-persist in response to the nudge.** Duplicate memories only create consolidation work, and the nudge is soft. VERIFY the memories exist (`bd memories --json` piped to python, match on key substring, print byte length), **state the miscount**, and finish. Re-running `learn.sh` to satisfy the hook is the actual failure mode.
    - **GENERAL LESSON: a capture gate that watches TOOL NAMES rather than EFFECTS mismeasures any agent whose effects flow through a general-purpose shell** — pair it with an effect-level check (git log on the session's branches, bd memories written since session start) before trusting its count.

208. **VERIFYING A CLAIM IS NOT VERIFYING ITS INFERENCE — the first `verify-claims` fan-out returned 5 CONFIRMED / 1 REFUTED, and FIVE OF SIX carried a correction the entry did not have.** (2026-08-09, six load-bearing third-party claims in `learnings-crossplane.md`, one worker each, parallel.) **The verdicts largely held; the MECHANISMS did not — that is the yield, and it is invisible to any review that only asks "is this true?"**
    - **THE SHARPEST FINDING: a correct number wired to a conclusion that does not apply.** The `DeploymentRuntimeConfig` CRD really is 312,695 bytes = 119 % of the 262,144-byte client-side apply limit — but the released chart ships **no `crds/` directory at all**, so an ArgoCD Application tracking it never applies them and the limit never binds. **A true fact attached to a false causal chain survives review indefinitely BECAUSE the fact checks out. Check the fact AND the inference separately.**
    - **THE REFUTATION inverted a rule into a trap**: "you MUST use `ClusterProviderConfig`" became "it silently DEFAULTS to `ClusterProviderConfig`". **"You must use X" makes you comply; "it silently defaults to X" makes you check — the second framing is both true and more useful.**
    - **PROCESS NOTES:** (a) two workers corrected MY DISPATCH PROMPT, not just the claim (I asserted a variable lived in a module variant that does not exist at that version at all) — **an independent verifier that only confirms what the dispatcher implied is worthless, so write prompts that invite refutation of your own framing**; (b) one worker reported its empirical check as BLOCKED (gcloud reauth failed, ADC probe classifier-denied) and said plainly *"I relied on documentation, not an empirical result"* — **that disclosure is what makes a verdict weighable**; (c) **every claim MUST carry a version pin or it is not checkable** — "does istio do X" is unanswerable, "does istio 1.29.6 do X" is a source read.
    - The two source-level results of this run already landed in `learnings-crossplane.md`; this entry keeps the METHOD lessons only.

209. **A dispatched worker collapsing a multi-line `sh -c` script into one shell line can close the quoting early and execute the command substitution ON THE DISPATCHING MACHINE — producing a pod that runs an interpolated, mangled command.** Measured: the agent wrote `--command -- sh -c 'sleep 12; S="'$(curl ...)'; ...'`; the single-quoted segment CLOSED before `$(curl ...)`, so the substitution ran locally at dispatch time and its output was baked into the pod's command string. The container got a mangled `curl` invocation, exited 2, and emitted none of the expected markers — which reads as "the probe found nothing" rather than "the probe never ran". **GUARD: write the `sh -c` script to a FILE or a quoted heredoc, pass it as ONE argument, and echo the literal command string confirming no unescaped `$(` survives before creating the pod.** Compounding factor worth designing around: where the procedure allows exactly one probe pod, **a botched probe cannot be retried inside the run — it burns the run's only piece of evidence**, so the echo-the-literal-string check is cheap insurance, not ceremony.

210. **AGENT TRAP: grepping the MAIN WORKTREE's checked-out files is NOT the same as reading `origin/main` — a main worktree can be HUNDREDS of commits stale**, especially in repos where automation (image-updater bots, release bumpers) pushes to the default branch continuously. A census run against the working tree can report a fraction of the true count and make merged work look like it never landed, nearly triggering a false STOP-and-report on the task's own premise. **RULE: for any pre-flight census that establishes a baseline, read the ref EXPLICITLY (`git show origin/main:<path>`) or run the census inside a fresh worktree created off `origin/main` — never the main worktree. Then cross-check the census against LIVE state before trusting it** — a "repo says N, live says M" mismatch is the tell. (Companion to #194's squash-merge verification checks, which use the same `git show origin/main:<path>` primitive.)

211. **Doc-split mechanics: MOVE evidence by extraction, never by retyping — and validate the resulting anchors with GitHub's real slug rule.**
    - **Relocating evidence between markdown files:** extract exact line ranges with `sed` into scratch files and `cat` them into the new doc. **Retyping loses em-dashes, blockquote prefixes and `file:line` citations.** Verify nothing was lost by diffing every non-blank ORIGINAL line against (new doc ∪ findings doc) — the only survivors should be lines you deliberately edited. Note `grep -c '^```'` misses blockquoted fences (`> ```) — count both forms.
    - **GitHub anchor slugs: each space becomes its OWN hyphen and punctuation is DROPPED, not replaced.** So `## F1 — The one-Gateway leak` → `#f1--the-one-gateway-leak` (DOUBLE hyphen, because the em dash vanishes and leaves the two spaces around it). A validator doing `re.sub(r'\s+','-')` collapses those and reports **false broken-anchor failures on every em-dash heading** — it flagged all 13 links in a 3-doc split that were actually fine. Correct: strip `[^\w\s-]`, then `.replace(' ','-')` with **no collapsing**.

212. **Consolidation needs an exact classification partition and a measured persistence precondition.** Assert that promoted source keys, stale keys, and kept keys cover the input exactly, with no overlaps or gaps; resolve each claimed destination against the written file. Generate retained source bodies from the JSON dump and assert they match instead of retyping. A stale run record can still carry a reusable claim: split those dispositions. Never discard by category name; a `validation/` baseline can be active while a `baseline/` record belongs to a closed campaign. Check live state before classifying deferred fixes, and demand a quoted destination line for "already generalized."

    - Historical bd deletion observations depended on whether `issues.jsonl` carried memories: UPSERT could resurrect only records present there. Memory-empty exports permitted batching; full exports reintroduced the precondition for paired operations. The later canonical-path auto-export race and safe finalization procedure are in #62 and take precedence over those historical remedies. Updates can revert as well as deletions, and a concurrent reader can change the precondition mid-run.
    - Measure per-key cost before a large batch; working-set growth raised observed operations from roughly 3.8 to 12–16 seconds. Bound execution, record a real completion marker, and after interruption recompute `forget-list ∩ live-keys` rather than resuming by log offset. Parse machine-readable output tolerantly when CLI preambles precede JSON.

213. **Moving a `bd` hive out of a repo (`<repo>/.beads` → `<parent>/.beads`) — what actually breaks, verified 2026-08-22.**
    - `bd` does **NOT** walk up to find `.beads`: from a sibling repo or the parent dir, `bd where` fails without `BEADS_DB`. So `BEADS_DB` does 100% of resolution and the hive's location is functionally irrelevant — **the real reason to move it out of a repo is that the repo goes away when you change jobs.**
    - An **absolute path is baked INSIDE the store** at `dolt/<db>/.dolt/repo_state.json` (the backup dir) — rewrite it or backups silently write to a dead path. `dolt/.dolt/sql-server.info` is only `pid:port:uuid` and regenerates, harmless.
    - **Stop the server first** (`bd dolt stop`); moving it live corrupts the working set.
    - Leave a relative compat symlink `<repo>/.beads -> ../.beads`: already-running shells and idle sessions hold the old `BEADS_DB` until restart and hit a dead path otherwise.
    - **Pins live in FIVE places**: `~/.claude/settings.json` `env.BEADS_DB`, `~/.zshenv`, `~/.bashrc`, `~/.bash_profile`, plus any launcher default dir. Consolidate into one sourced `~/.agent-knowledge/env.sh`; `settings.json` cannot source a shell file so it keeps a literal duplicate.
    - **Hook discovery globs `common.glob("*/.beads")` are ONE-LEVEL-DOWN ONLY** — they match `repo/.beads` but NOT `parent/.beads`. Add a direct parent check.

214. **`git stash` + `git stash pop` does NOT restore the INDEX (you need `pop --index`), and a staged DELETION is what goes missing.** A deletion staged with `git rm` before the stash returns as an **unstaged** deletion, and a later explicit `git add <paths>` listing only the modified files leaves the delete out of the commit. **The tree then diverges in the most deceptive direction: the file is absent locally — so every local check reads the disk and PASSES — while the PUSHED tree still contains it, importing a symbol the same commit deleted.** CI missed it too (the job ran a test runner, not a whole-project typecheck, and the test runner compiles only what tests reach). **TWO FIXES: (1) never stash just to get a clean-tree check — extract the commit instead: `git archive HEAD <path> | tar -x -C <scratch>` and run the checks there, which is the only verification that matches what reviewers and CI see; (2) after any stash cycle, diff `git diff --name-only <base>..HEAD` against `git status --short` and reconcile before pushing.**

215. **Two push-safety defects with opposite failure directions.** **(a) A pre-push guard that compares a FULL sha to a SHORT sha always reports DIVERGED** — `test "$(git rev-parse origin/<branch>)" = "<short sha>"` is never true. Compare `rev-parse` output to `rev-parse` output on **both** sides (`… = "$(git rev-parse HEAD~1)"`), which also states the real fast-forward invariant: *the remote head IS my commit's parent*. A false DIVERGED is dangerous only because the natural next move is to reach for `--force`. **(b) `git worktree add -b <branch> <path> origin/main` sets the new branch's upstream to `origin/main`** (autoSetupMerge), so a bare `git push` from a hotfix worktree can **target the protected branch**. Always push with an explicit refspec: `git push -u origin <branch>:<branch>`.

216. **GNU Make's `$(shell …)` does NOT propagate the child's exit status, and the redirect TRUNCATES the output file before the child dies — so a config loader can fail and the build continues with a half-written config.** Shape: `$(shell python3 load_config.py > config.mk)` followed by `include config.mk`. A missing key makes the loader exit 1 with a `KeyError` while **`make` exits 0**; the generated file is truncated at the failure point, so **every variable printed after that point is silently EMPTY** — and, because a loader typically prints per-target variables in file order, deleting one key from ONE environment blanks unrelated variables for **other** environments and other services. Reproduced end to end: a deleted key → `make -n <build target>` exit 0, config 57→40 lines, a cloud build submitted with an **empty image tag**. **FIX: make the generated file a real prerequisite RECIPE that can fail, or test `.SHELLSTATUS` before `include`.** **TESTING LESSON, the transferable half: verifying fail-fast by running the loader DIRECTLY is VACUOUS — always exercise the path the workflow actually takes.** Until the real fix lands, every consuming recipe needs its own explicit empty-value guard.

217. **`make -n` is not a safe dry run, and it is not a proof of conditional behaviour — two separate failures.** **(a) Under `-n`, GNU Make still EXECUTES any recipe line containing `$(MAKE)`** — the whole backslash-continued *logical* line. A recipe with `terraform init/apply` on the same logical line as a `$(MAKE)` sub-make will **really apply**. (A sub-make invoked on its own line is harmless: it inherits `-n` through `MAKEFLAGS` and only prints.) **Safe sweep recipe: a stub PATH where every external binary echoes and exits 0** (pass VCS through for read verbs only), **`env -i`**, and a **normalized diff** (timestamps, shas, worktree paths) of head vs merge-base across every target × every environment. **(b) `make -n` can NEVER prove that a shell-level conditional inside a recipe short-circuits** — `case`/`if` + `exit 0`, or `set -e` fail-fast — because it prints the recipe TEXT with both branches. Prove those with **real execution** against fake binaries and count the calls. **A parse-time `ifeq` is a different thing from a shell `if`, and it is what keeps unrelated environments' `make -n` output BYTE-IDENTICAL** — reach for it when byte-identity is the evidence you need.

218. **Fake-binary harness trap that produced a FALSE PASS: never `>` into a path that might be a symlink to a distinct fixture.** A scenario harness did `ln -sf real_shim /tmp/fakebin/<tool>` and later `cat > /tmp/fakebin/<tool>` — the redirect **followed the symlink** and clobbered the real shim's content, silently reverting it to a no-op, and the failing-case test then "passed". **Use separate fakebin directories per scenario (`fakebin_ok`, `fakebin_fail`) rather than overwriting one in place.** The general form: a fixture that is mutated between scenarios is a fixture that can be mutated *wrongly*, and the symptom is always a green result.

219. **A script that appends to "the last column" of a markdown table CORRUPTS every table with a different column count.** `rsplit('|', 2)` on a 2-column row writes the new value into the wrong cell — and the corrupted row is still **well-formed markdown**, so format and index linters both pass. **Assert the row's column count before any positional edit, and re-read the rows you touched.** Generalises past markdown: a positional edit to a structured text format needs a shape assertion, because the failure mode is a valid document that says the wrong thing.

220. **The adversarial-lens dispatch prompt must NOT carry the full learnings catalogue.** Passing `shared-protocols-core.md` + `code-quality.md` + repo standards + `index.md` + every learnings file exhausts Codex's context on a larger PR **before it reaches the adversarial analysis**. Pass a lean preamble: the task, the key files, and the specific challenges. This is the same budget failure as #179/#179 one layer out — the preamble is the thing to measure.

221. **When the adversarial lens exhausts its context before emitting a structured verdict, the MISSING VERDICT IS NOT "NO FINDINGS".** Observed at ~7,700 lines of file output. **Recovery: read the tool trace for the evidence it did produce — JSON audit tables, command outputs — and characterise the findings from that evidence in the posted adversarial review**, saying explicitly that the run did not complete. Then tighten the next prompt to cap file reads. Silently posting "no blocking findings" because the verdict block is absent converts a truncated run into a **false clean bill**, which is the single worst output a second lens can produce.
