222. **Fan-out pattern that respects "workers are read-only, the orchestrator writes": have workers draft into SCRATCHPAD FRAGMENT FILES, never the deliverable.** One distinct file per worker ⇒ zero collision; the orchestrator then assembles, renumbers and verifies. **TWO TRAPS.** **(1) A worker still writing when you read its fragment hands you a STALE PARTIAL** — one fragment held 39 entries at read time and 67 at assembly time. **Re-verify entry counts AFTER every worker has reported, not when the file first appears.** **(2) Give workers a fixed numbering OFFSET (`new = old + K`) only if nothing is dropped**; tell them to recompute against **actual final position** if they drop anything, because a real corpus is rarely contiguous (one had gaps at 51, 54-66 and 70, plus two entries out of order).

223. **Measuring how far behind a CURATED SUBSET is: compute a WATERMARK, not an entry-count delta.** When a public/sanitized twin is a curated subset of a private corpus, the raw count difference (112 vs 18) wildly overstates the work. For each **twin** entry find its best private counterpart by **token overlap** (Jaccard + min-overlap, threshold ~0.35); the highest matched private number is the **last sync point**, and only entries above it are the real delta. **String similarity on the first N characters FAILS here** (0.2-0.3) because twin entries are rewritten and generalised rather than copied — token overlap succeeds on the same pairs.

224. **Refinement to #151 ("check the twin first, it is often already correct"): that asymmetry holds for CONFIG FIXES, not for NEW SECTIONS.** On one port of 7 week-old private additions, **6 of 7 were entirely absent** from the twin; the one partial was present only as a live script grep-target, undocumented in the twin's own taxonomy table. **Patched-in-place rules exist on both sides; brand-new protocol sections only ever exist privately until ported. Grep the twin for each item's distinctive phrase before assuming either way.** **Also: a script header that cites a NUMBERED rule from a private standards file ("Rule #12 is …") is a LEAK OF STRUCTURE, not of identifiers** — the twin has no such numbering, so it must be rewritten as a path reference. Pure-logic scripts can be copied delta-wise once verified denylist-clean, confirmed by `diff private twin | grep '^>'` returning only shortened counterparts.

225. **Two knowledge-base craft notes.** **(a) Per-project retrospective FIRST, rollup second.** The owner wants a per-project review at `<project>/summaries/<project>-summary.md` — Executive Summary, Agent Architecture, How Agents Were Used (by phase), What Agents Caught, Learning Loop, What Worked Well, What Needed Improvement, Time Estimate table, Current State — and only then a cross-project productivity rollup in its own directory. **(b) The usage metrics carry NO TIMESTAMPS.** `learning-reads.jsonl` / `learning-citations.jsonl` record only read/cite, source and transcript; dating requires mapping the transcript filename to its mtime under the projects tree, which **degrades the moment a transcript is touched**. **RECORD THE NUMBERS IN THE MEMORY OR REPORT — do not plan to re-derive them later.**

226. **To let the adversarial lens EXECUTE an application module from a PR-head blob without checking the branch out, pre-empt import-time settings.** Two fixes together: put the module's required env vars in the environment (a settings object built at import time raises a hand-rolled error, not a validation error, so the message does not name the variable), **and set `sys.modules[module.__name__] = module` BEFORE `exec()`** — otherwise `dataclasses` raises `AttributeError` on `NoneType.__dict__`. That combination is what makes "reproduce this function's behaviour at the PR head" a one-command check instead of a checkout.

227. **The comment-discipline gate's 13-line docstring cap catches LENGTH, not DISPROPORTION — check proportion and duplication by eye.** One PR passed clean with **12 lines of docstring against 6 lines of code**. The two smells the line count cannot see are **(a) prose volume exceeding the code it explains** and **(b) one function's docstring restating another function's branch logic**, which becomes a second thing that has to be kept true and will silently rot. **Treat the line count as a FLOOR, not the standard.**

228. **Four mypy/lint harness gotchas that each cost an iteration, none of them obvious from the error message.** **(1) Running the type checker directly over "all the files" surfaces ~26 bogus errors from generated/migration directories**, because those are excluded by a **GLOBAL pre-commit exclude** rather than by the checker's own config — **run the real hook (`pre-commit run <hook> --files …`)** or replicate the exclude. **(2) A per-package venv lacks the ROOT's dev stubs** (`types-*`) and sibling-package dependencies, so the checker needs a **root-level** sync with the right group, not a package-scoped one. **(3) Two parallel runs sharing one `.mypy_cache` crash with `INTERNAL ERROR` — give each run its own `--cache-dir`**, which matters the moment you compare two dependency sets side by side. **(4) A git pathspec exclusion of the form `':!*conftest.py'` excluded EVERYTHING on git 2.50.1** — silently producing an empty file list, i.e. a vacuously green run. Use `grep -v` on the file list instead. Underneath all four: **a type-ignore comment is only "unused" relative to a specific dependency set** — the same `# type: ignore[...]` is required against a typed distribution and an error against an untyped one, so a lint result is a claim about the environment as much as about the code (the portable fix is the `[code, unused-ignore]` form).

229. **A citation to a renamed file must resolve through the tombstone chain.** Replacing `old-file.md#N` with `new-file.md#N` is insufficient when the latter is a retired pointer into `orgs/<org>/<topic>.md#M`. Read each tombstone until the actual content is reached, then repair the aggregate table that drives ranking. Keep append-only event logs unchanged: the filename used at the time is accurate history. Stable retired numbers make these moves followable across both filename and tier boundaries.

230. **Partition a parallel config-edit fan-out by FILE OWNERSHIP, not by finding.** Run of 5 concurrent agents, zero write conflicts: findings that touched a shared file were pulled out of the fan-out entirely (the coordinator took the shared settings file; one lane owned its whole file). A finding-per-agent split collides whenever two findings touch one file, and the collision is a lost edit, not an error. Second rule from the same run: **a lane that reads another lane's file mid-run reports STALE state** — two such cross-lane reports were already fixed by the time they arrived. Verify a cross-lane claim against disk before acting on it. Mid-run hand-off to the owning lane (rather than a second pass) worked and cost nothing.

231. **Porting agent configs to a new estate by SWAPPING PATHS recreates the same defect at the next move — replace each hardcoded "key paths" list with a DISCOVERY PROCEDURE plus an explicit NEGATIVE BRANCH.** The durable shape: a probe the agent actually runs (`find . -name Chart.yaml`, `grep -rln 'kind: Application'`, `ls .github/workflows/`, `cat .pre-commit-config.yaml`), a requirement to **report what it found**, and a sentence saying what it means when the probe returns nothing — *"no `Chart.yaml` means no in-repo charts; report that rather than inventing a `charts/` tree."* Without the negative branch an agent reads an empty glob as a **missing file** and reports absence instead of switching procedure. Scope capabilities the new estate does not exercise behind a **precondition gate** (*"confirm this estate runs a GitOps controller"*) rather than deleting the section, so the skillset survives the next move. Cost of the pattern is real but small: +5.1 KB across 6 files — pay it only where the path was load-bearing routing.

232. **Narrowing an agent's ROLE leaves stale halves in THREE places, and the machine-readable one is the easy miss.** Worked instance (executor → planner): the prose **Phase** that still said "Dispatch", the frontmatter **`description`** that still said "dispatches them on approval", and the **`tools:` grant** that still carried the dispatch tool. Fix all three or the agent keeps the capability it was told not to use. Root-cause shape: the narrowing is written as a **new paragraph at the top**, while the contradicted execution phase and every **embedded cross-agent template** sit below and are never deleted — one such file also carried a review-prompt template predating the current review contract. Replace the execution phase with a **Handoff phase defining the OUTPUT contract** (owner, lane, blocked-by, verify-by) and a one-line pointer at the canonical contract; 183 → 127 lines. Then grep the whole tree for prose that still describes the old role.

233. **A skill that restates a canonical protocol rule must NAME the protocol file as the source of truth and add "if the two disagree, the protocol wins" — otherwise the copies drift and neither reader knows which is authoritative.** Same pattern's second half: where a skill INVOKES a gate script that resolves its own base branch, **omit the argument** rather than passing a computed value — the script's resolver cannot go stale, a literal written into the skill can.

234. **Audit a harness's skill/capability table against the INSTALLED set, because a table entry naming a skill that does not exist is a failed tool call, not a graceful fallback.** One pass found **three** named skills that were not installed anywhere — two never existed under those names, one was a wrong name for a real skill. The mechanical check: diff `ls <twin>/skills` against `ls ~/.claude/skills` during every harness pass — the twin ships skills the local install may not have, and the gap is invisible until an agent tries to invoke one.

235. **Three mechanical checks find real defects in a knowledge-INDEX keyword table, and a fourth plausible one destroys recall — do not run it.** **(1) Duplicates within a row:** `collections.Counter` over the split cell (found 4). **(2) Cross-row collisions:** build `keyword → {rows}` and flag `len ≥ 2` (found 26; only the **3-way** ones are worth acting on — pick one owner or qualify each copy). **(3) Stale keywords after a portability migration:** a keyword containing a **hard identifier** (hyphen / dot / slash / digit) that is **ABSENT from its own file but PRESENT in the instance-tier twin**. That combination is decisive and found six real misroutes. **(4) The check that does not work:** bare *"keyword absent from its file"* flags ~600 terms, almost all legitimate intent paraphrases. **Gate absence on the identifier test**, or you delete the index's recall.

236. **"Zero reads of the instance tier" is a DISPATCH-WIRING signal, not a coverage signal — and one specific explanation can be ruled out cheaply.** Observed shape: the heaviest work domains had near-zero reads of their own instance files while a general dispatch/workflow file and the cluster registry absorbed nearly all of them. The tempting conclusion ("the files are wrong/unneeded") is unsupported: **search-tool consults deliver file CONTENT AS EXCERPTS, and a read-counting hook cannot see those**, so a zero is consistent with heavy indirect use. What the evidence *can* settle: whether the search tool filters the instance tier at all (here it does not — it searches every org unconditionally), and whether dispatch prompts actually carry the prior-art step. Re-measure next pass and say which explanation the evidence supports rather than asserting one. **Never archive a learning for low reads** — ranking and gap-detection only.

237. **`gh run list --commit <sha>` can return NOTHING for a merge commit that has completed runs, so a wait-loop keyed on it spins forever.** Filter client-side instead: `gh run list --json headSha,createdAt,name -q '.[]|select(.headSha|startswith("<sha>"))'`. Same post-merge check, second half: on a service with chronic error noise (>10 k identical client-library close errors per 30 min), **compare error SIGNATURES before and after a deploy, never raw ERROR counts** — the raw count is dominated by the noise and moves for unrelated reasons.

238. **A page that "does not use the full width" is almost always TWO COMPETING WIDTH SYSTEMS, not a measure that needs tuning.** Symptom: prose stops well short of tables/figures, giving a ragged right edge against a hard one. Cause: per-element `max-width` on the prose elements while tables, figures and wrappers carry none and fill the column. Nudging the prose measure (66 → 72 → 78 ch) cannot close a 300-500 px gap and only moves the raggedness. Fix: **delete every per-element `max-width`, let ONE container control the column**, and raise the base font so a column-filling line is still a sane measure (16 px → 17.5 px turned a ~97 ch line into ~80 ch). Counter-intuitively, **NARROWING the wrapper** (1360 → 1180 px) made the page look fuller — fullness comes from alignment, not sprawl. A `max-width: none` override on elements that never had one is a silent no-op; check the computed constraint before writing the override. And ask for a screenshot rather than guessing: two blind attempts cost more than one look.

239. **Switching a project between mutually exclusive DEPENDENCY GROUPS in one virtualenv corrupts the type checker's cache, and the error is an INTERNAL ERROR with no useful message.** Cause: some versions of a dependency ship `py.typed` and older ones do not, so a cache built against one is invalid against the other. `rm -rf .mypy_cache` between groups. Two sibling traps in the same family: the newer, typed dependency surfaced **27** errors rather than the 13 an earlier sweep reported, because the sweep had only checked **three source files** and missed 10 method-assign errors in one test module and 4 union-attr errors in another — **enumerate the files the CI gate checks, do not sample**; and a bare type-checker invocation reports migration/generated-file errors that the repo's pre-commit hook **excludes**, so run it the way CI does (`pre-commit run <hook> --files …`) or you chase errors that do not exist in CI (extends #228).

240. **A path-validating index checker written for a PUBLIC repo layout FAILS BY DESIGN against a live, absolute-path knowledge tree — use it as a DELTA check, never as a pass/fail gate.** It resolves every back-ticked path relative to the index's own directory or to a root argument, so bare script names and bare topic-directory names can never resolve outside the layout it was written for. Baseline on a live tree: **exit 1, 38 "missing"** — all structural. Getting it to run at all needs a shim root of symlinks. **Capture the baseline list, re-run after editing, and treat only NEW entries of a NEW CLASS as real.** The real gate for an index is the harness's own drift check (every learnings file indexed, no dangling name, See-also symmetry), which reports clean on the same tree.

241. **`bd list --json` gives you the whole task graph in ONE call, but `dependency_count` is NOT blockedness and `--limit` defaults to 50.** Three facts, each of which silently corrupts a derived graph: **(a)** `dependency_count` is not `len(dependencies)` and does **not** mean blocked — it counts blocks-edges **including ones already satisfied** (a real task had `dependency_count=2` with both blockers closed). **(b)** `dependencies[]` mixes edge types; only `type == 'blocks'` blocks — `parent-child` never does, so an epic child with an open parent is still **ready**. **(c)** `bd list` omits closed issues, so a `depends_on_id` absent from the fetched set IS a closed blocker. The working derivation is `blocked = open AND has a blocks-edge onto an id present in the fetched set`, which reproduced `bd ready` **exactly** (12 of 18 open) on a live hive, so no second `bd` call is needed. Pass `--limit 0` or blocker resolution truncates silently. **Parentage comes from the `parent` field, never from a dotted id** — a live hive holds dotted ids with `parent=None` and undotted children under an epic.

242. **Linear's GraphQL `IssueFilter` SILENTLY DROPS a nested `number` comparator inside an `or:` list.** `issues(filter:{or:[{team:{key:{eq:T}}, number:{eq:50}}, …]})` matched the **whole team** (returned the newest 50 issues of that team plus unrelated issues from another). Two shapes that do work: **(a)** `issues(filter:{team:{key:{eq:T}}, number:{in:[…]}})` — exact, and a bogus team key returns an **empty node list with no error**, so it degrades cleanly; **(b)** aliased `a0: issue(id:"TEAM-50")` accepts the human identifier, BUT **one nonexistent id makes Linear return `data: null` for the ENTIRE response** (`errors[].path` names only the bad alias), so a single false-positive identifier destroys the whole batch. **Use (a): group identifiers by team key, one `issues()` block per team.** Verified live 2026-10-10 against `api.linear.app/graphql`. Separately: Linear **personal API keys use a raw `Authorization: <key>` header, no `Bearer`** — pass the key via the environment into the HTTP client so it never reaches `argv`/`ps`.

243. **A flat `head -N` over a multi-file search stream is a relevance bug, not a size control — rank files by match count and PRINT THE RANKING.** Measured on a real corpus query: 782 matching lines across the instance tier, capped to 60, of which one verbose file took **52** while the densest and most relevant file (126 matches) got **2**. `rg` walks files in **parallel**, so emission order is arbitrary *and* non-deterministic — two runs of the same query gave different survivors. This is why a heavily-used instance file showed ~0 reads for weeks: agents were not ignoring it, **the search never surfaced it**. Rank by match count, print that ranking (it is itself the routing signal — *which file to open*), then sample N matches **per file**. Drop `-C1` when corpus entries are one long line each: context tripled a 1-2 KB line (58 KB → 154 KB of output). Under `set -euo pipefail`, `[ test ] && printf` as the **last** command in a `$(...)` loop makes the subshell exit non-zero and kills the whole script — use `if`/`then` and `|| true`.

244. **When a checklist and the router it points at disagree, fix the CONSUMER — do not add a second routing rule beside it.** A Startup Checklist step said "from the *Topic learnings* table, load every file whose domain overlaps", scoping discovery to ONE of the index's two keyword tables while the index's own preamble said both must be scanned. Net effect: **the instance tier was undiscoverable at startup no matter how good its keywords were** — the one org file named by path took 52/219 reads and every other org file took 0-1. The wrong fix (adding instance-tier routing prose to the checklist) duplicates the router, and the two copies then drift. **Standing check: does this checklist step silently NARROW the document it points at?**

245. **Harness doc contradictions come in three recurring shapes, all of them a SUMMARY copy that drifted from its CANONICAL file.** **(1) A quick-reference list must name the EXCEPTIONS, not just the allowlist** — a positive-only list reads as permission for anything resembling it (one doc listed a command as compression-eligible that the canonical rules file forbids because the compressor silently drops whole files). **(2) Never restate a NUMERIC threshold from a script in prose — name the script instead.** Prose states the POLICY in words; the scripts own the numbers. Two capture gates were described as enforcing "the same bar" at a specific tool-call count while actually using different thresholds and different regexes. **(3) A routing table that restates a tier decision will contradict the tier rule it summarises** — point the row at the rule instead of re-deciding. Same family: a verify clause demanding ALL promotions be "generalized, not project-specific" contradicted the same workflow's legitimate instance-tier promotions; **verify each tier against its OWN bar.**

246. **A storage change has THREE sites, not one: the producer, the consumer, and the prose that tells a human to do it by hand.** Moving a write target without repointing its reader leaves a **write-only loop that nothing errors on** — strictly worse than the bug being fixed, and invisible because both halves still "work". Real case: a pre-compaction hook was changed to write a file instead of a memory key, the post-compaction reader still only read the memory key, and a third site (a protocol doc) still *mandated* the manual memory write that the same file's rules forbade — which is what regrew the keys after every sweep. **Grep for the OLD target string across the whole harness before calling such a change done.** Found by a cross-model audit, not by the author's own pass.

247. **When repairing a duplicated corpus entry, the dangerous failure mode is not redundancy — it is a copy that LOST a limitation.** Two real pairs: one entry restated another's heuristic as something that "proves" a property, while the canonical entry says it is an *observed-not-documented* signal and explicitly "a cheap corroborating signal, not the sole proof"; another pair disagreed about whether a negative control must patch one site or two, so the older copy's control silently fails for the wrong reason. An agent landing on the lossy copy makes a wrong call with no way to know. **Repair pattern that works and stays append-only:** keep the heuristic CANONICAL in one entry, turn the copy into a REFERENCE that explicitly names the limitation it used to drop, mark the canonical entry *"canonical HERE, must not be restated as proof anywhere else"*, and never delete or renumber. **Screen duplicate pairs by asking "does the copy agree, or has it drifted by SUBTRACTION" — not by text similarity.**

248. **Payload-vs-locator tiering, applied: a portable entry that quotes a CENSUS is instance payload in the wrong tier, even when the surrounding lesson is portable.** The fix is **not** to delete it: convert the measurement into the MEASUREMENT METHOD ("count the pods, then count the distinct pods actually carrying the series; separately confirm whether the metric exists at all") plus the interpretation caveat ("returned nothing" = *unmeasured*, never = *zero*), and leave the figures in the instance tier. The same test catches instance-tier duplication: **perishable access/config facts (folder UIDs, datasource UIDs, permission flags, approval rules) must have ONE editable home or they diverge** — nominate a canonical entry and make the others point at it while keeping only their own distinct observation.

249. **When one hook WRITES a session-keyed file and its partner READS it, the reader must look up by the SAME key — and a short fixture id hides the bug.** Three failures shipped from one pair: the writer keyed on `session_id[:8]` while the reader used the full id, so the round trip **never** fired (and an 8-char prefix also collides across sessions); the reader then globbed `ls -t … | head -1` with **no session filter and no age bound**, so every new session got another session's state injected as its own recovered context — and because `SessionStart` also fires on the startup matcher, that was *every* session; and the path component was never sanitised, so `../` escaped the directory. Convention that holds: `re.sub('[^A-Za-z0-9_-]', '', sid)` with a 128-char cap (a UUID is 36, so never truncated), files at `~/.<tool>/<hook>/<sid>.json`, swept at 7 days by mtime, and the hook's own cache **dot-prefixed** so the sweep's `*.json and not startswith('.')` filter cannot eat it. **Prove it with a two-session isolation control and a REALISTIC id** — a write-then-read happy path with `testsess` passes because truncation is a no-op on a short id.

250. **A per-prompt hook has a chicken-and-egg cost problem: the precision filter needs data, but you must not pay for it on prompts that do not match.** Fix is a **cheap SHAPE pre-scan on the prompt that gates everything else** — no shape, return before touching the data source, the cache or the filesystem (prove it with a shim that touches a marker file: marker absent, state dir not created). Then a disk cache with a short TTL covers the rest. Measured on a real `UserPromptSubmit` hook: 24.8 ms no-match early return, 25.3 ms cache hit, 467 ms cache miss, against a 16.7 ms bare interpreter startup floor — i.e. **~8 ms of hook on the hot path**.

251. **Get a matcher's precision from DATA the system already holds, not from a bigger regex.** A hook that asks whether a prompt names a tracker ticket matches the generic `[A-Z][A-Z0-9]{1,9}-[0-9]+` shape, then keeps only team keys **the task hive itself vouches for** (derived from existing `external_ref` values plus a configured team, minus the hive's own id prefix). That single data-derived filter rejects `CVE-2024-1234`, `UTF-8`, `HTTP-2`, `ISO-8601`, `RFC-9110`, `SHA-256`, `GPT-4` and internal task ids **with zero hardcoded team names**. Where a false positive is the annoyance the hook exists to avoid, union ALL refs per task (external ref + title + description) rather than one display ref.

252. **Calibrate a learning-capture gate against real transcript distributions; the scripts own the policy threshold.** In one measured sample, 378 primary-runtime sessions had a median of 34 read-only calls (p25 22), while 112 secondary-runtime rollouts had a median of 49. An 8-call cutoff classified 95–98% as substantive and barely discriminated. Around 20 calls, observed self-capture changed from 33% below the cutoff to 63% above it; the revised gate blocked 22.5% and 30.4% respectively, compared with 94.6% previously in the second runtime. These are calibration observations, not a portable default. Keep the active threshold configurable in the gate scripts and cite those scripts from policy prose (#245).

253. **A `PreToolUse` hook returning `permissionDecision: allow` does NOT override a settings `deny` rule.** To make a permission context-scoped (e.g. allow cluster writes against dev/staging only), the deny rules must be moved to **ask** and a hook must then allow only the explicit safe forms. The auto-mode classifier blocks the agent editing its own settings file as **Self-Modification**, even through a settings-management skill — the human must apply that change.

254. **Split a command string into segments AFTER tokenizing, never by regex on `|` first.** A guard that regex-split on pipes parsed a quoted `jq`/`grep` pattern containing `|` as a real second command and blocked it. `shlex` with `punctuation_chars=';&|'`, then segment on the punctuation tokens.

255. **A guard that matches a command by exact ARGUMENT SLOTS is bypassed by any flag order the real CLI accepts.** A wrapper excluded `<cli> <sub> <verb>` via `case "${cmd[0]} ${cmd[1]} ${cmd[2]}"`, so `<cli> <sub> -R owner/repo <verb>` — which the CLI accepts — fell through to a broader two-token allowlist and got compressed, **the exact silent whole-file data loss the guard existed to prevent**. Scan for the subcommand **past any flags**; over-matching toward the safe (raw) direction is always correct. Probe flag-reorder forms whenever reviewing a command-shape guard.

256. **Porting a private harness to a public twin: look for blocks stranded inside an interpolating heredoc, and for allowlists that drifted in more copies than you expect.** In one port, a hook's checkpoint read-back block sat INSIDE a `cat <<ENDJSON` body, so the shell assignments emitted as JSON text and a `$(ls -t …)` ran mid-string, producing invalid hook output — the port must recompute the value **before** the heredoc and inject an escaped placeholder. In the same port, a command the private rules file forbids from compression was listed as safe in **four** places (a policy doc, a tool README, an autoprefixer and a wrapper whose two-token `case` arm swallowed it) and needed a three-token pre-check before the allowlist `case` (see #255).

257. **The auto-mode classifier denies shared-resource mutations (cloud build submits, `make deploy`) from a SUBAGENT as "Modify Shared Resources", even with a coordinator message — user approval only counts in the main session.** Working pattern: **the subagent prepares the exact commands and configs, the main session runs the mutating calls, the subagent resumes for read-only analysis.**

258. **Never splice a shell variable into a dispatched worker's task string with a quote break inside `bash -c`.** The quote-close/dquote-var/dquote-quote form closes the outer single quote, the **outer** shell expands the variable (unset there), and the worker receives a truncated path — in one real run a read-only root, so it silently skipped all runtime reproduction. **Write the prompt to a file and pass it as `"$(cat <file>)"`.**

259. **Codex's rollout transcript format changed: shell calls are now `payload.type=custom_tool_call`, `name=exec`, with the command inside a JS script string at `.input`** — not `function_call.arguments.cmd`. In one real session store, **5,928** records use `.input` vs 58 using `.arguments` (and those 58 are agent-spawn calls, never shell). A hook parsing transcripts via `.arguments` extracts an empty command and **silently mis-grades every session** — one capture gate did exactly this and blocked 106/112 real sessions unconditionally. Also: `apply_patch` / `patch_apply_end` no longer appear; writes ride inside `exec` as heredocs.

260. **Codex CLI telemetry is opt-in EXCEPT metrics, which default to a vendor exporter.** In `~/.codex/config.toml` `[otel]`, `exporter` and `trace_exporter` default to `none` and `log_user_prompt` to false, **but `metrics_exporter` defaults to `statsig`** — set it explicitly. Separately, an open upstream bug means `codex exec` exports traces and log events but **no OTLP metrics**; per-turn token counts survive only as span/log attributes (`input_token_count`, `output_token_count`, `cached_token_count`, `reasoning_token_count`, `tool_token_count`). Any token-cost dashboard over dispatched workers must parse span attributes, not metrics.

261. **bash 3.2 — the `/bin/bash` every stock macOS ships — parses LITERAL BACKTICKS inside a quoted heredoc nested in `$(...)` as command substitution.** It dies with `unexpected EOF while looking for matching \``, exit 2, partial output, and no other diagnostic. bash 5 parses it fine, so a `#!/usr/bin/env bash` script tests clean on a homebrew-bash machine and is **dead for every adopter without one**. Reproduced on a startup-path script that renders a regex containing backticks: 2 of 6 sections emitted. Use `\x60` in the regex. **ALWAYS smoke-test a shipped shell script under `/bin/bash` explicitly, not just `bash`.**

262. **An UNQUOTED heredoc (`<<EOF`) runs backtick text as command substitution, silently blanking markdown code spans.** Editing a PR body this way turned `` `mamba clean` `` into an empty string. Use `<<'EOF'` and pass paths via `argv`.

263. **Python in a shell heredoc that prints to stdout needs `signal.signal(signal.SIGPIPE, signal.SIG_DFL)`, or `cmd | head` prints a `BrokenPipeError` traceback to stderr.**

264. **`exec python …` at the end of a shell script KILLS the script's `EXIT` trap** — the process is replaced, so any cleanup the trap owned never runs. Drop the `exec` when a trap is live.

265. **A `ThreadPoolExecutor`'s workers are NOT daemon threads: `concurrent.futures` registers an `atexit` hook that joins them, so an interpreter exiting while a worker sits in `subprocess.run` blocks for the rest of that subprocess's timeout.** Measured: SIGTERM with a 30 s child mid-flight took **7.79 s** to exit with a pool vs **0.47 s** with per-cycle daemon `threading.Thread`. For a fan-out of N fixed tasks per cycle, **N daemon threads + `join()` is both simpler and shutdown-safe.**

266. **`subprocess.run(timeout=N)` on POSIX does `kill()` then `wait()` — not `communicate()` — in the `TimeoutExpired` branch, so a child that forked a long-lived grandchild holding the stdout pipe still returns at N seconds.** The orphan keeps the pipe open but `run()` never blocks on it (verified on CPython 3.9.6). Means a subprocess poller does **not** need process-group kill just to honour its deadline — though it still leaks the grandchild.

267. **A shell helper of the form `f() { echo "$1@$(cmd …)"; }` DEFEATS `set -e`: the function's exit status is `echo`'s (always 0), so a failing command substitution yields a truncated value and `x=$(f …)` is a SUCCESSFUL assignment.** Reproduced with four independent fault injections on a real build helper — the inspect command exiting 1, an index with no matching platform, a single-platform manifest, and an index entry missing a key — **all four** produced a value ending in a bare `@` and the build died a step later with `invalid reference format` instead of at the lookup. `pipefail` and `inherit_errexit` do **not** fix an `echo` wrapper. `local d; d="$(cmd)" || return 1; [ -n "$d" ] || return 1; echo "$1@$d"`.

268. **Config plumbing that diverges across N copies fails SILENTLY, because the missing copy has no symptom until someone sets the key.** Two instances in one tool: one of four collectors never sourced the shared config file, so any knob it gained would simply not work; and the server resolved its cache directory **before** sourcing the config and never exported the resulting paths, so a config-set cache directory was invisible to both sides and the status command could read a directory the server was not writing. Route every consumer through one shared config function; resolve paths **after** loading config with environment values winning; **export** them so every child derives the same paths.

269. **Normalize untrusted plugin output at BOTH entry points — the live call AND the cache read — because a cache file can be corrupt.** Then the builder can trust the shape. One concrete trap: a membership test like `group in hidden_set` **raises `TypeError` (unhashable)** if the plugin sent a `dict`, which 500s the whole handler; write the guard as `not isinstance(group, str) or group not in hidden`. Mirror every such field into the stale/last-good path or the rule silently lapses when a collector fails.

270. **A shared library in a plugin-discovery directory must not match the discovery glob, and should be sourced AFTER the command dispatch.** A `_lib.bash` beside `*.sh` collectors stays invisible to a `glob('*.sh')` discovery; sourcing it after the `meta`/`run` dispatch means a **missing** library still answers `meta` (so the panel renders) and only `run` exits non-zero with the shell's own error on stderr — which the host surfaces as a loud failed panel, rather than the plugin silently vanishing.

271. **Make configuration precedence `defaults → config file → explicitly-set environment variable`, with the environment winning — that is what makes a tool testable on a spare port without editing the operator's real config.** Give the same treatment to every path the tool writes. If plugin discovery runs **once at startup**, say so: a newly added plugin needs a restart, and that surprises people.

272. **A shallow `dict(state)` under the lock is sufficient when the writer only REBINDS keys and never mutates the lists in place** — a full `json.loads(json.dumps(...))` deep copy on every request is pure cost. Serialize **once inside the lock** and hand the text to the writer. Proven by replaying recorded inputs through both versions and comparing bytes.

273. **An SSO credential cache holds two different file kinds — session tokens (hours) and client registrations (~90 days) — and only the token files are logins.** For AWS: the token filename is `sha1(<sso_session name>)` (or `sha1(start_url)` for legacy profiles), so a profile→file map can be built entirely offline. For gcloud, liveness without forcing a refresh = the account in `configurations/config_<active>` plus a row in `credentials.db` (open sqlite read-only, select the account id only). Treating a registration file as a login reports "signed in" for an expired session.

274. **Verify that two secrets differ WITHOUT reading either: compare a sha256 fingerprint prefix plus the byte length.** That catches the same secret pasted into two environments — the failure a "the secret exists" check cannot see — while keeping the value out of the transcript.

275. **`git revert` has no `-q` flag, and a failed revert followed by `commit --amend` silently amends the commit you already pushed.** Both bite in the same sequence; check `git log` before amending anything after a failed revert.

276. **Discipline/lint gates invoked with `--base <branch>` use the LOCAL branch, so a stale local ref makes the merge-base wrong and the gate reports unrelated merged files as findings.** Seen twice on two different repos. `git fetch` then pass `--base origin/<branch>`.

277. **A harness-value experiment needs a with/without counterfactual.** Run each case repeatedly with the plugin and without it, report both scores and their delta, and distinguish passing a case from the plugin causing that pass. One recorded first-party evaluator exposed `--ablation with-without|none`, `--runs` (default 3, range 1–50), `--threshold`, `--json`, `--no-publish`, `--max-cost-usd`, and `--trust-plugin`, with exit 1 for failure and 2 for the cost ceiling. Its isolated runs omitted user settings, hooks, context files, MCP, memory, and skills, requiring the harness to be packaged as a plugin; a skills-directory plugin was accepted. The original note did not identify the tool or version: verify that interface before executing it, and record what the evaluation actually includes.

278. **For an A/B that spans two different agent runtimes with their REAL native configs, a local Docker-based harness is the better instrument.** Harbor (from the Terminal-Bench team) pre-integrates both CLI agents, runs locally, and `--ak 'config=<path>'` feeds each agent its actual native config — so **hooks are testable**, unlike the isolated-run limit in #277. `--skill <local dir>` injects a knowledge corpus as skill directories (giving a clean with/without-corpus arm), `-k` sets attempts per task, `-n` concurrency, and the job lock file records each skill's SHA-256 for reproducibility. **Usage telemetry is ON by default and posts to a third party — disable it explicitly.**

279. **Know the statistical floor before quoting any harness A/B result: ~253 paired examples for 80 % power on a 5 pp effect** (one-sided, α .05); 50 examples ≈ **35 %** power, 100 ≈ **51 %**; at N=500 with 20 % discordance the minimal detectable difference is ~5.6 pp. The common advice to start with "20-50 simple tasks drawn from real failures" is for **NOTICING big breakage**, not for resolving a 5 pp quality delta. **Treat any sub-10 pp harness claim at N<100 as unmeasurable and say so.** Tooling exists for the arithmetic (Wilson CI, McNemar, paired-diff CI, minimal detectable difference).

280. **A small context-corpus study does not establish a correctness benefit.** A recorded study reported 288 evaluated runs across two agent runtimes, three Python repositories and three strategies with three repeats. Correctness differences were at most 2.3 percentage points on one runtime (omnibus permutation p=1.00) and 5.9 on the other (p=0.66); equivalence bounds only excluded effects larger than 10–15 points. A probe of two convention-related near-misses found no failure-to-pass conversion from the real context file. The authors attributed those failures to implementation skill and cautioned that context-injection cost can explain apparent efficiency differences. The source citation was not retained in the original note, so these figures remain reported evidence requiring source verification, not a universal finding. Keep corpus retrieval cheap and well routed (#243, #244) and measure its effect rather than assuming it.

281. **Literature claims should name a failure mode and preserve their evidence limits.** An October 2026 research note reported five candidate lessons: (a) compaction can lose soft organizational policy; "Constraint Pinning" quarantines policy text, reinjects it verbatim and checks entailment after compaction; (b) consolidation should retire contradicted claims from active guidance while retaining citation tombstones, distinct from pruning for low usage; (c) installed skills need supply-chain review; (d) blind retries can repeat latent faults, and routing should be tested adversarially; (e) richer visible tests can widen the visible-versus-held-out reward-hacking gap.

    - Reported figures were +50 versus +6 percentage points of policy violation, about 47 tokens of pinning and 0% violation across seven models; 3,984 surveyed skills with 36.8% having a flaw, 13.4% critical and 76 malicious; three of five retry failure modes never recovering; 0% versus 100% for keyword versus model routers on adversarial cases; about +27 points of reward-hacking gap per 10× LOC; and 79% of multi-agent failures attributed to specification/coordination. Original paper citations were not retained, so verify each figure and experimental scope before using it in a decision. A plain checkpoint is not automatically constraint pinning, and external findings do not measure the local harness.

282. **Fan-out design: the axis is DETERMINISTIC vs JUDGMENT work per target, not "can it be parallelised", and building the shell version found two crying-wolf bugs the agent version would have hidden.** Learned by building three fan-outs (2026-08-09). **Deterministic per target** (`helm template` × 15 clusters) → **PARALLEL SHELL**: 15 LLM workers running 15 identical commands are slower, cost more, and can hallucinate a result a shell cannot. **Judgment per target** (4 alert hypotheses, N third-party claims, N review lenses) → **AGENTS**. Built: (1) `render-matrix.sh`, renders a chart against every host-cluster values file and diffs vs merge-base, wired into `create-pr` pre-push, `pr-reviewer` step 2.6 and the helm-chart-engineer handoff; (2) the alert-investigator hypothesis fan-out, 4 read-only lenses (metrics / logs / change / drift) dispatched in ONE message so none sees another's conclusion, each told "do not conclude a root cause, that is the orchestrator's job" (the CHANGE lens decides it most often, because most alerts are downstream of something that shipped); (3) the `verify-claims` skill, R2 at fan-out width, one worker per claim, **each claim MUST carry its version pin or it is not yet checkable**.
    - **TWO BUGS FOUND BY RUNNING render-matrix, both the crying-wolf class:** (a) without `helm dep build` in the BASE worktree the base render produces nothing and **every cluster reports "new"**, a verdict computed from a render that never happened; fixed by adding a distinct `NO-BASE` state rather than letting it read as a change. (b) The "every cluster rendered IDENTICAL = possibly inert" warning fired on a clean main branch where unchanged is the CORRECT result; now gated on `git diff --name-only BASE...HEAD -- <chart>` being non-empty.
    - **Unexpected second use: run it on a clean branch and the matrix is a FLEET FINGERPRINT, not a diff**, `crossplane-compositions` renders 2111 lines on GCP clusters vs 416 on AWS, so a future AWS cluster rendering 2000 would be visible instantly.
    - Agent fan-out ceiling stays ~10 concurrent (~15 trips rate limiting, #29); **shell fan-out has no such cap.**

## Knowledge Capture

283. **Parallel subagent dispatches for independent cluster work**: When the same operation (validation, audit, deployment check) needs to run on N clusters with no cross-dependencies, dispatch N worker subagents simultaneously rather than sequentially. Each worker gets the same playbook + cluster-specific parameters. Aggregation happens in the orchestrator after all workers return. 3x+ speedup observed on STRICT mTLS validation (3 clusters in ~5min vs ~45min sequential).

## Investigation & Debugging Discipline

284. **"grep found nothing" is evidence about your SEARCH, not about the codebase, verify absence by PATH, not by a miss**: investigating whether `pilot_total_xds_rejects` still existed in Istio 1.29, a grep landed in `pilot/pkg/xds/` and found nothing; the metric actually lives in `pkg/xds/monitoring.go` and is CURRENT. Acting on the miss would have deleted the only error signal from a control-plane check. Confirm with a repo-wide search or the upstream code-search UI before concluding a symbol is gone: especially in Go repos with near-duplicate paths (`pkg/x` vs `pilot/pkg/x`, `internal/x` vs `pkg/x`). Related, same investigation: a metric absent from a running process's metric INDEX cannot distinguish never-fired from removed (the monitoring library doesn't export a counter until its first record), read the source at the pinned version to tell them apart (Reflex R2).

## bd & Scripting Gotchas

285. **An untriggered guard is an unproven guard, test guards by TRIGGERING them, never by reading them**: a bash `preflight()` gate meant to block a run on a broken catalog contained a bare `set -e`; that clobbered the caller's `set +e`, so the function's `return 1` tripped errexit and killed the script BEFORE it printed its refusal. Net effect: the run aborted with no explanation, and the mechanism whose entire purpose was to fail LOUDLY failed silently. Reading the code did not reveal it; feeding it a deliberately broken catalog did. RULES: (1) every guard/gate/alert needs a positive test that triggers it and asserts the OBSERVABLE outcome (message printed, exit code, artifact written) (not just the internal branch; (2) in bash, never `set -e` inside a function a caller runs under `set +e`) use errexit-exempt `if` conditions and leave a comment so it isn't reintroduced. Generalizes to alert rules, circuit breakers, admission policies, and validation gates. (Same family as #34 and the "gate that emits allow" trap in #142.)

## Docs, Reporting & Knowledge-Base Craft

286. **Productivity/leverage reporting: use hard `gh` counts and the HUMAN-ENGAGED time basis**: (a) Per-program retro PR counts massively undercount throughput (doc-summed floors were 2.6x low); pull `gh search prs --owner <org> --author <user> --merged` (flag form works; the query-string `merged:` range form returned empty) and always caveat "authored under the engineer's account driving agents." (b) AI-leverage accounting basis = human-engaged hours (prompting/reviewing/deciding), NOT agent wall-clock: an autonomous multi-week soak is the HIGHEST-leverage phase (~33x), human-steered alerting work the lowest (~5x). Repeated in-prod deep-validations raise the MANUAL baseline, not the AI cost. Keep leverage tables row-sum-consistent (total-manual / total-AI = headline).

287. **Program retros OVERLAP: before adding a program row to an aggregate productivity doc, grep the existing rows' phase tables for the same work.** Sharpens **#286**. A large program's manual baseline usually already contains, as explicit phase rows, work that is later proposed as its own program row; breaking it out double-counts. **RULE: attribute a program's PRs to whichever row already scores its hours, so the PR chart and the hour table agree.** A leverage table that is row-sum-consistent (**#286**b) can still be wrong at the top line if two rows claim the same work.

## Building Validation Frameworks (fan-out sweeps, check catalogs, gates)

288. **"Already generalized" must be checked against the LEARNINGS FILES, not against other memories, generalizing bd→bd loses nothing but gains nothing either**: A consolidation pass concluded that a 35-memory validation corpus was a "completed project" whose insights were already captured, and proposed deleting all of it. An audit found the opposite: the project was ACTIVE, ~13 memories were live working context, and, the real finding, **16 `k8s/methodology/*` memories had generalized the lessons only into other bd memories, never into any `learnings-*.md`**, so a reader of the learnings layer saw none of it. **Operational rule for every consolidation pass: coverage means "I can quote the line in a learnings file", not "this feels like a known pattern".** Classify each memory as COVERED (cite `learnings-<file>.md#<N>`), PROMOTE (append FIRST, verify it landed, THEN `bd forget`), or DISCARD (session bookkeeping / run records / superseded numbers with no reusable lesson), and never invert that order, because a delete that precedes the write is unrecoverable. Merge aggressively while promoting: N near-duplicate observations of one pattern on different targets should become ONE entry that names the pattern, not N entries.

289. **A tamper-based negative control must PROVE the tamper landed, a needle that matches nothing reports PASS and reads as "the check is vacuous"**: Validating a rendered-proof script by corrupting its input only discriminates if the corruption actually happened. Helm renders scalars QUOTED (`serviceName: "ask"`, `owner: "ainlp"`), so a control written as `text.replace('serviceName: ask', ...)` silently substitutes nothing and the tampered run still passes: the exact same output a genuinely vacuous check produces, so the failure mode is indistinguishable from the bug it was meant to detect. Worse, an unanchored needle can hit the WRONG object entirely: `owner: "X"` is a substring of the `example.com/owner: "X"` label, so the tamper landed on an unrelated resource's metadata instead of the claim spec under test. Two rules: (a) `assert text.count(needle) == 1` before replacing, and print the substitution count; (b) anchor on a multi-line span that pins the field to its parent (`'spec:\n owner: "X"\n serviceName: "Y"'`), not a bare `key: value`. Corollary for any generated-input control: read the real bytes of the artifact first, `rtk` and most pretty-printers strip indentation and quoting from what they show you, so the shape you *saw* is not the shape you must match.

290. **DERIVE MUTATIONS FROM THE SPEC, NOT FROM THE TEST FILE, and never mutate a `required`-guarded field.** Extends #292 with the two things that make a `cmp`-gated mutation suite still measure nothing. (The review-side framing (assertion non-vacuity vs mutation adequacy, and what render-level suites are systematically blind to) is `learnings-code-review.md`#58.)
    - **Reading the test file to build your mutation list measures your MUTATION SET, not the suite.** The mutations then only probe dimensions the suite already asserts, so they are all "caught" and prove nothing. Build the list from the SPEC and the resource's real semantics ("what is this guard supposed to prevent?"), then ask whether the suite notices.
    - **Mutating a field the schema marks `required` reddens the test via a RENDER FAILURE, not via the assertion under test, so it proves nothing about that assertion.** Hit on: mutating `environmentConfig.gcpProjectID` / `clusterID` (`gcp-custom-roles.yaml:23-24`) fails the render. Pick a NON-required field (`providerConfigName` on gcp, `awsOidcIssuerHost` on aws) when the target is an empty-fact sweep.
    - **Apply mutations in python with `assert old in s` AND `assert mutated != original` BEFORE running the gate, and print the applied mutation**: this is the portable form of #54's `cmp` rule and it also survives the GNU/BSD `sed -i` divergence.

## bd Deletion, Priming & Corpus Hygiene (supersedes #291)

291. **SUPERSEDED by #293: An explicit export to the canonical path can itself be overwritten by auto-export; use the later verified ordering.** **`bd forget` DOES persist: the working pattern is `bd forget "<key>" && bd export --all -o <ABSOLUTE>/.beads/issues.jsonl` with NOTHING in between.** Verified 2026-08-07 (149 deletions, corpus 480 → 330) and again 2026-08-08 (160+ deletions). Three layers, and the first two diagnoses were WRONG: (a) "`bd forget` is broken", false, it deletes from dolt correctly; (b) "refresh the export afterwards", necessary but NOT sufficient; (c) **ACTUAL CAUSE: every bd invocation imports `.beads/issues.jsonl` with UPSERT semantics** (`bd import --help` says so outright, new issues created, existing updated). Upsert never deletes, so **ANY bd call between the forget and the export, including a READ like `bd memories`, re-imports the stale file and resurrects the record.** The earlier "still resurrects" test failed because *the verification read WAS the bug*. Practical form: a loop of PAIRS, `while read k; do bd forget "$k" && bd export --all -o "$JSONL"; done`, never `forget k1 && forget k2 && export`. Batch-verify at the END, never between a forget and its export. Cost ~3.7 s per record (each call re-imports the whole file), so a 130-key batch is ~8 min: run it in the background or raise the tool timeout.
    - **Pass the export path ABSOLUTELY.** `-o .beads/issues.jsonl` is relative to cwd; a session that `cd`s elsewhere writes a phantom store, the real one keeps the record, and the command still returns rc=0: reproducing the exact "forget doesn't persist" symptom for a completely different reason.
    - **A CONCURRENT SESSION SHARING THE HIVE WILL RESURRECT YOUR DELETIONS.** With three Claude sessions live on the same `.beads`, ~8 of 126 deletions came back (another session's bd call imported an older jsonl and re-exported it), and new keys appeared mid-run. **Reconcile at the end of every batch and re-run the stragglers**; do not read a resurrection as the tooling defect above.
    - **SECOND, MORE DANGEROUS FINDING: auto-export EXCLUDES memories by default** ("may contain sensitive agent context"), `export-state.json` shows `{"issues":243,"memories":0}`. Mid-session `issues.jsonl` was observed with every memory line flushed. Nothing was lost because dolt held them and `bd export --all` restored all 479, but **anything rebuilding from that JSONL would have lost the entire hive.** Consequence for backups: a raw tar of `.beads` is not enough, take `bd backup sync` (the supported restore path) AND an explicit `bd export --all` into the archive.
    - **PROCESS FAILURE WORTH MORE THAN THE FIX**: `k8s/trouble/bd-dep-remove-race` (now #177) already described this exact defect for a different subcommand, and it was re-derived over several rounds because `knowledge-search.sh` was not run before diagnosing. That is the **third** recorded instance of the same retrieval failure (#37 and #180 are the others). **The reflex fires for incident triage but not when a CLI misbehaves: search first there too.**

292. **NON-VACUITY TESTING HAS TWO HALVES AND THE ADVERSARIAL ONE IS USUALLY SKIPPED.** Proving "mutation X breaks test Y" for a handful of mutations shows the assertions are not TRIVIALLY vacuous: it does NOT show the suite covers what it claims. **The missing question is: WHICH mutations can this suite not see?** On a chart-test PR a human reviewer found **12 mutations that left 28/28 + 31/31 green**, including (a) appending a wildcard-admin statement to an inline IAM `RolePolicy` and (b) appending an any-principal `sts:AssumeRole` trust statement, **because the assertions only read `.Statement[0]`**, and (c) pointing every `Usage.by` selector at a constant, so the teardown-ordering contract the suite exists to protect **was not actually covered at all**. **Rule: for each assertion ask "what is the worst change to the thing under test that still passes?"**, especially for security surfaces (assert the FULL statement/rule SET, never an index) and for any contract the suite was written to defend. **Related structural cause: a harness not wired into CI cannot be DISCOVERED to be vacuous, local-only + documented is not equivalent to enforced.**

293. **`bd`'s AUTO-export writes an ISSUES-ONLY `issues.jsonl`, and, measured 2026-08-27, it also RACES an explicit `bd export --all` written to that same path, so the documented fix is itself unreliable.** A cold `bd` start REBUILDS the database from `issues.jsonl`, so an issues-only file silently restores an **EMPTY hive**.
    - **Observed three times:** 2026-08-22, 2026-08-25 (268 issues / 0 memories, three days after a manual `--all` had fixed it), and 2026-08-27 at the START of a consolidation, the file had 268 lines / 0 memories while the Dolt store still held all 108. The store is authoritative, so nothing was lost; a rebuild from that JSONL would have destroyed everything.
    - **NEW, and it invalidates the old remedy: `bd export --all -o .beads/issues.jsonl` does not stick.** The command reports `Exported 267 issues and 109 memories`, the file is briefly 376 lines, and within the same second it is back to 267 issue-only lines. Exporting the SAME data to a DIFFERENT path is stable (376 lines, 109 memories, verified). Read: the explicit export writes the full file, then `bd`'s own default-path auto-export overwrites it issues-only. When `-o` is not the default path both files get written and only the default one is wrong.
    - **RELIABLE PROCEDURE:** `bd export --all -o <scratch>/full.jsonl` → verify it parses to N issues + M memories → `cp <scratch>/full.jsonl .beads/issues.jsonl` → verify again → **run no further `bd` command.** A plain `cp` is not racy; a second `bd` invocation can re-break it.
    - **THE STANDING CHECK IS A FALSE-POSITIVE GENERATOR AS USUALLY WRITTEN.** The usual form (an `awk` inversion that drops lines matching the issue `_type` tag, piped to `wc -l`) counts **blank lines** as memories. One issue plus two stray newlines reports **2** against a true count of 0; on a 267-line issues-only file plus 109 stray newlines it returned **109** and read as a pass. *(The literal command is deliberately not reproduced anywhere in the harness: it is copy-pasteable, wrong, and must stay greppable-to-zero so a sweep for surviving instances is meaningful.)* Count by PARSING instead: `python3 -c "import json;print(sum(1 for l in open('.beads/issues.jsonl') if l.strip() and json.loads(l).get('_type')!='issue'))"`, and if it raises rather than printing, that is *could not measure*, which must never collapse to "zero". Same family as #64: a check that greps a text surface instead of a structured one fails in the reassuring direction. **Fixed 2026-08-27 in `snapshot.sh`, `restore.sh`, `NEW-ORG-SETUP.md`, and the public harness's `new-org-setup.md` + `consolidation-workflow.md`; `snapshot.sh` also stopped exporting straight at the canonical path.**
    - Triggering is not write-gated: a **read-only** `bd memories --json` was enough to leave `export-state.json` at `"memories":0`. Do not assume "I only read" means the artifact is safe.
    - **A CHECK THAT RUNS BEFORE THE LAST OPERATION THAT CAN BREAK WHAT IT CHECKS IS NOT A CHECK.** Found 2026-08-27 one level above the broken count: `restore.sh` asserted the hive carried memories and *then* ran `bd dolt start`, a `bd remember` per memory for the path rewrite, and `bd prime`, each of which triggers the issues-only auto-export. Restore verified, did the things that un-verify it, and exited reporting success, leaving the next cold start to rebuild an empty hive. **Fix: re-export → parse-verify → `cp` → parse-verify as the script's LAST act, with zero-memories and could-not-measure raised as SEPARATE blockers.** Make the postcondition executable rather than a comment: defining `bd() { die "…"; }` after the final step means a future appended `bd` call dies loudly instead of silently re-breaking the file. Same rule as consolidation's "verify after the FINAL write, not the first"; general form: **order every check against the last writer, not the first, and always ask what runs after it.**
    - **Ordering inside the safe procedure is itself load-bearing, and the obvious order is wrong.** Export while the server is UP, verify, *then* `bd dolt stop`, `cp`, verify again. Putting `bd dolt stop` first, which reads as the cautious choice, runs the export against a stopped server AND places a bd invocation after the file is in place, which is the exact thing the procedure exists to prevent.

294. **`bd forget` cost degrades with Dolt working-set growth, and `dolt gc` fixes it, but ONLY when run in the DATABASE dir, not the server root.** Layout is `.beads/dolt/` (server root, ~1 MB `.dolt`) and `.beads/dolt/<dbname>/` (the actual db, whose `.dolt/noms` holds everything). Running `dolt gc`, or even `dolt gc --full`, from `.beads/dolt` returns **rc=0 and reclaims NOTHING**; it silently no-ops on the wrong dir. Procedure: `bd dolt stop` → `cd .beads/dolt/<dbname>` → `dolt gc` → `bd dolt start`. Measured 2026-08-22: noms 140 MB → 31 MB, `.beads` 206 MB → 81 MB (also truncated an 18 MB `dolt-server.log`), and `forget` cost went from 12-16 s/key back to 3.87 s/key. **Verify after:** memory count unchanged and a sample body still readable.

## Shell & Harness Traps (this runtime specifically)

295. **FALSE-NEGATIVE-AS-SUCCESS: three ways a search or a poll loop reports "clean / finished" because it never actually ran.** Extends #93 with the glob family and the poll-predicate family: the shared shape is that a *failed* query is indistinguishable from an *empty* one.
    - **zsh globs an unquoted `--include=*.tf`** and aborts `grep -r` with `(eval):1: no matches found`, the search silently never runs and reads as a clean result. It bit a `pr-reviewer` pass into nearly missing fleet-wide chart-version pins. Always quote: `--include='*.tf'`.
    - **zsh globs an unquoted `gh api` URL containing a query string**: `?` is the glob metachar, so `repos/…/actions/runs?branch=X` dies with `(eval):1: no matches found`. In one Terraform-repo poll loop it had `|| true`, so the failed call produced an EMPTY status string, the "any queued/in_progress?" grep matched nothing, and the monitor emitted `ALL-RUNS-TERMINAL` while **6 workflows were still running**. Two fixes, apply both: single-quote any `gh api` path carrying `?` or `&`; and **never `|| true` into an empty var in a poll loop: use `|| echo APIFAIL` and branch on it**, so a broken query is loud instead of reading as a terminal state.
    - **Never build a job filter from a substring of the TOOL name, and never let an `all(...)` predicate be emptiness-tolerant.** In another repo, a loop watching for the `plan` job used `test("erraform|plan|Plan")` against `gh pr checks` names; it matched **`terraform fmt check`** (a different, fast-finishing job) so "all matching jobs terminal" went true in 45 s while the plan job had not even been SCHEDULED. Anchor on the exact check/job name, and **always assert the expected set is NON-EMPTY and contains the specific job before letting "all terminal" end the watch.**
    - Related search-scope rule from the same family: **a raw `grep -rn` over a terraform repo picks up `.terraform/modules/**` vendored copies of the whole repo, use `git grep` for any scope claim.**

## Codex & Session Mechanics

296. **Judging a Codex worker DONE: four independent ways the obvious test lies, and the recovery when the report really is truncated.** Sharpens `learnings-code-review.md`#26 (prompt echo defeats the marker grep) and #171(b) (a frozen log is not death) with the remaining failure modes.
    - **THE PROCESS CAN EXIT WHILE ITS FINAL REPORT IS STILL FLUSHING.** In one review a reviewer polled `pgrep`, saw it gone, read the tail, found fixture dumps plus a sandbox-rejection ERROR on a cleanup command, and posted an adversarial lens claiming the run was truncated with 1 finding. **The real report landed at line 31061 of 31595, AFTER that read, carrying four blocking findings and a verdict of CHANGES REQUIRED, which INVERTED the merge recommendation.** So `pgrep` returning nothing is necessary but NOT sufficient. Wait for the harness task-completion notification, then re-read the WHOLE file and confirm the report's own end marker. **A sandbox-rejected cleanup command is not evidence of truncation.**
    - **THE OPPOSITE OF TRUNCATION: a large, plausible output file containing NO WORK AT ALL.** `codex-dispatch.sh` can die before doing anything and still exit ~0, on one Parallax dispatch it merely echoed the prompt after `ERROR codex_rmcp_client::oauth::refresh_transaction: failed to refresh OAuth tokens for server atlassian: invalid_grant: Invalid refresh token`, leaving **677 KB containing zero findings.** Fix that worked: raw `codex exec --skip-git-repo-check --cd <dir> "$(cat prompt.txt)" > out 2>&1 < /dev/null`. **RULE: always pair the byte count with a CONTENT assertion (grep for an actual verdict string)**, exactly as #192 pairs `pgrep`/`ls`/`wc -c` for the zero-output hang. Extends #37's capture rules.
    - **`pgrep -f codex` never terminates a poll loop on macOS**: unrelated processes (Cursor helpers, npm/chrome-devtools-mcp) carry `/var/run/com.apple.security.cryptexd/codex.system/…` in their PATH env, which `-f` matches, so the loop spins to timeout long after Codex finished. Use `pgrep -f "codex exec"` (with the space) or watch the output file's mtime. Cost 20+ min of wall clock on one PR.
    - **The opposite hang: a worker can finish its report and then HANG in its own Stop hook indefinitely**, the file stops growing while `pgrep -fl 'codex exec'` still shows it alive. Do not keep polling. Detect by *file static* AND *terminal marker present*, extract the final message with `rindex` on its first heading, then `pkill` the specific process **by its full `--cd <dir>` string** so you never kill another session's worker. Cost ~20 min of dead polling on another.
    - **READING THE OUTPUT: the file is the full terminal transcript (35 k lines on a deep run) and the final report appears TWICE**, once streamed mid-run, once as the final message after the diffs of any files the worker created. **Do not tail it.** Grep the verdict/end marker, take the **LAST** occurrence, and read backwards from there (`codex exec`'s final report is the text after the last `\ncodex\n` marker and BEFORE the trailing `diff --git` dump: a `tail -N` shows you the scratch diff instead).
    - **RECOVERY when the report genuinely is truncated** (detect: the marker appears only as the prompt echo, count == 1): re-dispatch with a tighter prompt that feeds back the FIRST run's own findings and caps re-verification effort, reserving budget for the report. This preserves independence from the correctness lens while salvaging the work.

## Git, Make & Shell Traps That Ship Broken Trees (2026-09)

297. **This runtime's `sed` is GNU, not BSD.** `sed -i "" -e …` treats `''` as a **filename**: the edit still applies to the real file and then errors *"can't read"*, which reads like a failure after a successful mutation. Use `sed -i -e …`, or the Edit tool.

## Cross-model dispatch, harness maintenance and metrics (2026-10)

298. **The `date` implementation differs between the ORCHESTRATOR and the Codex worker ON THE SAME MACHINE, and it fails in opposite directions, so #126 is only half the rule.** The orchestrator here sees **GNU** `date` first (BSD `-j -v` syntax errors, #126); a Codex worker runs with a `PATH` where `date` is **BSD** and has **no `-d`**. ⚠️ The BSD direction is the dangerous one, because `date -u -d "-N min"` does not fail loudly under `set -uo pipefail`: it emits a **blank** cutoff, and every window computed from it silently becomes unbounded or empty, producing a confident **false zero**. ✅ Rule: any script that BOTH harnesses may run must do time math in `python3`, never in `date`. Same class of defect: `set -uo pipefail` (without `-e`) lets a failed command leave a variable empty and the script exit **0**, a "no results" verdict that is really "no query".

299. **Waiting on a `codex-dispatch` worker with `until grep -q DISPATCH_EXIT= <log>` exits IMMEDIATELY, because the dispatcher echoes the prompt and the prompt contains that literal string (extends #82/#185).** The wait condition must be **anchored** (`grep -q '^DISPATCH_EXIT=' <log>`) **and** require the end-marker count to be **≥ 2** in the same condition: one marker is the echoed prompt alone. The two checks are not redundant: the anchor defeats the prompt echo, the count defeats a worker that printed the marker inside its reasoning before finishing. **Canonical for this rule since 2026-11-01; #322 is the weaker restatement merged into it.**

300. **Scope a cross-model adversarial/verification lens to 2-3 questions FROM THE START whenever the work needs an upstream clone: a broad prompt reliably hits the time cap and returns nothing.** Measured twice: a 5-question prompt needing upstream source plus a repo sweep burned ~476 k tokens and died (`exit 124`, no verdict); the narrowed retry (**3 numbered questions, an explicit sub-15-minute budget, a pre-extracted copy of the code under test, and a single named test invocation**) finished in ~15 min / ~116 k tokens with a full census. ⚠️ **Never salvage a verdict from a dead run's trace.** Separately, a capacity failure looks nothing like a timeout: *"ERROR: Selected model is at capacity"* kills the dispatcher in ~60 s with **exit 1 and no end marker**. Since the dispatcher takes no model flag, the working recipe is a **bash retry loop around it** (grep the output for `at capacity`, sleep ~120 s, retry (≈8 attempts)) and **only append the exit-code line when the output is NOT a capacity error**, or the marker lands on a dead log and the poller believes it succeeded.

301. **A dispatch snippet written as a PROSE-ADJACENT EXAMPLE drifts from the protocol the same file states elsewhere, and when it does, it tends to break in FIVE places at once.** One launch block carried all of: no `timeout` wrapper (the dispatcher ends in `exec` and cannot self-timeout), fixed `/tmp/<tool>*` paths that collide between sibling agents, the **repo** directory passed where the **worktree** was meant (the script is purely positional (the 3rd arg becomes the working directory), no *"Do not dispatch further workers"*, and **no request for an end marker despite the same file requiring one when reading the output**) a check that can never pass. ✅ The five mechanics travel together or not at all; when porting such a block to another harness, port the **change** into that harness's own vocabulary rather than copying the file, and re-derive the scratch path from a **stable id** (the PR number), never `mktemp`/`$$`: an agent's shell calls each run in a **fresh shell**, so no variable survives between blocks and the path must be written as a literal. Verified with a stub dispatcher: happy path marker_count=2/exit 0, hung-worker negative control marker_count=1/exit 124.

302. **A public/sanitized twin DIVERGES from its private original in every file worth porting, so a literal copy ships broken code: port the CHANGE into the twin's own vocabulary and DERIVE the duplicate list from the twin.** Concrete divergences hit in one port: a hook used different function and stats-key names and kept a guard the private copy had dropped; gate scripts were already generalized with different variable names and different `-h` line ranges; and the twin carried a duplicated template the private copy did not have **while lacking one the private copy did**. ⚠️ **Second trap, silent: learnings corpora number INDEPENDENTLY.** A ported `#N` citation resolves to an **unrelated entry** in the destination corpus: always check the destination's max entry number before citing across the boundary, and prefer citing by title.

303. **A hook that GRADES a subagent must select the transcript by EVENT, because the obvious field is the parent's.** On a `SubagentStop` event the worker's own record is **`agent_transcript_path`**; `transcript_path` on that same event is the **PARENT** session. A gate reading the obvious field inverts in both directions: a worker that captured nothing is let through (the parent had a capture) and a worker that did capture is blocked (the parent had un-persisted churn). ⚠️ Metrics fallout is worse than the gate: of 31 rows labelled `source: "subagent"`, **27 came from parent transcripts**, the label is unreliable but **repairable**, because the row's own transcript filename discriminates. And because a dedupe marker keyed on `sha1(path)` is shared when both events resolve to one path, the two sources **split one counter arbitrarily**. ✅ Fix shape: select by `hook_event_name`; fall back with a stderr warning **and a distinct source label**; and **BLOCK rather than exit 0 when the transcript cannot be read at all**, an unreadable transcript must never render as a clean pass. ✅ Method note that beat guessing at the payload shape: `strings -a <cli binary> | grep -n <field name>` reads the actual construction site; grepping your own session logs only finds your own prompt text (circular).

304. **Usage metrics that span TWO EMPLOYERS (or two projects) with no era boundary will produce a confidently wrong gap analysis: split by era BEFORE drawing any conclusion.** Measured: of 398 reads, **256 (64 %) came from 5 transcripts of the previous employer**, which made two files look like the hottest in the corpus when they were 93 % and 100 % historical and the current estate runs neither technology. The era is recoverable from the transcript's project directory. ✅ Archive the old era rather than deleting it. ⚠️ Second method caveat: these metric logs carry **no timestamps**, dating requires mapping a transcript filename to its mtime, which degrades the moment a transcript is touched. **Record the numbers in the consolidation record; do not plan to re-derive them.**

305. **Two shell-environment traps that waste an iteration each, both silent.** **(a) `zsh` history modifiers mangle a colon-separated path:** in `"$t:clients/..."` zsh reads `:c` as a modifier, so a VCS command built that way fails on a path that looks correct in the log. Write `"${t}:clients/..."`. **(b) A worktree-isolation guard can refuse a command for mentioning the version-control tool's NAME in any compound form**, and because the scratchpad path contains that substring, any pipeline touching the scratchpad is refused. ✅ Workarounds that hold: call the binary by **absolute path** (the bare name gets rewritten by the token-compression hook and then refused), write multi-step logic to a **script file** and run that, keep each shell call to one plain command, and avoid the tool's name even inside memory text.

## Porting the harness, and the traps in its own shell

306. **Worktree-isolated subagents: the token-compression rewrite plus the isolation guard refuse every version-control command typed by BARE NAME, and disabling the rewrite does not help.** Workaround: invoke the binary by **absolute path**. The guard also refuses compound shell (`&&`, `;`, redirects, shell variables) when any path contains the VCS name: e.g. a scratchpad under a `*-repos` directory, so use single plain commands with literal paths and `Write`/`Edit` instead of heredocs. ⚠️ Separately: `terraform fmt -diff FILE` **without** `-check` rewrites FILE.

307. **The Codex worker-launch contract has NO shared home, so every skill that launches a worker carries its own copy and WILL drift.** The canonical text (timeout wrapping the dispatcher, run-unique scratch dir, "do not dispatch further workers", a unique end marker, `DISPATCH_EXIT` appended INSIDE the log, and the completion test = marker count ≥2 AND **anchored** `grep -c '^DISPATCH_EXIT='` AND re-read the whole file) lived only inside two agent **role** files. Skills cannot inherit from a role, and three skills each had a different, broken copy: no timeout, no marker, fixed `/tmp` paths, and one explicitly told the reader to grep for the verdict *"rather than reading it whole"*. ✅ Minimal identical copies each naming one canonical source is the stopgap; **a shared reference section both skills and agents point at is the real fix.** ⚠️ Offline proof that the anchoring matters: on a log whose prompt echo quotes the string, unanchored `grep -c DISPATCH_EXIT=` returns 2 and anchored returns 1; a killed worker has marker count 1, correctly failing the ≥2 test.

## Measuring whether the harness is worth anything (2026-10)

308. **Two numbered knowledge corpora that mirror each other have INDEPENDENT entry identifiers: porting by number silently points every citation at an unrelated lesson.** Learned 25 times over in one fan-out (one worker per file) and true in every file: the copies diverge the moment one side extends an entry instead of appending, merges a duplicate, or omits a tombstone. ✅ **The method that works:** classify every source entry, build an explicit **source → destination map by LESSON**, then rewrite local citations through that map; never copy a bare `#N`. ✅ **Extend an existing destination entry rather than appending a duplicate**: grep the destination for the lesson *before* adding anything (two of four lessons one pass was about to port were already there, so the right move was extending, not duplicating). ✅ **Preserve tombstones and superseded bodies on the source side** so old citations still resolve, and **preserve stable identifiers** across the move. ⚠️ **Cross-FILE numeric citations are the worst case**: they cannot be validated during a single-file port at all, so downgrade them to a **filename-level pointer** rather than assert an unverified target number. ⚠️ Specific shapes seen: one destination entry already combining two source entries; a later source entry **correcting** the advice in an older destination entry (mark the correction *inside* the existing entry); a destination entry holding a completely different lesson at the same number as a source tombstone; and a source entry whose own description of two other entries **conflicts** with what those entries say, retain the conflict for later source verification instead of inventing a resolution.

309. **A redaction/denylist gate is proved NON-VACUOUS by streaming the finished file plus one injected bad token through the same scanner on STDIN: no contaminated file ever touches disk.** The pattern: `cat <file> <(printf '%s\n' "$TOKEN") | <scanner>` must exit 0 with **exactly one hit, on the appended line**, while the unmodified on-disk file through the identical scanner must exit 1 with empty output. ✅ This satisfies a write-boundary constraint (nothing outside the authorised paths is created) and a non-vacuity requirement at the same time, which is why it is the right default for any publish gate. ⚠️ A clean scan **alone** proves nothing: it is indistinguishable from a scanner pointed at the wrong file, a pattern file that failed to load, or a tool whose exit codes you have mis-read.

310. **Counts, consecutive numbering, and a clean denylist scan CANNOT detect a worker that summarised instead of transferring, only VOLUME can.** In a 25-worker port, 23 succeeded and 2 classified ~99% of entries as in-scope and then shipped **23-27% of the bytes**. Every check written in advance passed: entry count preserved, numbering consecutive, no duplicates, denylist clean *with a working negative control*. ✅ **The two checks that catch it:** output-bytes vs source-bytes (**≥70%**) and **bytes-per-entry within 15%**, the failures ran 0.82-0.89 KB/entry against 1.39-1.76 at source. ⚠️ **Size alone is not the cause** and must not be used as the predictor: the largest file that *succeeded* came through at 97%. ⚠️ **Make the volume check ONE-DIRECTIONAL**: a destination with *larger* entries is fine (an extended entry legitimately grows), and flagging it produced false positives at 96% and 120%. ✅ Strongest form: assert **exact normalised containment of each transformed source body**, with a tail-deletion negative control; byte ratios catch gross compression, per-entry containment catches a dropped tail. ✅ State the requirement in the brief as a **Fidelity clause** ("entries are MOVED, not summarised") because a worker left to infer it will optimise for readability.
