311. **A sync WATERMARK is not a completeness proof: the gaps sit in the middle, not after the high-water mark.** One corpus had counterparts for every recent source entry while a whole band of older lessons was entirely absent, so "we are synced up to N" was true and useless. ✅ Classify **every** source entry against the destination and build the full map; the only completeness statement worth making is per-entry.

312. **Entry extraction must terminate at a root SECTION HEADING as well as at the next numbered entry, or unnumbered prose gets welded onto the preceding lesson.** Corpora interleave numbered entries with unnumbered policy blocks and section headers; a splitter that only looks for `^\d+\. ` swallows the next heading and everything under it into the previous entry, which in a publish pipeline means employer policy riding out attached to a portable lesson. ✅ Terminate on `^#{1,6} ` too, and keep unnumbered policy blocks as **separate units** with their own classification.

313. **Stable entry IDs are not always integers: suffixed forms like `12a` or `31a/31b/31c` are INVISIBLE to a digits-only count regex.** A classification gate built on `^(\d+)\. ` reports a perfect count while silently omitting every lettered supplement. ✅ Validate the full **alphanumeric** ID set alongside the numeric count, so "entry count preserved" cannot be true and wrong at the same time. (This is the same family as `#178`: deriving the next number from a regex that parses the entry *title* skips entries with nested markup and produces colliding numbers nothing warns about.)

314. **A "local reference" checker must distinguish an UPSTREAM issue number from a local entry citation, or it rejects a correct port.** Text like `istio/istio discussion #53021` is not a reference to local entry 53021; a validator that treats every `#\d+` as local flags it as a broken citation and fails a clean result. ✅ Require a qualifier (`<org>/<repo>#N`, or a URL) for upstream references and exempt that shape from the local-citation check, and when *writing* an entry, qualify upstream issue IDs for exactly this reason.

315. **Generalising a DIAGNOSTIC away changes which failure layer the reader will suspect: that is a correctness regression, not a style edit.** Found in an existing public copy: a network-policy timeout (`curl` **exit 28**) had been generalised to "connection refused", and an xDS-related TLS failure (`curl` **exit 35**) to "HTTP 404". Both rewrites point the next debugger at the wrong layer. ✅ **Retain diagnostic exit codes, pinned source paths and version pins verbatim; redact the LOCATOR (cluster, account, ticket) without touching the MECHANISM.** ✅ Likewise preserve measurement-bearing provenance when stripping private references: "measured at 97%" survives the removal of the PR number that measured it.

316. **The enclosing SECTION HEADING sometimes carries the only version scope an entry has: carry it into the destination or the claim becomes unversioned.** An entry reading "provisioning adopts a dashboard with the same uid" is version-scoped only by a `## Grafana v13.2.x` header 40 lines above it. ✅ When extracting a single entry, check its ancestor headings for a version, product edition or environment qualifier and inline it into the entry body.

317. **Porting between a private harness and its public twin is BIDIRECTIONAL: the twin can be ahead, and a one-way copy overwrites a fix.** Two cases found in one pass: the twin's worktree recipe already resolved the default branch (`git symbolic-ref --short refs/remotes/origin/HEAD`) while the private copy still hardcoded `origin/main`; and the twin's command-policy doc was properly qualified (read-only **and** compressed-output-sufficient, with measured truncation numbers) while the private copy said "always prefix" twice in one paragraph. ✅ **Diff both directions before porting, and treat a twin-ahead case as a fix to BACKPORT.** ⚠️ The layout is not 1:1, one private protocols file splits into several twin protocol files, the instruction file maps to a *template*, and the twin's own top-level agent file is a short repo-about file, not an instruction file. Build the mapping once and write it down.

318. **A rule that lives in four copies has drifted in at least one of them: "some duplication is fine" applies to deliberate reinforcement, NOT to copies that disagree.** One rule existed in four places: correctly qualified in one, described as hook-automated in a second, stated absolutely ("always prefix") twice in one paragraph of a third, and issued as a manual instruction to every dispatched worker in a fourth. Because the automation is session-global, the manual instruction was **dead** for one worker class and **live** for another: a difference no reader could infer. ✅ **In a harness pass, grep for the rule's distinctive literals across every config file and diff the wordings against each other**, not against your memory of the intent. Deliberate duplication of a load-bearing rule is fine; duplication that has drifted is the defect.

319. **When a file states a "resolve it, never assume" rule, GREP THAT SAME FILE for the literal it forbids.** One protocols file carried a git-safety rule saying *"`origin/main` is a guess, and on a repo whose default is `dev` it measures an unrelated diff"*, and 110 lines later its own worktree recipe hardcoded `git fetch origin main`. The primary work repo's default **is** `dev`, so every worktree cut per that recipe branched from the wrong base. ✅ Self-contradiction inside a single document is invisible to review and trivial to grep for; make it a standing check when editing any protocol file.

320. **A `SKILL.md` with no YAML frontmatter still loads, but it has no `description`, and the description IS the routing surface the model matches intent against.** The loader falls back to the H1, so the capability appears in the picker as a bare title and can be reached **only** by an explicit trigger table elsewhere. Found across a local skill set where 30 of 34 had frontmatter and 4 did not, including the ones most likely to be needed without being asked for by name. ✅ Check with `head -1 SKILL.md | grep '^---$'` across the whole directory. ✅ Descriptions were picked up live, with no restart.

321. **The auto-mode command classifier blocks a class of action regardless of who approved it: an approval relayed through an AGENT MESSAGE does not count.** Three confirmed shapes: a commit that widened data export (turning on query-text capture for a production exporter) was refused as *"Code That Leaks When Run"* even though the user had approved it in the main session; `uv pip install --index-url <public index>` was refused as a package-registry bypass; and reading a local credentials file was refused as credential exploration, which also means a subagent's claim about that credential's contents cannot be spot-checked that way. ✅ **Do not retry.** Hand the user a ready-made command to run themselves (they can force it), and say which classifier rule you hit. ✅ Design around it in advance: reuse an existing dependency directory instead of installing, and plan verification paths that do not read credential stores.

322. **Anchor every marker grep against a worker log: the dispatcher ECHOES your prompt, so an unanchored match fires before the worker has started.** `grep -q 'DISPATCH_EXIT='` returns immediately because the echoed prompt contains that literal (it comes from the shared protocol text the brief quotes); `grep -q '^DISPATCH_EXIT='` does not. Same for the completion sentinel: this is why the completion rule counts **≥2** occurrences, not ≥1. ✅ Use `^`-anchored patterns, `grep -c`, or `sed` on a known line range in every until-loop that waits on a worker. **⚠️ MERGED 2026-11-01 into #299, which is canonical and stricter: the anchor and the ≥2 count are a CONJUNCTION, not alternatives. Read the “or” above as a list of ways to anchor, never as “anchor OR count”: the anchor defeats the prompt echo, the count defeats a worker that printed the marker inside its reasoning before finishing.**

323. **Never run an unanchored content grep over a worker log at all: one hit can dump tens of thousands of tokens.** A worker that reads a keyword-index file will have multi-KB **single lines** in its log, so `grep -n <pattern> worker.out` prints the whole row. ✅ Anchored markers, `grep -c`, or `sed -n '<a>,<b>p'` only. (Companion to #322: the same discipline, for cost rather than correctness.)

324. **A review bot's GitHub CHECK state is not its completion signal: a check reading `skipping` can be followed by a full review with inline findings seconds later.** Observed with a 6-minute `skipping` state and the review landing after it. ✅ Poll `reviews[]` and `pulls/<n>/comments` for the bot's login until your own time budget expires; never terminate the wait on check state alone. (See `learnings-code-review.md`#157` for the review-process consequence.)

325. **Append-only metrics files with NO timestamp field still have a recency window: it is the TAIL.** Two JSONL telemetry files carried only `{kind, source, transcript}`, yet their line counts matched the rolled-up totals exactly, which means the file *is* the event sequence in order. ✅ `collections.deque(fh, maxlen=N)` gives "the last N events" with no daily snapshotting and no extra state to keep correct. ⚠️ The result is "recent by COUNT, not by time": say which one you are reporting.

326. **A per-file citation count means nothing without the CORPUS-WIDE citation rate beside it: in a harness where citation is sparse by design, every zero reads as a defect.** Measured: 197 citations against 1,378 reads of the tracked files, a **14%** rate. ✅ Any panel or report showing per-file citation counts must show that rate alongside, and must state that citations **rank and gap-detect, never prune**. ⚠️ A file with zero citations is not evidence of low value; it is the expected outcome for most files at a 14% rate.

327. **One collector owns one metrics file end to end: two collectors reading the same file make the same rows eligible for both panels.** A standalone collector was built for a metrics file that an existing panel already read, and the overlap immediately produced rows appearing in both places with different framing; folding it into the owning panel fixed it. ✅ Treat "which component owns this data source" as a design decision made once, and record it, because the second collector always looks locally reasonable.

328. **Deleting a collector leaves an ORPHAN cache entry, and a status view that globs the cache directory then reports a permanent failure for something that is no longer served.** ✅ Prune on startup, keyed on **the collector file being ABSENT**, not on the discovery pass's results, because a collector that merely failed this run must keep its last-good cache. The distinction between "gone" and "failed once" is the whole correctness of the prune.

329. **Retained agent/tool outputs are PARTIAL instrumentation, never sum overlapping sources into a single count.** Three traps in one measurement attempt: a command-wrapper's records whose original command mentions a tool include **file reads** of that tool's config and may contain no actual launches; root execution-session records are a **separate** source that must be counted on their own; and background shell outputs appear in the same tree as agent outputs. ✅ Count each source separately, state what each one can and cannot see, and say "retained outputs are not a complete audit log" rather than presenting a derived total. ✅ A subagent output file's **first record** identifies whether it is a sidechain and carries the launch timestamp: that is the reliable per-agent anchor.

330. **Building a worker prompt inside a DOUBLE-QUOTED shell argument EXECUTES any backtick in the pasted text.** A seed probe containing the markdown literal `` `raise_on_error=False` `` reached the worker as `(e.g.)`: the shell ran the backticked text as a command and substituted its empty output, so the worker was dispatched against a probe that no longer said anything. Nothing errors; the dispatch succeeds and the finding it was meant to produce simply never appears. ✅ **Put any prompt holding pasted or markdown text in a QUOTED heredoc file (`<<'PROMPT'`) and pass `"$(cat file)"`**: command-substitution output is not re-expanded, so backticks inside it stay literal. ⚠️ Inside a quoted heredoc the trailing `' \'` line-continuations you would write in a normal shell string are LITERAL characters in the prompt, so drop them. Same family as #323: the dispatcher's text pipeline mangles payloads in ways a toy prompt never shows. (ref: Codex audit of `pr-reviewer.md`; 2026-10-24)

331. **A citation of the form `file.md#N` that crosses a corpus boundary must be resolved against the TARGET corpus, programmatically: resolving it locally proves nothing.** #308 establishes that two mirrored corpora have independent entry identifiers. The consequence nobody checks: a document written in corpus A that cites `file.md#84` and is then published into corpus B carries a citation that resolves perfectly where it was written and **dangles where it is read**. Real instance: an evidence page cited `#84`/`#85`, which existed only in the private corpus, created by a consolidation that ran AFTER the public twin was last synced, so the twin's copy of that file stopped at `#82`. ✅ **Gate: regex every cited `(file, N)` pair out of the document, resolve each against the TARGET tree, and fail on any miss.** Do this for anything published across the boundary, including a page whose own quotes you have already verified: quote-checking and citation-resolution are different properties and the first does not imply the second.

332. **A redaction DENYLIST is a known-pattern gate and cannot certify a sanitization pass, and the residue it leaves is organised BY CATEGORY, not scattered at random.** Porting ~1,062 entries to a public repo: secret scanners, a 47-pattern denylist **with a working negative control**, a format check and an index check ALL passed, and a real internal index name shipped in the text anyway, the worker had genericised every other such name in that same entry and missed one. ⚠️ **The misses are predictable.** Workers bracketed every cluster, project, account and identity name into `<placeholders>` and left SERVICE names bare, so one owner-confirmed leak predicted 23 more siblings sitting in the same lists and tables. ✅ **The sweep that actually works:** extract every hyphenated-lowercase identifier from the ADDED lines, subtract a public-tech allowlist, and test the residue against the real names **a human recognises**, the agent running the sweep only knows the vocabulary it happened to meet in-session, so its list is necessarily incomplete and the OWNER is the reviewer. ✅ **Detect bare-vs-bracketed properly:** check whether each occurrence sits INSIDE a `<…>` by matching `r'<[\w-]*$'` against the text *before* the match, not a one-character lookbehind, three names hid inside longer strings (`gke-<cluster>-default-<name>`, prose like `all non-X traffic`, a bracketed policy key). ⚠️ **Do NOT simply add the found names to the denylist:** a generically-shaped name matches its own bracketed `<name>` form and then fires on every future scan, which is exactly the "short fragment trains you to ignore the step" failure a prepublish checklist exists to prevent.

333. **Porting a read-only agent or skill: inspect every companion FILE, never trust the intake file's line count.** A skill whose `SKILL.md` is short can carry the bulk of its value in a reference file beside it (query examples, command catalogues), and a port sized from the entry point silently drops it. ✅ Preserve the read-only boundary while replacing private runtime enforcement, model names, unavailable sibling skills and mandatory vendor integrations with **capability-aware** procedures rather than deletions. ✅ Validate quoted placeholders and the shell/JSON examples as well as frontmatter and links: an index/link validator covers neither agent nor skill cross-references, so a port that passes it can still ship a broken example.

334. **A review or workflow rule that lives only in a MEMORY does not execute: land it in the agent file.** Found auditing a reviewer agent: a rule about respecting author-disclosed risk existed as a project memory and a `bd` memory, was followed by whoever wrote it, and had **never been written into the agent definition**, so no dispatched reviewer ever applied it. ⚠️ Two sibling drifts in the same audit, both the same shape, the agent file had gone stale against the learnings that superseded it: (a) it placed a worker's review worktree at the MERGE-BASE, contradicting the entries that say HEAD (get the base via a show/extract instead); (b) it told the second lens to "try to BREAK" the change while the same step elsewhere warned that offensive framing trips the provider's content filter: reword to REFUTE. ✅ **Treat "the agent file disagrees with the learnings file" as a defect class and sweep for it whenever you change either**: the memory tier is the fast write path, the agent file is what actually runs.

335. **`gh pr checkout` fails with *"already used by worktree"* when the AUTHORING agent's worktree still holds the branch: review in a detached worktree instead.** The reviewer cannot and must not take the branch away from the session that is still working in it. ✅ `git worktree add -d <path> <HEAD_SHA>` to review at the exact head, and push any fix with `git push origin HEAD:<branch>`, both work from a detached HEAD and neither touches the author's tree. Complements #107 (two agents sharing ONE tree collide); this is the clean way to have two trees instead.

336. **A GitHub GraphQL 5xx is TRANSIENT and must be classified as such, or a status view turns it into a false call to action.** The response *"Something went wrong while executing your query"* plus a reference id is a server-side failure (not auth, not rate limiting) and the CLI exits non-zero, so a naive collector renders a raw `exit 1` row that reads like the human must do something. ✅ Classify that string, plus HTTP 502/503/504 and "bad gateway", into the same **"will retry next poll"** bucket as rate limiting. General rule for any status surface: an error whose remedy is *wait* must never render in the same visual channel as an error whose remedy is *act*.

337. **A long-running consolidation races CONCURRENT writers: re-sweep the store before declaring it done, and expect a late arrival to CORRECT what you just wrote.** A knowledge-consolidation pass snapshots the memory store, triages it, then deletes what it promoted. Four memories written by another session landed BETWEEN the snapshot and the delete batch, so they were invisible to the triage and would have been silently carried into the next cycle, or worse, counted as "remaining" and mistaken for the active set. ⚠️ **One of them directly contradicted an entry written minutes earlier in the same pass**: the entry recommended a client constructor parameter as the fix, and the concurrent session had meanwhile read the vendor docs and found it marked *"should be considered private"*. ✅ **Two rules: (1) diff the store against your snapshot immediately before the final report and triage anything new on the spot rather than deferring it; (2) when a late arrival contradicts an entry from this same pass, CORRECT the entry, a corpus that contains both readings is worse than one that contains neither.** Record late arrivals in the audit log under their own heading so the counts still reconcile.

338. **A blanket re-derive overwrites DELIBERATE annotations in the column it is re-deriving: exempt any cell that is prose.** An index table carried a count column in which two rows deliberately read *"n/a: prose registry, not numbered entries"*, because those files' apparent "entries" are procedural list items that the counting regex cannot distinguish from real ones. A correctness-motivated sweep that recomputed every cell replaced both with the regex's number: strictly worse information, silently, inside a change whose whole purpose was accuracy. ✅ **Before a blanket recompute, snapshot the column and diff it; treat every non-numeric cell as a deliberate override to be preserved unless you can show it is wrong.** General form: an automated correction pass must distinguish *stale* from *intentionally different*, and only the diff tells you which you are looking at.

339. **Three consecutive passes deferred a table fix for a reason that did not survive inspection: re-derive the obstacle, do not inherit it.** A maintenance note recorded that a column could not be added to a markdown table because two rows carry embedded `|` characters inside a cell, so "a column insert cannot be done by splitting on `|` without corrupting them". True, and it was carried forward unexamined for three cycles. ⚠️ **The stated obstacle only applies to a column inserted in the MIDDLE.** Every row already ends with `|`, so appending the new column at the END of each line is a pure string append that never splits anything and is safe for every row including the two. ✅ **When a hand-off note says "X is impossible because Y", check whether Y constrains the particular X you need: a blocked approach is not a blocked goal.** And when you do take the unusual route, write down WHY in the artefact itself, or the next pass will "tidy" it back into the broken shape.

340. **Stripping a diff marker with `line[2:]` eats the first real character, and the comparison then fails on text that was never in either file.** `difflib.unified_diff` emits `-old` / `+new` with a ONE-character marker; `x[2:]` therefore yields `ld`/`ew`. A content-loss audit built that way reported 28 lines as destroyed when every one was an intact in-place insertion, and the failure direction is the dangerous one only by luck: the same bug can just as easily make a real deletion compare equal. ✅ Use `x[1:]`, and **prove any "nothing was lost" claim at CHARACTER level**: run `SequenceMatcher` on the old/new line pair and assert ZERO `delete` and `replace` opcodes on the old side, which is exactly the statement *this edit only inserted*. ✅ **Pair it with a negative control** (a line you really did delete, which the check must flag) because an audit that cannot fail is the one that reads as a clean bill of health. Same family as #54 and `learnings-validation-framework.md`#91`.

341. **`uv run pytest` inside a DETACHED git worktree can resolve imports through a broken per-session venv shim, so the tests you just "ran" never touched the worktree's code.** The shim points at a scratchpad venv that may already be deleted. ✅ Run the MAIN checkout's `.venv/bin/python -m pytest` with `PYTHONPATH=<worktree>/packages/<pkg>/src` prepended so imports resolve to the worktree copy, and **prove it** with `python -c 'import <mod>; print(<mod>.__file__)'` before trusting a green run: a pass against the wrong tree is the failure mode here, and it is silent.

342. **Low CITATION coverage across a knowledge corpus is a behaviour problem, and the measurement that proves it is at SESSION level, not entry level.** Entry-level coverage (4% of 2,559 entries ever cited) says nothing about who is failing to cite. The session-level split does: of 265 transcripts that READ a knowledge file, only 27 cited one (10% of readers), and even among sessions with ≥10 reads only 17% cited. 🔑 **Split by SOURCE and the cause is unambiguous:** subagents did 2,502 of 3,432 reads (73%) but only 61 of 308 citations (20%) (0.024 citations/read against the main session's 0.266, an **11× gap**) and subagents are the exact population every dispatch prompt instructs to cite. ✅ The behavioural number to watch is "readers who cite", not entry coverage. ⚠️ **See #344: the follow-on diagnosis built on this, that citation BEHAVIOUR was the bottleneck, turned out to be wrong one layer up.**

343. **ENTRY COVERAGE is the wrong metric to steer a knowledge corpus by, because its denominator grows on a schedule the numerator does not.** Consolidation adds ~23 entries/day to the denominator while the numerator only moves when an agent types a reference, so the ratio **deflates by construction**: it will decline during exactly the periods when the corpus is being maintained best. ✅ Steer by a ratio whose two sides move for the same reason (readers-who-cite, reads-per-search), and treat any metric whose denominator is fed by your own maintenance as a progress indicator, never a health one.

344. **Measure the DISTRIBUTION of the behaviour a gate fires on before you choose its threshold: a threshold set above the population's tail is indistinguishable from no gate at all.** A citation gate shipped at "≥3 distinct citable files opened" produced ZERO effect in 3 days (reader sessions 265→299, citations 308→308, distinct cited entries 95→95) because across 281 subagent sessions **51% open ZERO topic files and only 20% open ≥3**: the median was 0, so the gate could only ever apply to a fifth of them. 🔑 **And the root cause sat further upstream than the behaviour being gated:** 79% of subagent sessions DO run the knowledge search, but only 43% both search *and* open a file, the drop-off is **search → open**, not read → cite. ✅ Before adding a gate: print the distribution, locate the actual drop-off step, and gate THAT. ⚠️ This CORRECTS the diagnosis in #342; #342's measurement stands, its conclusion does not.

345. **A search tool's OUTPUT SHAPE can be the whole reason its results are not reused: fix the formatting before building a gate to compel the behaviour.** A knowledge search printed matches as `references/learnings-<topic>.md:29:8. **text**`: a LINE number, while a citation needs the ENTRY number. The entry number was *already present* (entries are one long line each, so the matched line IS the entry line and its leading `8.` is the entry), but the `:29:8.` shape hid it, so an agent that searched still had to open and scan the file to cite. ✅ When a matched line starts with `N. `, print `learnings-<topic>.md#8 <text>`, the string that IS the citation, and fall back to the old form otherwise. **Make the cheap output the correct output**; that is a formatting change, not a behavioural intervention.

346. **A "citation rate" is a CATEGORY RATIO, not a yield: 100% is neither the target nor meaningful, so do not read a low one as a failure.** A read is "I opened this file"; a citation is "I wrote `[file.md#N]` in my output". One read legitimately covers many entries and legitimately produces zero citations when the file informed the work without a specific entry being quoted. 📐 **Anchor on the baseline that killed the experiment:** citations were demoted to secondary telemetry at roughly **6 cited entries in two weeks** of heavy work; a corpus at 302 citations across 95 distinct entries is ~50× that. ⚠️ Also check the DENOMINATOR before comparing two figures: an independent recount over *all* read files gave 11% against the tool's 12% purely because the tool correctly excludes protocols/playbooks/index files. And exclude files whose `^N. ` lines are procedural list items rather than citable entries, or they show up forever in a "read, never cited" warning they can never leave.

347. **When a corpus is split into TIERS, every instruction that teaches how to REFERENCE it must name EVERY tier: an omitted tier reads as "not referenceable", and the omission is invisible because the instruction stays true about the tier it does name.** All three canonical places teaching citation named only `[learnings-<file>.md#<N>]` and never `[orgs/<org>/<file>.md#<N>]`, so a worker reading any of them would reasonably conclude the instance tier was not citable. The data shows it: **86 portable entries cited against 11 instance ones**, while the instance tier held about a third of the corpus (838 of 2,572 entries) and took 34% of a consolidation pass's promotions. ✅ Whenever a tier is ADDED, diff the instruction set against the tier set, and treat the resulting repetition as deliberate reinforcement of a load-bearing rule, not drift.

348. **Adoption verdict for a multi-persona review plugin (`google/elephant-goldfish-model`, Apache-2.0, 2026-10-27): DO NOT INSTALL, harvest the ideas, and the deciding axis was ISOLATION TYPE, not feature count.** Its isolation axis is **context only**: all of its reviewer personas run on the host harness's single model, so a two-lens cross-model review and a multi-model judge panel already cover context isolation *and* add the model axis it lacks. Three supporting objections, graded after a cross-model check (see #354): cost lands on the host subscription against a standing "offload to the other plan" criterion; scope mismatch (spec-driven feature development vs. infra PRs that need no design doc); and house-style conflicts in its mandated question/option conventions. 🔑 **What was genuinely missing from ours and worth taking:** a naive-reader/comprehension lens (every term defined inline or linked), an implementation-readiness "zero-decision" test, design-doc-vs-code drift detection, and **per-skill structural lint**, they ship frontmatter/link-integrity/load-limit tests plus per-skill scenarios, and a hand audit of ours found 4 broken frontmatters. ✅ *Harvest the lens, decline the runtime* is usually the right shape for a tool whose value is its checklist.

349. **ZERO-DECISION GATE for any dispatch that asks a worker to BUILD: the prompt FAILS if it leaves an architectural or behavioural choice unmade**, schema, API path, dependency, or a new edge case's handling. If the implementer must decide it, the dispatcher has not finished writing the brief, and two workers given the same prompt would produce two different systems. ⚠️ **Scope it deliberately:** ordinary implementation judgment stays with the worker, and research / exploration / triage dispatches are **exempt**, because for those the decision IS the output. Applying the gate to a research brief just produces a brief that presumes its own answer.

350. **COLD-READER GATE for a finished design doc: dispatch one cheap worker with the startup preamble explicitly OFF (no memory prime, no index, no knowledge search, no cluster registry, no learnings) and have it list every term it cannot resolve from the document and its links.** 🔑 **The priming ban IS the mechanism, and loading the corpus into the reader is an ANTI-PATTERN:** a primed reader silently resolves terms the document never defined and reports a self-contained doc, so the gate returns a clean result precisely when it is most needed. ⚠️ The earlier design judges cannot substitute: they have read the constraint table, which is where half the undefined terms are defined.

351. **ISOLATION GATE for a two-lens review: the lenses CLAIMED independence and nothing checked it, grep the second lens's prompt for copy-paste of the first lens's findings before dispatch, and require the lens to emit an explicit isolation marker.** Shape: take `cut -c1-60` of each blocking finding, `grep -i -F -f` the prompt file, and run a **planted-finding positive control** so a passing gate is not vacuous. ⚠️ **Both of its limits belong in the protocol, not in the author's head:** proven on 4 cases (clean 0, control 2, leaked 1, **paraphrase 0**), the grep catches **copy-paste only**, what actually covers paraphrase is the structural rule that *the second prompt is built from the open-pass seeds, never from the findings*. A gate whose coverage boundary is implicit will be trusted past it. (Two defects found in this gate after shipping: #352, #353.)

352. **Test the SEQUENCE, not the command: a gate written as a documented command sequence must be run END TO END in a scratch dir with a planted failure, because proving the individual steps proves only the steps.** Two gates shipped in one week were both non-functional for reasons hand-made fixtures could not see. (1) The gate said "append a probe line to `$D/prompt.txt` and grep it", and the very next block began `cat > $D/prompt.txt <<EOF`, which **truncates that file**, so the grep ran against a file that did not exist yet and the probe never reached the worker. The greps had been proven on fixtures, which proved the greps. ✅ Fix: the check must live INSIDE the launch block, after the heredoc closes and before the dispatch line. (2) The same gate asserted `grep -c '<MARKER>' == 1`, but the dispatcher echoes the prompt into the same log and the prompt now CONTAINS that marker, so the correct assertion is **≥ 2**: the author had restated that exact rule in prose and then violated it in his own check (see #299). (3) **Ported code is not done when the lines match:** a function ported verbatim into a twin tree asked for a dict key the twin's parser never produced, so every hook invocation raised `KeyError` and the capture gate silently stopped running. ✅ **After porting, run the fixtures IN THE TARGET TREE.**

353. **`grep -F -f <patternfile>` with an EMPTY pattern file matches EVERY line, so any gate that greps one artifact for strings extracted from another reports a spurious total "hit" whenever the extraction is empty.** Seen on a review isolation gate: with 0 blocking findings the pattern file is empty, the grep returns all lines, and the gate reads as a maximal leak when in fact **there is nothing to leak and the prompt is seeds-only by construction**. ✅ Guard with `[ -s patterns ] || skip` and state the zero-input case in the gate's own protocol text, because the failure presents as the most alarming possible result at the least alarming possible moment.

354. **A cross-model check refuted three of four supporting claims while leaving the VERDICT standing: record the corrections, not just the conclusion, because the next decision reuses the claims and not the verdict.** The three: (1) a persona count was inflated by counting orchestrator files as reviewers; (2) a "15-16 model calls per review" figure came from a test that ran *brainstorm + draft + review* together, a review alone is 14 calls and its stages run **sequentially**, so peak concurrency is 3-4 and the concurrency objection was simply invalid, leaving only a weakened cost objection; (3) "N skills and zero tests" was wrong because **`find(1) does not follow symlinks`** and 23 of 58 entries were symlinks. ⚠️ That third one generalises: **a count built with `find` over a tree that contains symlinks is a count of the tree, not of the content**, use `find -L` (and accept the cycle risk) or resolve the links explicitly. 🔑 A verdict that survives its evidence being corrected is a verdict that was over-argued; keep the surviving argument and drop the rest.

355. **ALWAYS `mktemp -d` a run-unique directory for a render/scratch harness: a FIXED path under a shared scratchpad gets clobbered mid-run by a sibling agent, and the result is a plausible-but-wrong render rather than an error.** On a re-review, a hand-copied cloud module silently became a DIFFERENT cloud's variant between two Bash calls; the render it produced was mathematically impossible (a derived memory limit above the node's allocatable) and nearly shipped as a finding. ✅ Two rules, both needed: **(a)** unique scratch dir per run for the harness *and* for any worker worktree; **(b)** `grep`-verify the harness file's own content (the machine types, the allocatable constants, whatever identifies WHICH module it is) before trusting ANY output from it. ⚠️ A negative control only works if the harness is the file you think it is; the control passed here while the harness was wrong.

356. **`make -n` is NOT a safe dry run: GNU make EXECUTES any recipe line containing `$(MAKE)` even under `-n`, `-q` or `-t`.** A deploy target whose single backslash-continued recipe line embedded `$(MAKE)` would have run `terraform init`/`apply` for real during what was meant to be a review-only dry run. ✅ Grep every target you intend to `-n` for `$(MAKE)` first; for the ones that have it, build a **stub PATH** (dummy `terraform`/`gcloud`/`aws`/`kubectl` first on `PATH`) and run it under the stubs, or skip that target entirely. Targets without `$(MAKE)` dry-run cleanly, so the split is mechanical, but it is not the default assumption.

357. **A `kubectl`/`helm` write guard that allowlists contexts parses `--context` LITERALLY, and both halves of that bite.** (a) A context missing from the allowlist is denied for **every** write, including `create ns`/`create pod` in a throwaway namespace, so dispatching a worker to run a live repro on an un-allowlisted cluster fails at its first command, and the fix is for the human to add the context (or run the apply themselves) **before** dispatch, never to route around the guard with an alias context. (b) Because the parse is literal, a shell loop writing `--context $c` is blocked regardless of what `$c` holds: **write one explicit context name per command**.

358. **Inside a worktree-isolated agent, a plain version-control command can be REFUSED even when it is read-only, because a token-reducing `PreToolUse` rewrite hook changes the command and the worktree guard then cannot prove the rewritten operand stays inside the worktree.** ✅ Prefix with `/usr/bin/env`, one plain command per call, **no globs, pipes or chains**. ⚠️ The same guard refuses any shell command whose TEXT mentions the VCS name, including a memory-write whose body happens to quote it, so phrase such writes to avoid the literal token, or write them through a non-shell path.

359. **An agent coding harness will DENY staging or committing a change that removes another commit's work (`git revert --no-commit`, `git add -A` of a deleted file) even when a coordinator relays an owner's direction to drop that commit. Do not work around it.** ✅ Finish the independent work (builds, measurements, drafts, a ready patch file) and hand back the **exact commands** for the owner to approve and run. ⚠️ A related conflict: a reviewer agent's own "never force-push" rule collides with a coordinator asking for `--force-with-lease`; a **revert commit gives the same final PR diff with an ordinary push**, so prefer it and leave the history rewrite to the human.

360. **Subagents over-comment generated config (~35% comment lines on Terraform locals) even when the dispatch explicitly cites a minimal-comment standard and demands WHY-only one-liners: the abstract instruction does not land.** ✅ Put a **concrete density ceiling and a one-line worked example** in the dispatch prompt, or budget an explicit comment-trim pass afterwards. Two independent workers on the same project did it identically, so treat it as the default behaviour to design against rather than a worker defect to correct case by case.

361. **Validate the full COMPOSED prompt for an autonomous-profile change, not the named template you edited.** An agent's system prompt was the concatenation of a profile-specific template **and** a shared chat template, and the shared half retained a mandatory first-round plan approval plus a blocking message, which is exactly the behaviour an autonomous single-turn run must not have. Reading only the file you changed shows none of it. ✅ Compose the prompt offline and read what the model will actually see; pin the composition in a test. ⚠️ This validates the TEXT, not the behaviour: runtime agent behaviour still needs a run.

362. **Replace a REGEX findings-parser with a machine-readable appendix the agent itself emits.** Have the agent end its final report with a fenced ```json block (fingerprint / title / severity / category), take the **LAST** such block, validate it is a list of dicts, and strip it from the human-readable report. 🔑 For run-over-run dedup, carry a **ledger keyed on fingerprint** (`first_seen`/`last_seen`/`title`) as a CI artifact: download the prior successful run's artifact best-effort in an `if: always()` job, merge, and write the merged ledger **into this run's artifact**. ⚠️ Ordering matters: the report step must run BEFORE the artifact upload or the ledger never lands in the artifact, and the ledger must be written before any notification is posted. See `learnings-code-review.md` for the classification bug this shape invites (membership tested against the mutating ledger instead of the pre-run snapshot).

363. **Slack's external file upload is a THREE-step flow and the middle step is a `POST`, not a `PUT`**: `files.getUploadURLExternal` (filename + length) → **POST the raw bytes** to the returned `upload_url` → `files.completeUploadExternal` (`files=[{id,title}]` as JSON, plus `channel_id`, and `initial_comment` to post a summary as the file's comment). Bot token as `Authorization: Bearer xoxb-…`. ⚠️ `PUT` is the intuitive verb and is wrong; Slack's own SDK uses POST. An HTTP client you already depend on is enough: no Slack SDK needed.

364. **Two zsh expansion traps that corrupt image/registry references in a shell loop, both silent.** In zsh, `$t:h` inside an image reference is a **history modifier** (take the head), and `$c:uv.lock` applies the `:u` modifier rather than appending a literal `:uv.lock`. ✅ Write `${c}:uv.lock` with explicit braces, or run registry/`crane` loops under `bash`. ⚠️ Unrelated but same class of "the tool helpfully did something else": `gcloud beta <group>` prompts to install the component and therefore **hangs a non-interactive run**, use the GA command, or pre-install the component.

365. **To edit one CELL of a markdown table safely, insert at the cell's OPENING boundary, never append at its end, and never split the row on `|`.** Rows whose cell content contains a literal `|` (a regex class, an alternation, a shell pipe in a keyword list) break any `split('|')`, and an "append to the end of this cell" regex needs to know where the cell ends, which is the same problem. ✅ The row's opening prefix (`| \`<key>\` | `) is unambiguous in every row, so inserting immediately after it edits exactly the intended cell with no parsing at all. 🔑 This extends #339, which established that appending a whole COLUMN at line end is safe; the complement is that editing an existing cell is safe only from its left edge.

366. **An entry number cited while DRAFTING is a guess: resolve every `#N` against the file on disk before the text ships, because the citation will resolve to the wrong entry rather than to nothing.** Writing a batch of entries and citing them by predicted position produced four off-by-one citations in one pass; every one of them **resolved successfully** to a real entry about a different subject, so a "do all citations resolve?" check passed them all. ✅ The check that catches it is "resolve each citation and PRINT the target's first line next to the claim": a human or model scan of the pairing, not a existence test. ⚠️ A citation that resolves to the wrong entry is worse than a dangling one: a dangling reference announces itself, a wrong one quietly sends the next reader somewhere plausible.

367. **`git stash` in a LINKED WORKTREE writes the SHARED `refs/stash`, so a stray stash in a verification command silently reverts another worktree's working tree mid-task.** There is one stash list for all worktrees of a repository. Never put `stash` in a verify script. To recover, find the entry naming your branch in `git stash list` and `git stash pop stash@{N}` it AT ONCE, before another worktree's user pops it.

368. **Squash merge makes every 'is this branch merged?' check give the WRONG answer, in three different ways.** Where the forge squash-merges, the default branch gets a NEW commit and the branch tip is never an ancestor of it. **(1)** `git merge-base --is-ancestor origin/<branch> origin/<default>` reports UNMERGED for every squash-merged branch; the authoritative check is `gh pr list --state all --json headRefName,state`, or grepping the default branch for a distinctive string the branch introduced. **(2)** `git branch -d` refuses the same branches for the same reason; `-D` is correct, but only AFTER proving the content reached the default branch, for example `git grep -c <string> <default> -- <path>` per branch. **(3)** `git branch -r` keeps listing remote branches the forge auto-deleted on merge: those are STALE REMOTE-TRACKING REFS, not remote branches, and the fix is `git fetch --prune`, not `git push --delete`. All three were reported to the owner as fact before being caught. Related: when a bulk destructive loop is refused by the permission classifier, running ONE instance by hand is both the safer method and often the diagnostic, which is how the already-gone refs were revealed.

369. **ALWAYS assert the occurrence count before a scripted string replacement, and grep for the OLD name after any rename.** A Python `str.replace` in a heredoc silently matched NOTHING because the target line's indentation differed from the sibling block the pattern was copied from, and the rename shipped half-done. It was caught only by an independent acceptance grep the owner had specified, not by the edit. Rule: every scripted edit gets `assert s.count(old) == 1`, and every rename gets a separate grep-for-the-old-name check afterwards. **A no-op replace is indistinguishable from a successful one without the assert.**

370. **A Bash call that starts `VAR=...; cd $VAR/sub || ...` runs in the REPO ROOT when the directory does not exist and the `cd` is unguarded, so scratch tooling writes into the working repository.** This is how `terraform init`/`console` left `r.tf` and a `.terraform` directory in a real repo. Always `mkdir -p` and then `cd X || exit 1` IN THE SAME CALL, and run `git status --porcelain` before any `git add`.

371. **A tool-call timeout is a statement about the HARNESS, never about the algorithm.** A full-file character-level difflib audit was abandoned after a 120 second foreground timeout and a cheaper per-line substitute was used instead, with the faster check written up as though the authoritative one 'does not finish'. Backgrounded, the authoritative check COMPLETED and independently confirmed the result (and falsified the claim written hours earlier in the same pass, which was then corrected in place). Say 'did not finish within N seconds', and prefer BACKGROUNDING the authoritative check over silently substituting a cheaper one.

372. **REUSE the guard sitting next to the code you are writing; the second instance of a trap is the expensive one.** Building a query-logging extractor, a new matcher treated the script name followed by whitespace anywhere in a command as a user query, so `grep -n knowledge-search.sh <file>` logged the FILE PATH as a query. The same file already documented the identical lesson for a sibling counter ('a search FOR a filename is not a read OF it') and already shipped the regex guard for it. Fix was to reuse that guard. **The negative control that caught it was a fixture whose only content was a command MENTIONING the script; without that control the dataset would have quietly filled with file paths instead of queries.** Note the same latent bug can survive in a sibling cheap check with lower consequence (inflating a count rather than a dataset), and is still worth naming.

373. **Shell startup is split by shell, and a function defined in the wrong profile silently does not exist.** On a machine whose login shell is zsh, helper functions and aliases defined in `~/.bash_profile` are NEVER read: `command -v <fn>` and `zsh -ic 'type <fn>'` both return not found while the user believes the command is available. **If a user says 'I run X in my terminal' and X is not on PATH, check the other shell's profile before concluding the command does not exist.** Also read the function body before assuming a name means an identity: a wrapper named for a tool may add flags that change the product's behaviour, which is why sessions launched by one path differed from the user's own. Three porting traps, all hit: (1) `eval "$(fzf --bash)"` becomes `fzf --zsh` and must be guarded on `[ -t 1 ]`, because the key bindings need ZLE and an unguarded eval prints "can't change option: zle" in every non-tty shell including `zsh -ic` and scripts; (2) a bash `PS1` with a command substitution becomes a zsh `PROMPT` and needs `setopt PROMPT_SUBST`, or the substitution is evaluated once at shell start and frozen forever; (3) `alias ls='ls --color=auto'` only works with GNU `ls`, so porting the alias without the PATH entry that provides it breaks `ls`. Placement rule: PATH entries in `.zprofile`, interactive aliases/functions/prompt in `.zshrc`, each PATH prepend guarded on the directory existing plus an already-present check so re-sourcing does not stack duplicates. **Surface rather than silently port anything that weakens security** (an `ssh` alias disabling host-key checking applies to git over ssh too), and anything large enough to need testing rather than copying.

374. **America/Vancouver is UTC-7 YEAR-ROUND (British Columbia permanent daylight time, tzdata abbreviation `MST`), so 'Pacific' and 'PST' differ by an hour from early November to March.** Verified with Python `zoneinfo` against system tzdata. **A fixed-offset rule of the form 'PDT is UTC-7, PST is UTC-8' is therefore WRONG for Vancouver in winter: convert with `zoneinfo` for `America/Vancouver`, never a hardcoded offset.** This matters whenever a schedule is compared against a local wall clock: a cron pinned to `America/Los_Angeles` fires at 00:00 PST, which is 01:00 in Vancouver during those months. Keep the owner-facing LABEL preference separate from the arithmetic: the label is a presentation choice, the offset is a fact.

375. **Mining a per-project native memory store into a shared hive: the split is almost perfectly along the frontmatter `type` axis, and knowing that makes the triage cheap.** Measured across 39 files from a single estate. TECHNICAL findings typed `project`/`reference` are mostly ALREADY in the shared corpus (cluster facts, access models, guard mechanics), so they cost nothing to check and rarely promote. **BEHAVIOURAL rules typed `feedback`/`user` leak almost COMPLETELY**: 27 of 39 promoted on the first pass, nearly every one carrying a dated owner quote. That is STRUCTURAL, not accidental: the native-memory contract asks for exactly those two types, and they are the ones with no path into the shared tiers, while a preference learned in one project does not even apply in another because the store is per-project. Worse, a capture gate that accepts a write to that store counts it as capture, so an agent can satisfy the gate and still leave the knowledge stranded. **Three method rules.** (1) Decide coverage by the CLAIM, never by vocabulary: quote the rule, grep a distinctive PHRASE from it, show the matched line. A vocabulary-overlap score once reported 50 of 54 files covered and was wrong, because two documents about one estate share nouns regardless of whether the same claim is recorded. (2) RE-VERIFY an inherited 'confirmed absent' list: a brief listed a rule as absent from both corpus and hive when a matching entry was already there, because a hand-check goes stale between sessions. (3) Many files are PARTIALLY covered, with the generic half in the corpus and the estate-specific or owner-quote half missing: promote only the uncovered half and cite the covering entry inside it, rather than skipping the file or duplicating the covered part. **Promotion is a COPY: the native files still serve their own project session, so never delete or edit them.**

376. **A probe whose matcher is looser than its intent will match its own tooling, so write the negative control from the tooling, not from the data.** Generalised from the two traps above: when building any extractor, counter or coverage test over transcripts or a corpus, the first fixture should be an artefact that MENTIONS the thing being counted without being an instance of it. Both a query extractor and a file-read counter failed this way, and in both cases the shipped guard already existed a few lines above the new code.

377. **An isolation gate that greps one file against another FALSE-POSITIVES when the first file is empty, and BSD and GNU grep disagree.** With zero findings the 'mine' file is empty, so the derived probe file is empty, and BSD `grep -F -f emptyfile` matches EVERY line and reports a large leak count instead of nothing. It is vacuously safe, because no findings exist to leak. Treat an all-lines-match count with an empty input as N/A, confirm the downstream prompt was built from the intended seeds only, and proceed.

378. **Three macOS and classifier gotchas that each silently invert a check.** (1) `/usr/bin/grep` on macOS has NO `-P`, so a `grep -nP '\x{2014}'` probe for a long-dash rule ERRORS OUT, and a trailing `; echo done` then reads as a clean pass; use `perl -CSD -ne 'print if /[\x{2013}\x{2014}]/'` with a POSITIVE control. (2) zsh does not word-split an unquoted variable, so `for args in "--set a=1 --set b=2"; cmd $args` passes ONE argument; write the commands out. (3) An auto-mode classifier can allow posting reviews and replies while DENYING the GraphQL thread-resolution mutation as an external write, so thread resolution must be reported as left open for the human rather than silently skipped. Related classifier facts worth knowing before planning a verification: running a DOWNLOADED binary is denied as external code, so offline rule unit tests via a vendor CLI are unavailable and the fallback is live range queries plus a server-side dry-run through an allowed path; and `terraform apply` is denied even in a throwaway directory with no backend, which is why `terraform console` and `terraform graph` are the read-only substitutes.

379. **A restore or migration script that expands a payload file list into ONE `tar` argv hits `ARG_MAX` and silently restores NOTHING.** Measured: 26,939 paths is about 2.5 MB of arguments against a 1 MB `ARG_MAX`, so `tar` dies with `E2BIG`; a `2>/dev/null` hid the error and a `|| warn` downgraded total failure to a yellow warning, after which control fell through to a legacy branch that appended exactly the anti-pattern the script existed to strip. **`--dry-run` CANNOT catch it, because it never executes that line.** The script's own header admitted it had never been run end to end and its installers were only tested against a stub. How to apply: do not trust a clean-looking restore run; hand `tar` the few TOP-LEVEL payload paths and let it recurse, then run the script's in-place mode to finish the rewiring, and afterwards VERIFY the destination directories exist rather than reading the log. Complements #81 (cp drops directories, verify what is in the artifact) and #324 (a check that runs before the last operation that can break what it checks is not a check); the `ARG_MAX` arm is in neither.

380. **Entry-level BM25 beats file-level match counting for retrieval over a corpus with wildly uneven file sizes, and the eval that shows it must hold the query constant.** Measured on a 59-file corpus: the ripgrep path ranked FILES by raw match count with no length normalisation and no IDF, so the two largest files won queries they had nothing to do with. On 8 realistic queries, 5 got a DIFFERENT top hit under length normalisation, and 16 of 24 top-3 slots went to the four biggest files, with the single largest file topping three unrelated queries. Scored known-item retrieval over 60 queries built from cited entries' own titles: correct FILE ranked #1 went from 32 percent (match count) to 95 percent (BM25), with entry-level MRR@10 of 0.92. **State the eval's weakness: queries drawn from the target entry make the absolute numbers optimistic, but both methods got IDENTICAL queries so the comparison is fair.** Ship it behind a backend switch with a silent fallback to the old path when the new script is missing, and verify the flip three ways: default emits the new output, the env override restores the old one, and a missing script falls back rather than failing.

381. **The non-circular ground truth for a retrieval change is the pairing of a query with the files that session went on to OPEN, and the transcript id is the join key.** Discarding the query text makes 'is our ranking any good' unanswerable without scraping transcripts, which in one case recovered 321 queries and only 7 query-to-file-opened pairs. Log the query, the source, the backend and the transcript id at search time.

382. **A test-discipline or LOC-ratio gate can be BLIND to a language and report a clean pass on a PR that is almost entirely that language.** One such script does not count `.tf` or `.tftest.hcl`, so a PR with 248 added test lines and about 45 production lines reported 'clean (0 test / 0 prod added LOC)'. For an unsupported language, compute the allowance by hand and say in the PR that the gate did not cover it.

383. **A comment-discipline gate that diffs against a base reads COMMITTED content, not the working tree or the index, so running it before committing reports clean on code it will later flag.** Without an explicit base or staged flag it falls back to the PR base branch and ignores stdin, and with a stale `origin/HEAD` it reports OTHER PEOPLE's commits, so pass the real base explicitly. To gate uncommitted work, stage the files by name and use the staged mode; re-run against the base after committing. Two scoring details that produce surprising fires: its comment-block counter includes a SHEBANG, so a template header of shebang plus two comment lines always trips a three-line rule; and a density rule above roughly 0.35 comment lines per added non-blank line with a four-line floor means two short comment blocks in a small new file are enough. **When it fires, the cheapest honest fix is to move the rationale somewhere a configurer actually reads it (a variable `description`, for example) and delete any comment that description now restates, rather than deleting a real why.**

384. **Write-allowlists for an agent's forge operations are SPELLING-SENSITIVE, so record the exact accepted form.** One harness allows `gh pr review:*`, `gh pr comment *`, a reply endpoint pattern, and exactly ONE GraphQL resolve form; any other spelling of the same mutation hits the classifier and is blocked as an external write. When a reviewer agent cannot post replies or resolve threads, check the allowlist spelling before debugging credentials.

385. **Codex sandbox modes, measured at codex-cli 0.153.4, and the ONE configuration that actually works for our dispatch contract.** `--sandbox read-only` is UNUSABLE here because bd takes a LOCK on open, so even `bd prime --memories-only` fails with `openat LOCK: operation not permitted`, which silently strips a worker of the hive. `workspace-write` blocks writes outside the workspace AND blocks `.git` writes inside it, so `git commit` fails on `.git/index.lock`. **Network is a SEPARATE AXIS, and an earlier probe in the same session concluded wrongly that workspace-write refuses network: that was a default-config artefact.** `-c sandbox_workspace_write.network_access=true` restores curl, gh, git and helm fully. A naive workspace-write run then still breaks `helm dep build` (needs `~/Library/Caches/helm`) and `gcloud` (writes a log under `~/.config/gcloud` before doing anything), and BOTH fail SILENTLY as a degraded worker rather than crashing. **Working bounded mode = `workspace-write` + `network_access=true` + `writable_roots=[$BEADS_DB, ~/Library/Caches/helm, ~/.config/gcloud, ~/.cache]`**, shipped as `CODEX_SANDBOX=bounded` in `codex-dispatch.sh` with the default left at full access so no existing caller regresses.

386. **A bounded Codex worker CAN commit, which makes an unwatched authoring lane technically viable, and the remaining blockers are policy and plumbing rather than permission.** Measured at codex-cli 0.153.4: `.git` is write-protected by default even inside the workspace, which is the real reason a plain `workspace-write` run fails; naming the worktree's own `.git` on the writable list (`-c sandbox_workspace_write.writable_roots=["<dir>/.git"]`) makes `git add` and `git commit` succeed, verified on disk as a real commit with real content, while writes to `$HOME` stay refused. That is exactly the containment an authoring lane needs: it can edit and commit in its own disposable worktree and nothing else. **The actual blockers to name when this comes up again:** the standing "writes stay on Claude" policy line, the shared checkout across peer sessions (it needs a worktree per lane), no mid-run visibility, and the need to swap the review lenses so Codex-authored code is not reviewed by the Codex lens.

387. **A third-party Codex plugin was audited and REJECTED; do not re-audit it without new evidence.** Audited at commit `7d10d22` (2026-11-14). Code quality was good: no external egress, loopback and unix-socket only, 0700/0600 modes re-asserted, the only environment read is `NODE_OPTIONS`, 115 tests, and its message self-approval is scoped to a single-use id. **Two blockers.** (1) `hooks/model.ts:290` HARDCODES `workspace-write` with `writableRoots: []` and `networkAccess: false`, with no setting to override, which is precisely the configuration measured as broken for us (no network, bd cannot open the hive), so the plugin offers only full access or a bd-less worker. (2) It requires Codex CLI 0.160.0 and we run 0.153.4. **Separate finding worth keeping regardless of the plugin:** a repository's own `.claude/codex.json` can set `approvals: yolo`, which forces full access with no prompts, so a hostile cloned repo is a live path. Revisit only if Codex is upgraded AND writable roots become configurable.

388. **`drift-check.sh` silently SKIPPED dead cross-references until 2026-11-07, which means the check that existed to protect cross-refs was the thing hiding the broken ones.** The symmetry loop ran `[ -f "$REFS_DIR/$b" ] || continue` BEFORE comparing, so a `See also:` pointing at a renamed or deleted learnings file was skipped rather than reported. It now warns. The instance tier had the same hole and was never visited by that loop at all, so a second pass covers `orgs/*/*.md`, where symmetry is NOT expected (an org file points at its portable twin, not the reverse) but a pointer to a missing file is just as dead. Implementation note for anyone editing it: the script defines only `REFS_DIR` and `BD_DIR`, there is no `ORGS_DIR`, so the org path is derived as `${HARNESS_ORGS:-$(dirname "$REFS_DIR")/orgs}`. Proven with two planted controls, one per tier, each firing by name, with the corpus restored and the checker clean afterwards. **The general shape to look for: a guard whose skip condition is the same predicate as the fault it is meant to detect.**

389. **Before POSTING anything from a scratch directory, grep each artifact for an identifier of the current run, because a run-unique directory does not help if a PREVIOUS run left a complete set of plausible files.** Extends `#343`. A fixed scratch directory still held a COMPLETE set of review artifacts from an earlier review of a DIFFERENT pull request: adversarial, summary, split and worker report. Posting by FILENAME without checking provenance would have put another PR's review on this one, and nothing about the files looked wrong. **Detection that worked:** `ls` the directory before writing, then grep each artifact for the PR number AND a domain keyword (one file had 401 hits of the right domain term and 0 of the other PR's number, so it was genuinely this run). **Quarantine strays into a `_stale_from_<id>/` subdirectory rather than deleting them**, and name your own outputs with the run identifier in the filename.

390. **A local `PreToolUse` hook can block a read-only command on the basis of the context it CANNOT read, so an agent sees a permission error where none exists.** Measured: a hook blocks `kubectl apply --dry-run=server` against the production context, and ALSO blocks it whenever the context is supplied through a shell VARIABLE, because the hook matches on the literal command text and cannot resolve the variable. Dev and staging dry-runs work with a LITERAL `--context`. Pair this with the separate server-side fact that impersonation (`auth can-i --as=<sa>`) fails on clusters where your user lacks the impersonate permission: read the ClusterRole bound to the ServiceAccount instead. Both failures look like RBAC and only one is.

391. **Running a third-party binary's source locally for an offline proof can be DENIED by the harness classifier, so plan the fallback before downloading a toolchain.** Measured: a scratchpad Go toolchain download succeeded, and `go build` of the downloaded source was denied. Building and running an upstream component locally needs explicit owner permission; the fallback that worked was replaying the component's config paths in Python against `kubectl get <kind> -o json` plus source reads at the pinned version. The cheap lesson is to decide which of those two you are doing BEFORE spending a download.

392. **Resolving a review-bot thread through the GraphQL `resolveReviewThread` mutation is DENIED to a subagent by the auto-mode classifier, while posting REPLIES and submitting reviews are allowed.** So the reachable shape is: reply to the thread, then list it as still open in the summary. Do not work around the denial; the open-thread list is the honest artifact and a human resolves it in one click.

393. **In a tracker's issue text a bare `#N` AUTO-LINKS to an unrelated pull request in a connected repository, so a corpus citation written as `file.md #15` becomes a link to someone else's PR 12.** Observed in Linear. Write `entry 12` instead of `#12` in tracker prose. The corpus citation form `[learnings-<file>.md#<N>]` with no space is safe because the autolinker needs the `#` to stand alone.

394. **README review heuristic: count how many times the document explains what the project IS before the reader reaches a command.** One README did it three times (a lede, a blockquote restating the lede, and a nine-row benefits table) and then spent sixty lines on an ASCII architecture diagram, putting install at line 80 of 161. Reordering to "what it is, which runtimes, install" and deleting the diagram took 13,775 bytes to 7,374 with no content loss. **Two smells that generalise.** A table cell running past 400 characters is prose in the wrong container. And an ASCII diagram in a README that ALSO has a rendered docs site is a worse copy competing with a better one; point at the site and spend those lines on what the picture could NOT say. **The verification that matters for any doc rewrite:** extract every relative link from both versions, assert each still resolves, and DIFF the two sets so a dropped link is a decision rather than an accident (29 before, 24 after, 0 broken, 2 of the 6 dropped put back on review). Counts in a README go stale silently, so re-derive them from the tree. **Unrelated but found the same way:** a redaction denylist pattern of a bare ticket prefix, three letters plus a hyphen, matches ordinary prose case-insensitively: one real prefix hits the ArgoCD term "App-of-Apps". Anchor it as `<PREFIX>-[0-9]` or the gate trains its reader to ignore it.

395. **Citation measurement after four days of a search reshape plus a gate-threshold drop: the HEADLINE RATE WAS FLAT and the composition was the only thing that moved, which is the result to expect from a tooling change that makes an action cheaper rather than more likely.** Readers-who-cite held at 9 to 10 percent across three measurements (29 of 340 sessions at the last one); citation events moved 308 to 320 and distinct entries cited 95 to 103. 7 of the 12 NEW citations were subagent-sourced against 20 percent historically, which is the right direction at a sample size too small to call. **The threshold change did what it was designed to do and was still not sufficient:** of 41 new subagent sessions, 16 (39 percent) opened at least one topic file so the gate could fire, against 0 of 34 under the old threshold, and of those 16 eligible only 2 cited. **The unchanged drop-off is the real finding: 33 of 41 sessions ran the knowledge search and only 16 opened a topic file, so 25 consulted the router and stopped.** Making citing FREE for whoever opens a file does not make more sessions open one. Also confirmed: entry COVERAGE fell from 4 percent to 3.3 percent purely because a consolidation added 220 entries to the denominator, which is the mechanical deflation that makes "readers who cite" the metric to steer by rather than coverage.

396. **A tracker MCP can accept a workflow-state change by NAME, return success, and do nothing, so always RE-READ the status after writing it.** Measured on Linear: `save_issue` with the state name `In Review` on one team left the status at `In Progress` with NO error; passing the state's UUID worked. The general rule for any tracker write through an MCP: treat a name as a convenience that may silently miss, resolve to an id when the write matters, and verify by reading back rather than by the tool's return value.

397. **`sed` on this machine is GNU sed, so the BSD idiom `sed -i '' file` treats the empty string as a SCRIPT and then the filename as a second file, failing with `can't read`.** Use `sed -i` with no suffix argument, or `perl -pi -e` when you want one form that works on both. This bites every time a recipe is copied from macOS documentation, and the error message does not say "wrong sed". Pairs with the shell traps in `#249` and `#99`: the Bash tool's shell is zsh and its `sed` is GNU, which is an unusual combination to carry assumptions into.

398. **Two ways a background Bash task lies about its own progress here.** (1) **A loop using plain `sleep N` exits after its FIRST iteration**, because the harness blocks `sleep`; use `perl -e 'select(undef,undef,undef,N)'` as the delay instead, or the poll loop silently becomes a single check. (2) **The `run_in_background` completion notice can fire while the wrapped process is still running.** Observed for a `codex-dispatch.sh` wrapper: the notice arrived, the log kept growing, and no exit marker was present. ✅ Trust the `DISPATCH_EXIT=` marker written INSIDE the log, plus the end-of-report sentinel, never the harness notice alone. Extends the worker-completion tests in `#121`.

399. **`bd update --notes` REPLACES the issue's notes rather than appending, and the lost text is recoverable only from history.** Use `--append-notes` to add. If notes were already overwritten, recover them from `bd history <id> --json` (`.Issue.notes` per commit). Worth knowing before any scripted note update over a set of tasks, where one wrong flag silently discards every task's accumulated context.

400. **NEVER use `pkill` or `killall` with a PATTERN on the owner's machine: resolve the exact PID and kill that, after confirming the PID is the intended process.** Evidence, and the reason this is absolute rather than advisory: `pkill -f "<script>.py" -U "$(id -u)"` was run to clean up ONE test server. On macOS `pkill` the pattern must be the FINAL operand; with `-U` trailing it the binding is unreliable, and it reaped UID-WIDE, killing the live server AND every browser session the owner had open while he was mid-work. The tabs survived only because the browser keeps session files. ✅ **How to apply:** capture the PID when you start something (`cmd & echo $!`, or a pid file) and kill THAT PID. If you must search, PRINT the matches first and kill by PID; never pipe a pattern straight into a kill. A test server gets its own port, its own cache directory and its own recorded PID, and cleanup targets that PID and nothing else. ⚠️ Note that `#23` recommends `killall` INSIDE a container for PID-1 signal handling: that is a container-only exception and does not apply on the host.

401. **One more refusal shape for the worktree isolation guard, and the escape that always works. Extends `#165`, `#179`, `#191` and `#269`.** Inside a worktree-isolated agent under the repositories root, the guard refuses any COMPOUND Bash command whose text also contains a path containing the repositories-root directory name, SCRATCHPAD PATHS INCLUDED, reading it as an unverifiable version-control call. The same refusal fires for loops, pipes into scripts, and flags such as `--file`/`--staged`. ✅ Write the multi-step render or diff work into a SCRIPT FILE in the scratchpad and run `bash <script>` as a single command: one command, no inline path, no compound. This is cheaper than fighting the pattern and is the same workaround `#191` reaches for a different trigger.

402. **Four more zsh expansion traps that corrupt a verification loop silently, and the two token-compression rules they travel with. Extends `#99`, `#249` and `#352`.** (1) **An unbraced variable followed by a COLON takes a history modifier**, so `git show $t:charts/x.yaml` expands `$t:c` as a command-path lookup and produces a broken ref (`origin/devharts/...`). Same trap in an ARN: `arn:aws:iam::$ACCT:role/x` applies `:r` and yields `<acct>ole/x`, and `:secret:y` applies `:s`. **Always brace: `${t}:path`, `${ACCT}:role/x`**, or run the loop under `bash <<'EOF'`. (2) **Unquoted `$args` is not word-split**, so a variable holding several flags arrives as ONE argument (`helm: unexpected arguments`, or `kubectl auth can-i` seeing `'verb resource'` as a single arg). The same bites `${4:+-f $4}`, which passes `-f path` as one argument. (3) **`-o 'custom-columns=...[*]...'` globs** unless quoted. (4) **`read` with `IFS=<tab>` COLLAPSES empty TSV fields**, so a cluster-scoped row with an empty namespace column shifts every later field. ✅ Put any multi-argument loop in a `#!/bin/bash` script. Two companions: PATH `sed` here is GNU, so `sed -i ''` fails (use `sed -i` with no suffix, or `perl -pi`), and the token-compression hook COMPRESSES `gh pr diff` output (dropping hunks, printing a '[full diff: ...]' marker), so read the diff with the compression disabled or from a worktree.

403. **Three probe-depth bugs in consolidation's own verification, each of which reported a clean result while hiding a real one.** (1) **The index re-derive CLOBBERED two deliberate prose overrides because the guard tested for the ASCII form `n/a - prose registry` while the file carries the EM-DASH form of the same sentence.** Caught only because the correction list was read line by line. ✅ Guard on the `n/a` PREFIX alone, never on the full override sentence, and never on a punctuation character the corpus mixes. (2) **A header-presence check written as `head -8 | grep '^See also:'` reports a FALSE MISSING** on any file whose See-also line sits below a longer intro block (one org file has it at line 9). ✅ Use `head -20`. (3) **A landing check must assert the destination entry EXISTS before scoring any similarity against it**; without that assertion two promotions pointing at entry numbers that were never written scored as fine. ⚠️ And the governing rule over all three: **a token-overlap coverage scorer is a POINTER to candidate entries, never a verdict.** One ranked 70 candidates of which roughly half were genuine duplicates and half merely shared vocabulary. Every COVERED verdict still needs the destination line QUOTED.

404. **Never spot-check a promotion by grepping the memory KEY against the corpus: promotion generalises and rewrites, so the key string is absent BY CONSTRUCTION and a key-grep reports a false loss.** This error was made on a verification pass and briefly concluded that a memory had been dropped; it had landed intact under a different wording. ✅ **Correct method: read the discard log's `Where its content now lives: <file>#N` line for that key, open that entry, and confirm the CLAIM is present** using the same hard-literal test the pass itself is told to use (file:line references, quoted error strings, flags, version pins). The discard log IS the key-to-destination index; that is most of why it is worth writing.

405. **A consolidation discard log should cover EVERY forget, not only the discarded bucket, because a promotion is a lossy rewrite too.** Earlier passes logged only stale records (9 records against 279 forgets on one run), reasoning that promoted content is recoverable from the corpus. It is not symmetric: **a promotion is a REWRITE, usually a MERGE of several memories, so the original wording, its ticket ids and its negative-control detail are gone from the hive the moment you forget it, exactly like a discarded record.** Logging all forgets on a 309-forget pass cost about 274 KB and made the pass fully auditable: each record carries its class (PROMOTED, COVERED or STALE), the destination `#N`, and the full original text. It also makes the mandated count check unambiguous, since `grep -c '^## '` then equals the number of memories forgotten. ⚠️ **One formatting guard is required: indent any body line that begins with `## ` by two spaces**, or quoted memory bodies inflate the record count.

406. **Trimming a router index's keyword column is safe to automate only behind two gates, and the recovery step must re-add SUBJECTS rather than restore phrases.** (1) **BM25 coverage veto:** a phrase whose own file an entry-level BM25 search cannot return in its top 5 is an INTENT HOOK, not a restatement of a finding, and deleting it destroys router recall. (2) **The word-count boundary splits on IDENTIFIERS, not on length:** three-word phrases divide into restatements and intent paraphrases, and the paraphrases are exactly what must never be deleted. The rule that worked: drop at 4 or more words, drop at 3 words ONLY when a name-shaped token ties the phrase to a specific finding, and start the instance tier at 4 regardless because its proper nouns ARE the route. Measured result on one index: 126110 to 91494 bytes, keyword column 44 percent lighter, 1172 phrases dropped, 0 of 2056 kept phrases lost their route, 100 percent of dropped phrases still recoverable by BM25. ✅ **Recovery step:** where a dropped phrase held the row's LAST mention of a recurring subject, re-add the SUBJECT as a bare hook, never the whole phrase. Emit the whole NAME RUN (adjacent capitalised or identifier tokens) or a two-word product name degrades into its second word and routes nothing. Anchor 'is this a subject' on corpus document frequency of at least 5 AND name-shaped; frequency alone recovers ordinary English and restored 1072 of 1425 drops on the first attempt. A row can only regain a token it already had, so recovery cannot push any row above its pre-trim score.

407. **A mechanical trim rule needs a HUMAN PASS on the biggest rows, and the cheap detector for what the safety nets miss is a token-set diff with NO filters.** On one index trim, the first row read by hand OVERTURNED 46 of 150 mechanical drops, about 30 percent, and the same rate held elsewhere: the rule had dropped symptom phrases such as 'subagent timed out', 'why did my command do nothing', 'pod Running but restarting' and 'exit 137 reason Error not OOM'. **Neither safety net catches that class:** head-noun recovery rescues only NAME-shaped tokens, and a BM25 veto passes any generic wording when the owning file has hundreds of entries. ✅ **The detector: diff the token set of the SCORED TEXT (keywords plus the domain summary) before versus after, with no name-shape and no frequency filter. Everything in that lost set is unprotected, and reading it is what the judgment call actually costs.** Budget for that reading rather than trusting the measured recall number.
