408. **When a ranker lowercases a whole structured ROW, every column becomes a keyword column: score the content columns, never the link columns.** Measured on a router index whose rows carry a cross-references column of backticked file names: a query for one domain ranked an unrelated file at the top, ABOVE the correct one, although the query term appeared in zero of that file's 270 keyword phrases. Its only hit was the backticked name of the correct file inside its cross-references column. Because both tiers of the table carry such a column, every densely cross-linked row scored free hits on its neighbours' topics, and the two largest rows won queries they had nothing to do with. ✅ **Fix: strip backticked file-like tokens (`.md`, `.sh`, `.py`, `.json`) from the row AFTER the first one, keeping the row's own name.** That shape is independent of which table the row is in. Verified on 12 known-item queries: 6 false hits removed, no correct file lost a position.

409. **A router-index gap class worth auditing periodically: a row whose FILE is plainly about topic X while no column of its row ever says X.** Found by probing each row for its own obvious companion terms: a mesh file with zero sidecar or injection hooks, so a sidecar-injection query routed to the wrong file; a compositions file that never said 'Composition' despite 70 mentions inside it; an operators file that never said 'webhook' despite 7; a policy file naming egress but never ingress; a cluster file carrying only the hyphenated spelling, so the unhyphenated query missed. ✅ **Probe the Keywords AND the domain-summary column**, because the router scores both and a term present only in the summary is not a gap. This audit is cheap, finds real misroutes, and is the complement to a trim: a trim removes restatements, this adds the missing subjects.

410. **Verifying a column-scoped edit to a markdown table: compare cells BY POSITION FROM THE END, never by fixed index.** A row whose cell content contains an unescaped `|` splits into more cells than the header declares (two rows in one table split into 9 where the header declares 5), so a fixed-index check of 'column 4 is the cross-references column' silently compares the WRONG cells on exactly the rows most likely to be damaged. Position-from-the-end is stable because the trailing columns are short and pipe-free. ⚠️ **Second finding, which saves a false alarm: a cell-COUNT CHANGE is not automatically a regression.** A keyword trim that happened to remove the pipe-bearing phrases took those rows from 9 cells to 7, well-formed for the first time. Check whether the BEFORE or the AFTER matches the header's column count before judging it. Pairs with `#353` on editing a single cell safely.

411. **State risks ONCE, clearly, in prose, then either act or ask, never both.** When you have already surfaced the risks of an operation and the owner replies with a direct instruction, EXECUTE it: do not follow up with a confirmation dialog re-asking the same decision. Evidence: during a destructive restore the blast radius was flagged, and a confirmation dialog was still opened offering stop / run / extract-elsewhere; the owner interrupted the tool call and said 'run real restore now'. The risk disclosure had already done its job and the second ask was pure friction. ✅ Reserve an interactive confirmation for decisions you genuinely cannot resolve, not for re-confirming one the owner just made. Taking a cheap reversible safety step first (a backup) is still welcome and does not need its own prompt. Distinct from the 'answer, do not escalate' and 'raise a risk once' rules in `shared-protocols-core.md`, which cover diagnostic questions and already-accepted risks; this one covers re-asking AFTER a direct instruction.

412. **The two halves of the dispatch gate, which fail in opposite directions and were both learned the hard way.** (1) **Do not dispatch BEFORE the shape is approved.** After writing a spec or shape document, STOP and wait for an explicit go: the owner reads the document first. Two workers were dispatched before a shape was final and the owner had to kill both ('kill the subagent, let's lock down the shape', then 'don't dispatch yet. let me read the doc.'). ⚠️ Answering a follow-up question such as 'where should this be recorded' is NOT approval to dispatch. (2) **Do not keep working inline AFTER it is approved.** Once the owner approves a shape, the implementation goes to the matching specialist EVEN WHEN the change grew out of a chain of small inline edits during the back-and-forth. One such chain reached 20 files across charts, an app-of-apps and two Terraform roots plus a rebase, and the owner asked 'wtf did you not dispatch this work to a subagent'; each step had looked small, so the delegation rule was never re-checked. ✅ **The trigger is the moment the owner says 'go' or 'this is better' about a shape: stop editing and write a dispatch brief.** Inline work after that point is limited to lookups, rendering to answer a question, and single-file edits under two minutes.

413. **Two task-tracker hygiene rules that specialists get wrong by default, both of which hide real state.** (1) **A specialist whose configuration says claim/execute/close closes its task at PR-OPEN, which hides live unfinished work.** Confirmed on one specialist and likely true of every agent with that wording. A change is NOT done at PR-open: it can take review findings, fix commits, or be rejected, and it still has to merge and deploy. ✅ **Every dispatch prompt must state the close condition explicitly** ('do NOT close the task on PR-open; leave it open until the PR is MERGED and, for production infrastructure or monitoring, the deploy is green and verified; open the PR, return the URL, stop'). When a specialist hands back 'CLOSED' with only a PR URL, reopen it and append the real condition. The orchestrator owns the final close after merge plus deploy. (2) **Set the external ticket reference on EVERY task created for a ticket, not only the epic.** The owner expects each subtask to be traceable to the tracker on its own, and linking only the epic was called out by name. Pass the reference in every child creation.

414. **A tracker ticket moves to Done only after the change has landed in PRODUCTION, even when the owner says 'close it', and the task tracker's lifecycle deliberately diverges.** Until production it stays In Review, even when merged to the default branch and proven on the lower environments. Task-tracker tasks still close on merge, so the two lifecycles diverge on purpose: SAY WHICH ONE YOU CLOSED. Evidence: three tickets were marked Done after lower-environment checks and the owner reverted all three. Why it matters on this kind of estate: a handover that moves resources between systems is not finished until the production release has applied it. ✅ Before setting a ticket Done, confirm production has the change (the release applied, and the production controller or plan showing it). If 'close it' arrives earlier, close the task and leave the ticket In Review. ⚠️ **One standing exception, granted explicitly: a ticket that ships only INERT parity (sync-off applications, render parity) whose behavioural adoption is deferred to a separate follow-on ticket CAN go to Done at merge**, because nothing it shipped is live. Three such tickets were moved at merge on the owner's say, with the adoption ticket carrying the real change.

415. **NEVER post comments, updates or edits to a tracker ticket yourself, and ALWAYS put the ticket link in the PR body: the two halves point opposite ways and both are mandatory.** Owner on the first half: 'I don't want you to post anything to the ticket.' Never call a tracker write tool (save issue, save comment, status change) without an explicit ask IN THAT TURN; report findings in chat and let the owner post. Owner on the second half, after four PRs shipped without it: 'why the fuck did we not link the linear ticket in the PRs.' Every PR for a ticket carries the ticket link in its body, and the back-link the tracker then creates on the ticket IS wanted, which is exactly the thing the no-posting rule might otherwise seem to forbid. ✅ Tell subagents BOTH halves, because a prompt carrying only the first produces PRs with no ticket link.

416. **A PR description should read in ONE PASS and target about 15 visible lines, well under the 40-line harness cap. Sharpens `#210`.** Shape: ticket link, a few plain-language WHY bullets, a few WHAT CHANGED bullets, one or two validation lines. Tables, query output and per-target numbers go in a SINGLE collapsed block. Owner, on an evidence-heavy body: make it 'tight enough that this is explained easily', 'no long ass descriptions'. ✅ **The 40-line cap is a CEILING, not a target**, and evidence-heavy bodies hide the point they were written to make. State the 15-line target and the bullet shape in every dispatch that opens a PR, and read the body yourself before reporting the PR as done.

417. **PR titles follow Conventional Commits `<type>(<scope>): <title>` REGARDLESS of the repository's own commit history, and the head commit subject must MATCH the title.** Most repositories here have unconventional history and copying it is wrong. Type selection that keeps coming up: infrastructure cost or noise reduction is `chore`; a behaviour correction is `fix`. ✅ **The head commit subject must equal the PR title**, or a squash merge produces a default-branch commit that disagrees with the PR it came from, which breaks later archaeology. Encoded in the PR-creation skill.

418. **Three rules for text the owner READS rather than runs, each learned from a correction.** (1) **Tracker tickets and ticket comments are SHORT:** one-line problem, one-line cause, 2 to 3 evidence bullets (numbers, log strings, times in the owner's timezone), one-line fix. Draft at roughly 6 to 8 lines; deep analysis goes in the knowledge layer or a runbook, never in the ticket. Owner on a detailed description: 'don't write a fucking book'. **Use PLAIN LANGUAGE in them too:** asked 'wtf does this line mean' about an abstract mechanism sentence, so name the concrete missing setting and its effect rather than the mechanism. (2) **Show a draft RENDERED, never inside a markdown code fence.** Shown a closing comment as a fenced block, the owner said 'i dnt read in markdown'. Paste the draft as ordinary prose or a table in the reply, then post the same markdown to the tool afterwards. (3) **In any table that leaves the chat, an empty cell says 'no data', never a bare dash.** The owner replaced the dashes by hand after a posting. A dash is ambiguous between zero, not-applicable and not-measured. Use 'n/a' ONLY when the metric genuinely does not apply; use 'no data' when a value was not measured.

419. **Two shapes for messages that leave the team, both of which fail by including the work rather than the decision.** (1) **Pitching a PLAN to a lead (not a peer review):** say 'the work splits into N parts', label each part by what it DOES (cleanup / move / remove / build-only) and whether it changes what ships, give the saving or effect per part, and say what STAYS and why. **Leave OUT the 'how we prove nothing breaks' section and internal labels** such as 'chunks' or part codes. Explain mechanisms in plain language with one measured number. Attach only a self-contained evidence document, never links to local files. The owner rejected a first draft with 'I don't like it at all. do better': he could not follow the technical explanation and did not want the proof section or the internal vocabulary. (2) **A note about a PR review:** keep it to the ONE real question, phrased as a question, not a verdict. No 'rest looks good', no side checks, no FYIs. The owner cut one draft down to a single question. ✅ Both route through the stakeholder-comms skill; this entry is the measured shape it should produce.

420. **Run each part's before/after control against the CURRENT MERGED DEFAULT BRANCH with the previous parts already merged, never stacked on an unmerged branch, and keep the parts SEQUENTIAL.** Owner on a multi-part measured change: 'I would like to do before/after from dev only... coding doesn't take most of the time so I guess it is better to wait'. ✅ Wait for the previous PR to MERGE before starting the next; do not propose stacked PRs or parallel parts, and keep the task chain sequential. The reason is measurement validity, not politeness: a baseline taken on an unmerged stack measures the stack, not the change.

421. **Standing owner decisions about the knowledge corpus itself, recorded so no later pass re-proposes them.** (1) **The previous employer's memory corpus was NOT recovered, by decision:** 'I am not interested in any bd issues that are carried over. we can start clean.' Do not propose restoring it and do not treat a sparse hive as a bug to fix; build the new estate's memory from scratch. Background in case someone re-derives the loss: it predates the snapshot (the export state recorded zero memories and the archived file carried 2 against the roughly 103 the migration plan documents), and older in-hive backups still hold real corpora, so recovery remains POSSIBLE and is simply not wanted. (2) **Retention decisions that are settled:** keep a learnings file whose tool is unused at the current employer rather than retiring it, and do NOT create an instance-tier workflow file for the current org yet, since reads of the previous org's equivalent are acceptable until there is real content to justify one. Do not re-propose either in a future consolidation.

422. **The owner's `~/.ssh` and his SSH backup directory are PERSONAL keys, not employer property: never flag them for deletion or cleanup during an org switch or a harness cleanup.** He corrected this explicitly; both directories stay in place, with permissions tightened to 700/600. Generalisation worth carrying: during an employer migration, separate the machine's personal surface from the employer's artifacts BEFORE proposing any cleanup list, and when unsure leave it and say so.

423. **User-facing text inside artifacts we build (dashboard panel descriptions, tooltips, tags) is 1 to 2 plain sentences, at most about 200 characters: what it shows plus the one thing to watch.** No preambles, no long dashes, and NO ticket IDs in tags. ⚠️ **Dashboards are generic long-lived views, so no change-specific narrative in titles, rows or descriptions:** no 'the dip after the cap is expected', no before/after framing, no rollout notes, and no production baselines unless essential. Evidence: the owner rejected a first dashboard for long panel descriptions with long dashes and a ticket-ID tag, and separately rejected a structured what/good/bad/source description format on the same dashboard. ✅ Put the limit in the dispatch prompt for any dashboard, alert or doc-text work and check the output before relaying it.

424. **Hand-written onboarding and architecture docs cite a file path plus the SYMBOL name (function, class, constant), NEVER `file.py:123`.** Why: line numbers rot within weeks in an active repository, and **a wrong line number is worse than none, because it sends the reader to unrelated code with full confidence**, while symbol names survive refactors and are greppable. Evidence: an audit of eleven architecture documents found 188 stale claims, and essentially every one of their 454 line-pinned references had drifted. ✅ The same rule covers COUNTS and VERSION STRINGS in such documents: measure before writing one down. Do not reintroduce `:NNN` when editing them. ⚠️ Note the deliberate contrast with knowledge-corpus entries, where `file:line` at a PINNED version is required evidence: the difference is that a pinned-version source citation is a claim about an immutable tree, while an onboarding doc points at a moving one.

425. **Code comments must be STANDALONE: no task-phase or ticket-number narrative baked into source files.** Project sequencing belongs in the task tracker and the PR, not in a `.tf` or `.py` comment, where it reads as clutter to anyone who arrives later without that context. ⚠️ Workers do this BY DEFAULT even when told comments are WHY-only, so the dispatch brief has to say it in those words: no ticket or task references in code; a bare `TODO` with no ID is fine. Pairs with the minimal-comments rule in `code-quality.md` and the zero-comment chart floor in `learnings-helm-ci.md`.

426. **When consolidating PRs or dropping commits, every posted text must stand ALONE: never reference closed or superseded PRs, or changes that were dropped along the way.** That covers the body, review posts and comments. The reader of the surviving PR has no access to the history that makes such a reference meaningful, so it reads as a dangling pointer. ✅ Sweep every posted text for such references BEFORE posting, not after; this is cheap to check and expensive to fix once the PR is public.

427. **Keep guardrails PROPORTIONATE, and run only RELEASED upstream versions.** When an action is already recorded (a PR, a merge, an audit trail), 'someone could misuse it' is not a reason to add guard workflows, extra required checks or custom validation: build the basic control, not layers of it. And never pin to an UNRELEASED upstream commit to pick up a fix. Evidence: on one design an adversarial review pushed a privileged-trigger guard check, an unreleased upstream fix and a skip-if-older-build check; owner: 'we are overindexing on guards ... if some one misuses it, it is already tracked', 'we run only what is released', and on the out-of-order-build concern 'we only update an existing branch so why are we worried about this'. ✅ **When an adversarial lens proposes extra safeguards, weigh LIKELIHOOD against the existing audit trail and present them as OPTIONAL, not blocking.** This is the orchestration-level twin of the minimal-template rule in `learnings-helm-ci.md`.

428. **'Get this PR ready' means ALL of it, with no separate ask: full body from the repository template, mark it ready, dispatch the two-lens review, AND trigger the repository's own in-repo AI review.** Owner: 'I mean get parallax and ai-self-review done. make sure whenever I ask for a pr to be made ready those things are done.' Earlier PRs got the two-lens review only. 'get it ready', 'make it ready' and 'ready for review' all trigger both, and even a trivial-tier PR gets both unless the owner says skip. Drafts get NEITHER, per the draft carve-out. ✅ **Tell the two-lens reviewer that the in-repo review is running**, so it VERIFIES those findings rather than duplicating them: two independent reviews producing the same finding twice is noise the owner then has to deduplicate.

429. **Driving an agent CLI over SSH: two argument-parsing traps that make the remote run exit 0 having done nothing.** (1) **A variadic tools flag SWALLOWS a following prompt as a tool name**, so put the prompt immediately after the prompt flag, or after `--`. (2) **The agent CLI ALSO READS STDIN**, so when the remote script is fed through `ssh host -- bash -s <<EOF`, the agent consumes the REST OF THE HEREDOC as its prompt and the remaining lines never run: exit 0, output silently missing. ✅ **Always run a remote agent CLI with `< /dev/null`.** This is the same stdin hazard that makes a headless dispatch hang locally, in a shape where it fails quietly instead. Calibration once fixed: a 4-file, 122-line PR review took about 20 seconds of wall time and 4 turns on a modest cloud devbox.

430. **Running a terminal-multiplexer agent harness against REMOTE cloud devboxes: the setup that works, and the four behaviours that make a naive attempt look broken.** Setup (tuios 0.11.1 over SSH-config-based devbox hosts): add the host WITH an explicit `--command <absolute path to the binary on the box>` or the link reports no-binary, then sync with `--start`. **Its integration hooks run under `/bin/sh`, so symlink the binary into a system path** or the stop hook fails and session state silently falls back to coarse heuristics. Creating a session with `--host` refuses a working-directory flag (create the session over SSH on the box instead); `new-window` does not take a `host:session` target but `start-agent -s <host>:<session>` does. ⚠️ **A fresh agent CLI STALLS the start on its first-run theme picker:** pre-merge `hasCompletedOnboarding` and the per-directory trust flag into its config file first. ⚠️ **Asking an agent that has already finished returned an EMPTY answer in 3 seconds although the reply was visible on screen**, so hand results back through a file or the repository, not through the ask channel. ⚠️ **The remote agent came up in auto-approval mode, so an allow list is NOT a gate**: set the permission mode explicitly. And remote workers consume the OWNER's subscription limit, which is the real cap on fan-out width. ⚠️ **A persistent host link counts as workspace ACTIVITY and blocks idle-stop:** with no sessions on the box, the deadline still moved every 3 to 4 minutes and last-used every minute; after removing the link both froze within a minute (the negative control). So a linked devbox never idle-stops while the laptop is awake. ✅ Add the host only for the duration of a dispatch, or stop the workspace after the job.

431. **Working documents go in the owner's own documents tree under a topic directory, never ONLY in the session scratchpad.** He will not navigate a long scratchpad path, so a meeting or design document left there is effectively unwritten. ✅ Write it to the durable topic directory directly and give that path in the reply. The scratchpad remains correct for intermediate artifacts and scripts; the distinction is whether a human is meant to open it.

432. **Harness drift worth checking after any owner-preference change: the preference reaches the PROSE of an agent's instructions but not its posted-output TEMPLATE.** Concrete instance: the owner's no-long-dash rule is wired into the shared protocol files and into agent prose, while the reviewer agent's POSTED-OUTPUT template (verdict line, lens-split line, finding lines) still contains them, so every posted review violates the rule unless the reviewer rewrites them by hand at post time. ✅ **When a formatting preference lands, grep the agent definitions for TEMPLATE blocks and fenced examples, not only for instruction prose**; those are where a rule silently fails to propagate, because they read as data rather than as instructions.

433. **A design doc is a DECISION, not a spec, and the three principles that follow from that, including the hedge that must NOT be made mandatory.** House style here, per the Head of Engineering: design docs are deliberately LIGHT and capture decision-making at the OUTSET of a feature, not a full spec or a systems-level impact analysis. The three principles of the contributed template: (1) **Strong opinions, loosely held.** Make a recommendation and argue for it, and be clear about confidence IN EITHER DIRECTION, because **a hedge you do not mean invites relitigation of something you had already settled.** (2) **Lead with what matters most:** strongest reason first, assuming readers stop after two paragraphs. (3) **Push detail into an Appendix child page** so the main page stays a decision. ⚠️ **Do NOT build in ritual hedging.** An earlier draft made 'What would change my mind' a REQUIRED section and the owner rejected it as something that could be quoted back at him; it is now 'Open questions', OPTIONAL, with an explicit instruction to DELETE it rather than invent doubt. ⚠️ **Same spirit for meeting and approach documents (2026-11-19):** he kept the analysis but cut the 'Prepared for X' line, the section on in-flight PRs, and trimmed 'Decisions for the meeting' to a short list headed 'Decisions'. **The document is about the problem and the choice; PR status and authorship framing are noise for that audience.** So: no audience or date preamble, no PR-status section, decisions as a terse list of open calls.
